Formulation of parton shower evolution beyond leading order for electron-positron annihilation Preprint: DESY-26-054
Zoltán Nagy, Davison E. Soper

Abstract
Parton shower evolution is given by a renormalization group equation that reflects the infrared behavior of perturbative QCD including color and spin and including quantum interference. With added approximations, such an evolution equation can provide the basis for a parton shower event generator. To date, splitting functions for parton shower evolution have been derived only to leading order in α s \alpha_{\mathrm{s}} . We provide a framework that can provide splitting functions beyond leading order, and in particular at order α s 2 \alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2} . This requires a representation of Feynman graphs in which partons are off shell and are characterized by vector or spinor indices instead of on-shell spins.



I
Introduction

It is an unsolved problem to specify splitting functions for a parton shower in which the splitting functions are defined at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}
or beyond. The splitting functions should be based on the soft and collinear singularities of quantum chromodynamics (QCD)
NSAllOrder
. Thus what one needs is to translate the singularities of Feynman graphs into functions from which the parton splitting functions are constructed. This is not a trivial project beyond leading order in
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
because one has both real emissions and virtual exchanges and both soft and collinear singularities and combinations of these. Thus one seeks a method that constructs the needed singular functions directly from Feynman graphs. At order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}
, we need splitting functions describing two real emissions, one real emission and one virtual loop, and two virtual loops. We describe here a general framework for constructing such functions for a parton shower for electron-positron annihilation to hadrons.
1
1

1

For lepton-hadron collisions and hadron-hadron collisions, one would need to include the factorization of parton distribution functions.
We also illustrate how this framework works for leading order splitting functions. We defer to later work the application of this framework at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}
.

There are many meanings in the literature for the name “parton shower,” just as there are many meanings for “factorization.” Having many meanings in circulation can lead to confusion. We start, therefore, with a specification of what we have in mind in this paper as a parton shower.

First of all, there are parton shower event generators. These began in the mid 1980s and before with work that included
Pythia

EarlyPythia
,
Herwig

EarlyHerwig
,
Isajet

EarlyIsajet
and work by Gottschalk
Gottschalk
. Since then, they have become much more sophisticated, as reviewed in Ref.
SjostrandReview
. Parton shower event generators are very useful, even essential, for understanding events at electron-positron, electron-proton, and hadron-hadron colliders. A parton shower event generator is implemented in computer code that generates simulated events consisting of final state hadrons plus, sometimes, leptons and electro-weak vector bosons. The core of this event generation reflects perturbative QCD and generates partons (quarks and gluons). There is then a model of hadronization to turn the partons into hadrons.

The parton shower part of the event generator, leaving aside hadronization, is often described using the structure of its computer code. However, we find it useful to use a more abstract and more mathematical description. The mathematical description
NSI
uses linear algebra and the language of quantum statistical mechanics. We use states
|{\rho})
that describe the quantum numbers of any number,
m=2,3,\dots
, of partons. The space of such states is the
statistical space
. The quantum numbers for the partons consist, at a minimum, of their momenta and flavors:
\{p,f\}_{m}=\{p_{1},f_{1};p_{2},f_{2};\dots;p_{m},f_{m}\}
. Then there are states
|{\{p,f\}_{m}})
in which there are
m
partons such that parton 1 has momentum
p_{1}
and flavor
f_{1}
and, in general, parton
i
has momentum
p_{i}
and flavor
f_{i}
. These states form a basis for the statistical space. In this paper, we include also color and spin quantum numbers.

The states evolve. The evolution can be thought of as the solution of a renormalization group equation, in which a scale parameter
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
decreases from an initial high scale (say
1\ \mathrm{TeV}
) to a much lower scale of a few GeV, at which the shower is turned off.
2
2

2

There is also a renormalization scale
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
. In the final splitting functions, it is helpful to set
\mu_{\textsc{r}}=\mu_{\textsc{s}}
.
3
3

3

This description applies to first order showers with ordering variables related to transverse momentum or virtuality in a splitting. A first order angle ordered parton shower needs a somewhat different description.
(Then a hadronization model may be applied.) The shower scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
is often related to a transverse momentum in parton splittings. As
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
decreases, the number of partons either stays the same or increases. In a parton shower event generator, this evolution is quite literal. As the code runs, more and more partons are produced as partons split, with the scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
that describes the splittings decreasing at each step.

In the mathematical description of this process, the state at a smaller value of the scale is related to the starting state at a hard scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}=\mu_{\textsc{h}}
by a linear operator
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})
:

\big|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}\big)=\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})\big|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{h}})}\big)\;.

(1)

The operator
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})
obeys a renormalization group equation

\mu_{\textsc{s}}\frac{d}{d\mu_{\textsc{s}}}\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})=-\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})\;.

(2)

The solution of this can be written

\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})=\mathbb{T}\exp\!\left(\int_{\mu_{\textsc{s}}}^{\mu_{\textsc{h}}}\!\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\mathcal{S}(\mu)\right)\;,

(3)

where
\mathbb{T}
denotes ordering according to the scale, with smaller
\mu
to the left. The operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu)
is the shower splitting operator. It is typically evaluated at first order in perturbation theory, so that it is proportional to
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
. At first order, it either creates a new parton or leaves the number of partons unchanged.

The description presented in the previous three paragraphs can, we think, describe the common structure of many forms of what is meant by a parton shower.

Now we offer a caveat. We define as a parton shower the solution, in principle, of the evolution equation (
2
). We distinguish this from a parton shower event generator, which requires that the evolution equation be implemented in computer code. This implementation may require approximations, as discussed in Sec.
II
and later in Sec.
XIII
.

There is another caveat. At the end of the shower, one can apply an operator that measures an infrared safe observable
J
with scale
\mu_{\textsc{j}}
. Here infrared safety means that splittings below this scale do not affect the result beyond contributions suppressed by a power of the yet smaller scale divided by
\mu_{\textsc{j}}
. An infrared safe observable is calculable in QCD with corrections for hadronization neglected. When
\mu_{\textsc{j}}\ll\mu_{\textsc{h}}
, the value of the observable is typically affected by large logarithms,
\log(\mu_{\textsc{h}}/\mu_{\textsc{j}})
. One may wish to sum the contributions to the observable that contain the most powers of the logarithm for each power of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
. For analytical purposes, one typically considers a quantity
\mathcal{I}_{\textsc{j}}
equal to the logarithm of an appropriate integral transform of the observable. Then one asks how accurately
\mathcal{I}_{\textsc{j}}
is approximated. The accuracy of the summation is typically designated as leading logarithmic accuracy (LL), next-to-leading logarithmic accuracy (NLL),
etc
, This style of analysis is applied to particular sorts of infrared safe observables, typically those called
global event shapes
.

One way to sum large logarithms like these is by simulating events using a parton shower of the form of Eqs. (
1
), (
2
), and (
3
). Since the measurement of global event shapes provides a good test of QCD, the analysis of logarithmic accuracy is an important research problem. But it is not the subject of this paper.
4
4

4

We have, however, provided an analyses of the summation of logarithms in Refs.
NSThreshold
;
NSThresholdII
;
NSThrustSum
.
This paper is concerned with the application of a parton shower evolution equation to provide results for
any
infrared safe observable. For this purpose, we impose strict requirements on the nature of the statistical space and on the properties of the spitting operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu)
. These strict requirements are not met by many formulations of Eqs. (
1
), (
2
), and (
3
) that are useful for particular observables.

The variety of parton shower discussed in this paper may perhaps be called a
quantum parton shower
, borrowing from the title of Ref.
NSI
. For such a parton shower, we treat the operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu)
as having a perturbative expansion in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
:

\mathcal{S}(\mu)=\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\,\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\mu)+\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\right]^{2}\!\mathcal{S}^{(2)}(\mu)+\cdots\;.

(4)

Then we examine the effect of using QCD perturbation theory applied to any starting state
|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{h}})})
with high momentum partons whose momenta are not close to being collinear. After some number of splittings and virtual exchanges, we find infrared singularities, both in the form of factors
1/\epsilon
from integrations over virtual loop momenta in
4-2\epsilon
dimensions and in the form of singularities when subsets of produced partons become collinear with one another or soft (
p_{i}\to 0
). If the exponent in
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})
is truncated at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{N}
, then we require that the small
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
behavior of
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{h}})})
reflect these infrared singularities of QCD perturbation theory up to order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{N}
. We specify this requirement more fully later in this paper, especially in Sec.
IV
.

In order to make this work, we need to include color and spin as quantum numbers describing the partons. Furthermore, we need an amplitude (the ket amplitude) with
m
partons in the final state and a conjugate amplitude (the bra amplitude) with
m
partons in the final state. Both of these are given by a sum of Feynman diagrams. This means that we need basis states
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}})
for the statistical space, with
\{c,s\}_{m}
being the colors and spins for the ket amplitude and
\{c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m}
being the colors and spins for the bra amplitude. The splitting operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
is then an operator on the statistical space including colors and spins. The mathematical formulation of a quantum parton shower thus uses the language of quantum statistical mechanics, with
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}})
representing basis states for the density matrix in color and spin space.

The approach to a parton shower using the quantum density matrix in colors and spins, or just colors, is a topic of important recent research
AngelesMartinez:2018cfz
;
Forshaw:2019ver
;
Forshaw:2020wrq
;
Forshaw:2020wrq
;
DeAngelis:2020rvq
;
Holguin:2020joq
;
Hoche:2020pxj
;
Platzer:2020lbr
;
Platzer:2022jny
. To emphasize that both the ket amplitude in color and spin space and the bra amplitude evolve, this approach is sometimes called amplitude evolution. There has also been extensive work using other methods aimed at adding order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}
corrections to the splitting functions in a parton shower
JadachJHEP
;
JadachPolonica
;
HartgringNLO
;
SkandsNLO
;
HocheNLO1
;
HocheNLO2
;
HocheNLO3
.

II
From the evolution equation to an event generator

Our aim in this paper is to define an operator based formalism that yields evolution equations for a parton shower in electron-positron annihilation. The splitting functions for this shower evolution have contributions proportional to
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{1}
,
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}
, or, in principle, any order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}
. There is an approximation inherent in using a parton shower description: the scale
\mu
of a shower splitting should be substantially smaller than the scale of the preceding splitting. This approximation can be improved by using more terms in the perturbative expansion of the splitting functions. Aside from this approximation, the evolution equation is exact: it includes full quantum color; it includes full quantum spin; it includes interference between the bra and ket amplitudes.

As noted in the Introduction, the evolution equation (
2
) for a quantum parton shower as analyzed in this paper has the same general form as the evolution equation for a parton shower event generator. However, it is typically not possible to implement the exact evolution equation as a parton shower event generator. Having the exact evolution equation is analogous to having the exact Schrödinger equation for the energy levels of a molecule. We need this starting point, but it does not produce numerical results without some approximations. Similarly, one will need approximations to produce a practical Monte Carlo event generator from the exact quantum shower evolution equations.

An example of the barriers to implementing Eq. (
2
) for a quantum parton shower as a parton shower event generator concerns color. The most straightforward implementation of Eq. (
2
) without color would involve the exponential of an operator that does not change the number of partons or their momenta or flavors and represents the inclusive probability for the partonic state to generate a splitting between two scales. With color, the operator in the exponential acts as a matrix on the color space. However, color space for many partons is too large to allow a numerical calculation of this exponential.
5
5

5

For example, the color space for twenty partons has something like
(20!)^{2}\approx 10^{37}
dimensions.

Thus approximations are needed. The parton shower event generator can then be the solution of the evolution equation (
2
) using an approximate splitting operator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{approx}}
. One can then check how good the approximation is by calculating a cross section of interest using
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{approx}}
together with a finite number of contributions from
\Delta\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}-\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{approx}}
. The contributions proportional to
[\Delta\mathcal{S}]^{n}
should then be reasonably small. An example of this approach is available in Ref.
NSNewColor
.

In this paper, we define a formalism that can generate a parton shower evolution equation that fully accounts for the requirements of quantum mechanics. We also apply this to define first order splitting functions
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
in this formalism. We address the issues in implementing the resulting shower evolution approximately as a parton shower event generator only briefly, in Sec.
XIII
.

III
Singularities of QCD perturbation theory

The quantum parton shower formalism presented in this paper is based on the infrared (IR) singularities of QCD as seen in electron-positron annihilation. We consider
e^{+}e^{-}\to hadrons
. The analysis of the production of electroweak bosons or other particles that do not carry color is similar, but we do not address the changes needed to go beyond
e^{+}e^{-}\to hadrons
in this paper. In addition, we treat all quarks as massless in order to keep the theory as simple as possible.

Consider a perturbative contribution to the QCD ket amplitude times the bra amplitude for the cross section to produce
m
partons in electron-positron annihilation. These amplitudes include self-energy diagrams on final state parton lines with coefficients determined from the perturbative expansion of the Lehmann-Symanzik-Zimmermann (LSZ) factors on the final state parton lines described in Sec.
VI.4
.

Let
Q
denote the total
e^{+}e^{-}
momentum. The parton momenta are
\hat{p}_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,\hat{m}\}
. For the Feynman diagrams considered, there can be poles
1/\epsilon
in
d=4-2\epsilon
dimensions coming from virtual loop integrations and there can be singularities when some of the partons become collinear to one another and some partons become soft in the sense that their momenta approach zero. For the collinear singularities, there will be some number of directions in momentum space, labeled
j
, such that, for each of these directions, a subset
J(j)
of the momenta become collinear with their total momentum,

p_{j}=\sum_{i\in J(j)}\hat{p}_{i}\;.

(5)

That is, in the collinear limit,
\hat{p}_{i}=x_{i}p_{j}
for
i\in J(j)
. There is also a set
S
of soft final state partons, whose momenta approach zero in the limit. That is, in the soft limit,
\hat{p}_{i}=0
for
i\in S
. Thus in the singular limit, there are
m\leq\hat{m}
infinitely narrow jets with momenta
p_{j}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
..

When we speak of a singularity of a Feynman graph for a ket amplitude times a bra amplitude, it is important to note how strong a singularity is. We always have in mind integrating over a region near the singularity with the covariant measure given in Eq. (
22
) below and using integrations
d^{d}\ell
for the virtual loops. We are interested in singularities that are strong enough to produce a logarithmically divergent integration if we integrate in
d=4
dimensions or a pole
1/\epsilon^{n}
if we integrate in
4-2\epsilon
dimensions. We call this a
logarithmic singularity
. Weaker singularities are not significant in our analysis.

The relation of these singularities to the Feynman graphs is rather simple to understand if we work in a suitable gauge, such as the interpolating gauge of Doust
Doust
and of Baulieu and Zwanziger
BaulieuZwanziger
. In Ref.
Gauge
, we have explored the properties of this gauge that make it useful for the description of parton showers. In interpolating gauge, gluons exchanged between partons with momenta in different directions generate only soft divergences. Furthermore, the interference between emitting a gluon from one parton in the ket amplitude and another parton in the bra amplitude generates only a soft divergence.

One can use Feynman gauge for the analysis of the infrared singularities, but then one must apply Ward identities to bring the singularity structure into its simplest form. This is a significant complication, so we use interpolating gauge in this paper.

The gluon propagator in interpolating gauge is
Gauge
:

D^{\mu\nu}(q)=\frac{N^{\mu\nu}(q)}{q^{2}+{\mathrm{i}}0}\;,

(6)

where

\begin{split}&N^{\mu\nu}(q)=-g^{\mu\nu}+\frac{q^{\mu}\,\tilde{q}^{\nu}+\tilde{q}^{\mu}\,q^{\nu}}{q\cdot\tilde{q}+{\mathrm{i}}0}\\
&\quad-\left(1+\frac{1}{v^{2}}\right)\frac{q^{\mu}\,q^{\nu}}{q\cdot\tilde{q}+{\mathrm{i}}0}-\frac{\xi-1}{v^{2}}\,\frac{q^{2}\,q^{\mu}q^{\nu}}{(q\cdot\tilde{q}+{\mathrm{i}}0)^{2}}\;.\end{split}

(7)

In
D^{\mu\nu}(q)
,
v
is a parameter with
1<v<\infty
. For instance, one might choose
v=2
. There is another parameter
\xi
, which we usually choose to be
\xi=1
. There is also a vector
n
, which we set to
n=Q/\sqrt{Q^{2}}
. The vector
\tilde{q}
is related to
q
in a frame in which
n=(1,\vec{0}\,)
by

\begin{split}\tilde{q}^{0}={}&\frac{1}{v^{2}}\,q^{0}\;,\\
\tilde{q}^{i}={}&q^{i}\;,\hskip 28.45274pti\in\{1,2,3\}\;.\end{split}

(8)

The gauge propagator has two contributions
Gauge
,

D^{\mu\nu}(q)=D^{\mu\nu}_{\mathrm{T}}(q)+D^{\mu\nu}_{\mathrm{L}}(q)\;.

(9)

The propagator for
T
gluons is

D^{\mu\nu}_{\mathrm{T}}(q)=\frac{1}{q^{2}+{\mathrm{i}}0}\sum_{s=\pm 1}\varepsilon^{\mu}(q,s)^{*}\,\varepsilon^{\nu}(q,s)\;,

(10)

where the polarization vectors are solutions of
q\cdot\varepsilon(q,s)=n\cdot\varepsilon(q,s)=0
with
\varepsilon^{\star}(q,s)\cdot\varepsilon(q,s^{\prime})=-\delta_{ss^{\prime}}
. Physical cross sections are constructed from the S matrix for quarks, antiquarks, and T gluons.

The propagator for L gluons is constructed from polarization vectors in the plane of
n
and
q
. In a frame in which
n=(1,\vec{0}\,)
,
D^{\mu\nu}_{\mathrm{L}}(q)
is

\begin{split}D^{\mu\nu}_{\mathrm{L}}(q)={}&\frac{1}{q\cdot\tilde{q}+{\mathrm{i}}0}\bigg\{\frac{(q^{\mu}-q\cdot n\,n^{\mu})(q^{\nu}-q\cdot n\,n^{\nu})}{v^{2}\,\vec{q}^{\,2}}\\
&\quad-n^{\mu}n^{\nu}-\frac{\xi-1}{v^{2}}\,\frac{q^{\mu}q^{\nu}}{q\cdot\tilde{q}+{\mathrm{i}}0}\bigg\}\;.\end{split}

(11)

Note that
D^{\mu\nu}_{\mathrm{L}}(q)
does not have a pole at
q^{2}=0
. Thus
D^{\mu\nu}_{\mathrm{L}}(q)
is not singular when
q
is collinear with a lightlike momentum.

As argued above, in interpolating gauge, the infrared singularities arise from graphs in which the
m
infinitely narrow jets arise from
\hat{m}
on-shell partons with momenta
p_{i}
that emerge from a hard density-matrix element. This is illustrated in Fig.
1
, in which two of the
\hat{m}
partons are grouped into one infinitely narrow jet and there is a
1/\epsilon
factor that arises from the exchange of an infinitely soft gluon.

To start with, the loop momentum
\ell
of a virtual gluon that is part of a soft exchange in the ket amplitude flows through the hard subgraph for the amplitude or the conjugate amplitude. It is the integration over
\ell
that produces a pole
1/\epsilon
. Because
\ell
is soft, we set
\ell\to 0
inside the hard subgraph. If we expand the hard subgraph in powers of
\ell
, contributions with any power greater than zero do not produce a
1/\epsilon
pole.

The logarithmic infrared singularities have a simple flavor structure. The final state partons have flavors
\hat{f}_{i}
. A parton with
i\in S
not only has
\hat{p}_{i}=0
in the singular limit but also must be a gluon, so that emitting this parton does not change the flavor of the emitting parton. Thus the flavor of jet
j
is

f_{j}=\sum_{i\in J(j)}\hat{f}_{i}\;,

(12)

with summation of flavors defined in a simple way as adding vectors in an
N_{\mathrm{f}}
dimensional vector space, where
N_{\mathrm{f}}
is the number of flavors and the gluon is represented by the zero vector. The same result is obtained using the Feynman rules for the diagram for the ket amplitude or the diagram for the bra amplitude.

Figure 1:
Example of singularities in QCD.

Thus the infrared singularities are represented by graphs like Fig.
1
, in which the yellow blobs represent hard subgraphs that produce
m
on-shell partons with momenta
p_{j}
and flavors
f_{j}
. In this example,
m=\hat{m}-1
.

In each graph that exhibits an infrared singularity, there are
m
partons emerging from the hard density matrix. In the singular limit, they have momenta
p_{j}
, with
p_{j}^{2}=0
, and flavors
f_{j}
. Notice that
p_{j}
and
f_{j}
are the same in the hard scattering ket amplitude and in the hard scattering bra amplitude since they are determined by Eqs. (
5
) and (
12
). The on-shell partons in the hard scattering ket amplitude have colors
\{c\}_{m}
and spins
\{s\}_{m}
. The on-shell partons in the hard scattering bra amplitude have colors
\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}
and spins
\{s^{\prime}\}_{m}
. Thus the description of the infrared singularities requires colors
\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}
, spins
\{s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}
, momenta
\{p\}_{m}
and flavors
\{f\}_{m}
. Separate momenta and flavors for the bra and ket amplitudes are not required.

IV
Infrared singularities in the statistical space

In this section, we review the general formulation of a quantum parton shower given in Ref.
NSAllOrder
as it applies to electron-positron annihilation. In this formulation, the soft and collinear singularities of QCD are represented by an operator
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
that has a perturbative expansion in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
. Then the parton shower evolution operator
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})
is derived from
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
using Eq. (
32
) below. The splitting operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu)
is obtained from the derivative of
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})
according to Eq. (
2
).

The operator
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
acts on a linear vector space, the statistical space. We turn first to a description of this space.

IV.1
Statistical space

As stated in Sec.
I
, we view a parton shower using the language of quantum statistical mechanics. At any stage of the shower, specified by a scale parameter
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
, the state of the partons is described by a vector
|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{s}})})
in a vector space that we refer to as the
statistical space
. This vector tells how many partons there are and what their quantum numbers are. In the earliest versions of parton showers
EarlyPythia
;
EarlyHerwig
;
EarlyIsajet
, the quantum numbers consisted of just the momenta and flavors of the partons, specified by a list
\{p,f\}_{m}=\{p_{1},f_{1};p_{2},f_{2};\dots p_{m},f_{m}\}
. Then
|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{s}})})
was a linear combination of basis states
|{\{p,f\}_{m}})
. With just momenta and flavors,
({\{p,f\}_{m}}|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{s}})})
represents the probability that there are
m
partons with momenta and flavors
\{p,f\}_{m}
. As we have seen in the previous section, we need separate colors and spins for the bra and ket amplitudes. Our description of colors and spins follows Ref.
NSI
.

Each gluon has a spin index
s
, which takes
2-2\epsilon
values when we work in a
d=4-2\epsilon
dimensional space-time. These values correspond to the
2-2\epsilon
choices for the polarization vector
\varepsilon(p,s)
with
p\cdot\varepsilon(p,s)=n\cdot\varepsilon(p,s)=0
, with the normalization
-\varepsilon(p,s)\cdot\varepsilon(p,s^{\prime})^{*}=\delta_{s,s^{\prime}}
.
6
6

6

When
\epsilon=0
we use helicities.
Similarly, each quark or antiquark has a spin index s that takes two values. Then we use spin basis states
\big|{\{s\}_{m}}\big\rangle=\big|{\{s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{m}\}}\big\rangle
labeled by the spin indices. We take the spin basis states to be orthogonal and normalized:

\big\langle{\{s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{s\}_{m}}\big\rangle=\delta_{\{s\}_{m}}^{\{s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\;.

(13)

Each gluon has a color index
c
, which takes
N_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-1
values. Here
N_{\mathrm{c}}
is the number of colors, which we take to be possibly different from 3. Each quark or antiquark has a color index
c
, which takes
N_{\mathrm{c}}
values. Instead of specifying the colors for the individual partons, it is useful to specify the color state of all of the
m
partons together, restricting the joint state to be invariant under rotations in the color space. That is, the partons together are in a color singlet state. Then we use color basis states for
m
partons that we designate as
\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle
. There are many possible choices for color basis states. We prefer the trace basis
tracebasis
, as specified in Sec. 7 of Ref.
NSI
. Other investigators prefer different choices of basis
colorflowbasis
;
orthogonalbasis
. For the purposes of this paper, we do not need to choose a color basis as long as each state
\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle
is a color singlet.

We allow for the possibility that the color basis states
|{\{c\}_{m}}\rangle
are not orthogonal and normalized, as is the case for the trace basis. Then we also define a dual basis
|{\{c\}_{m}}\rangle_{\textsc{d}}
such that

\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle=\delta_{\{c\}_{m}}^{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\;,

(14)

as discussed later at Eq. (
81
). For spin, we always choose an orthogonal and normalized basis, so that
|{\{s\}_{m}}\rangle_{\textsc{d}}=|{\{s\}_{m}}\rangle
.

To include color and spin, we use the language of quantum statistical mechanics. In this description, a pure state in the color and spin space is a quantum vector

\big|{\Psi}\big\rangle=\sum_{\{c,s\}_{m}}\psi(\{c,s\}_{m})\big|{\{c,s\}_{m}}\big\rangle\;.

(15)

The associated probability for this state is

\begin{split}\big\langle{\Psi}\big|{\Psi}\big\rangle={}&\sum_{\{c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\psi^{*}(\{c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m})\,\psi(\{c,s\}_{m})\\
&\times\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle\big\langle{\{s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{s\}_{m}}\big\rangle\;.\end{split}

(16)

A statistical mixture of pure states is described by a mixed state

\begin{split}\big|{\rho}\big)={}&\!\!\!\sum_{\{c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\!\!\!\rho(\{c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m})\big|{\{c,s\}_{m}}\big\rangle\big\langle{\{c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|.\end{split}

(17)

The function
\rho
here gives the matrix elements of the density matrix in spin and color. The probability for this mixed state is

\begin{split}P={}&\!\!\sum_{\{c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\!\!\rho(\{c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m})\big\langle{\{c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{c,s\}_{m}}\big\rangle\;.\end{split}

(18)

We define basis vectors
\big|{\{c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)
in the color and spin part of the statistical space so that
\big|{\rho}\big)
in Eq. (
17
) is written as

\begin{split}\big|{\rho}\big)={}&\!\!\!\sum_{\{c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\!\!\!\rho(\{c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m})\big|{\{c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(19)

Including color and spin, a set of
m
partons is produced starting with a set of fewer hard partons according to a Feynman graph for the ket amplitude. These partons have a color and spin state
|{\{c,s\}_{m}}\rangle
. This set of partons, with the same momenta and flavors
\{p,f\}_{m}
, is produced according to a possibly different Feynman graph for the bra amplitude, starting from
m
partons in a possibly different color and spin state. These partons have a color and spin state
\langle{\{c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m}}|
.

With this description, the partonic state
|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{s}})})
is expanded in basis vectors according to

\begin{split}\big|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}\big)=\sum_{m}\frac{1}{{m}!}\int{}&d\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}\\
&\times\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
&\times\big({\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}\big)\;.\end{split}

(20)

A factor
1/{m}!
is included because the partons are treated as identical. The integration measure is

\begin{split}\int\!d\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}={}&\int\!d\{p\}_{m}\sum_{\{f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\;,\end{split}

(21)

where

\begin{split}\int\!d\{p\}_{m}={}&\prod_{i=1}^{m}\left\{\int\!\frac{d^{4-2\epsilon}p_{i}}{(2\pi)^{4-2\epsilon}}\,(2\pi)\,\delta_{+}(p_{i}^{2})\right\}\\
&\times(2\pi)^{4-2\epsilon}\delta\!\left(\sum_{i}p_{i}-Q\right)\;.\end{split}

(22)

The completeness relation for the basis states is

\begin{split}1={}&\sum_{m}\frac{1}{{m}!}\int\!d\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}\\
&\times\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\big({\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\;.\end{split}

(23)

At the end of the perturbative shower at scale
\mu_{\mathrm{f}}
, we have a state
|{\rho(\mu_{\mathrm{f}})})
. This state is expanded as a linear combination of basis states as in Eq. (
20
). Assuming that we do not need to apply a hadronization model, we next apply an operator
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}
that measures an observable on this state. That is

\begin{split}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}&\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
&=J(\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m})\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;,\end{split}

(24)

where the eigenvalue
J
is a numerical function that gives the value of the observable for partons with the quantum numbers specified. Typically,
J
is a function only of the momenta of the partons, but we can allow for a more general dependence.
7
7

7

The partons described in the basis states
\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}
carry labels
i=1,\dots,m
. However, we take the
m
partons to be physically identical, distinguished only by their different quantum numbers. Thus for any allowed observable
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}
, we have
J(\{\tilde{p},\tilde{f},\tilde{c},\tilde{c}^{\prime},\tilde{s},\tilde{s}^{\prime}\}_{m})=J(\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m})
when
\tilde{p}_{i}=p_{\pi(i)}
and likewise for the other quantum numbers for any permutation
\pi\in S_{m}
.

Finally, we multiply by a statistical bra state
({1}|
defined by

\begin{split}\big({1}&\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
&\quad=\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle\big\langle{\{s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{s\}_{m}}\big\rangle\,.\end{split}

(25)

The quantum inner products give the probability for color and spin parts of the statistical basis states. This gives us

\begin{split}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\hskip-62.59596pt&\\
={}&J(\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m})\\
&\times\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle\big\langle{\{s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{s\}_{m}}\big\rangle\,.\end{split}

(26)

IV.2
Infrared singular operator

The representation of singularities of QCD in Fig.
1
is instructive, but not yet adapted for constructing a parton shower. The problem is that it depicts what happens only exactly at an infrared singularity. For instance, if
\hat{p}_{k}
and
\hat{p}_{l}
are the momenta of two final state partons, we can represent the singularity when
\hat{p}_{k}=\lambda_{k}p_{j}
and
\hat{p}_{l}=(1-\lambda_{k})p_{j}
, but it does not help away from this limit. What we need is an approximation that is built from the singular subdiagram like that in the example in Fig.
1
but that multiplies the singular factor by the hard subdiagram evaluated exactly at the singularity. This approximate version is illustrated in Fig.
2
.

{\cal D}^{(1,1)}(G)

Figure 2:

Example of the contribution of a particular graph
G
with one real emission and one virtual exchange to the singular operator
{\cal D}^{(1,1)}
. The translation from the graph to a contribution to
{\cal D}
has several steps. This figure is a representation of Eq. (
XI.4
) later in the paper.

Let us denote the momentum of parton
j
as it enters the singular amplitude by
q_{j}
. The singular diagram conserves momentum exactly, so that
q_{j}
is a linear combination of the final state momenta
\hat{p}_{i}
and the momenta of exchanged virtual gluons. When the singular amplitude is evaluated close to, but not exactly at, the singular limit for the
\hat{p}_{i}
and with soft exchanged gluons close to having zero momentum, the resulting momentum
q_{j}
is close to but not exactly equal to
p_{j}
.

We need one more ingredient. As we integrate over the splitting parameters, we get further from the singular limit. We introduce a scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
that restricts how far the final state after splittings deviates from the final state at the singular point. For instance, one could say that the transverse momentum in a nearly collinear splitting is restricted to
k_{\perp}^{2}<\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2}
. There is evidently some freedom in choosing the definition of the integration restrictions imposed by
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
. We propose what we think is a sensible set of choices in Sec.
VII.2
below. In the formalism of this paper,
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
does not restrict integrations over virtual loops.

This construction will define a perturbative operator
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
that operates on the statistical space. This operator depends on the shower scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
. We construct
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
so that it reproduces the poles and logarithmic singularities of QCD order by order in perturbation theory. This requirement allows some freedom: we have to match singularities, but finite remainders remain unspecified. We arrange the definition so that the inclusive probability
\big({1}\big|{\rho}\big)
associated with a state
\rho
is not changed by applying
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
:

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\big({1}\big|\;.

(27)

Acting on a statistical basis state
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}})
,
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
gives a linear combination of basis states
|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}})
according to

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}}

\displaystyle)\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)

(28)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\hat{m}\geq m}\frac{1}{\hat{m}!}\int\!d\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}\,\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big)

\displaystyle\times\big({\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big|\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.

The operator
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
has a perturbative expansion

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}})}{2\pi}\right]^{n}\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{r}})\;.\end{split}

(29)

Here
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
is the renormalization scale that is used in the
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
renormalization of virtual diagrams. We retain
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
as a free parameter. Normally, we do not indicate the dependence of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}})
or the coefficients
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{r}})
on
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
.

At
n
th order, there are terms with
n_{\textsc{r}}
real emissions and
n_{\textsc{v}}
virtual loops:

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\mathop{\sum_{n_{\textsc{r}}=0}^{n}\sum_{n_{\textsc{v}}=0}^{n}}_{n_{\textsc{r}}+n_{\textsc{v}}=n}\mathcal{D}^{(n_{\textsc{r}},n_{\textsc{v}})}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\;.\end{split}

(30)

To define the shower operator
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})
, we also use the inverse operator to
\mathcal{D}
, which is defined by its perturbative expansion:

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}^{-1}={}&1-\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\right]\mathcal{D}^{(1)}-\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\right]^{2}\left\{\mathcal{D}^{(2)}-\mathcal{D}^{(1)}\mathcal{D}^{(1)}\right\}\\
&+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})\;.\end{split}

(31)

The shower evolution operator is defined using
\mathcal{D}(\mu^{2})
by
NSAllOrder

\begin{split}\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})={}&\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})\;.\end{split}

(32)

Then the splitting operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
is defined by Eq. (
2
).

When we apply the perturbative operator
\mathcal{D}^{(n_{\textsc{r}},n_{\textsc{v}})}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
to a state
\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)
with
m
partons, we obtain states with
\hat{m}=m+n_{\textsc{r}}
partons. We define
\mathcal{D}^{(n_{\textsc{r}},n_{\textsc{v}})}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
so that the corresponding matrix elements contain powers of
1/\epsilon
and singularities when the
\hat{m}
final state parton momenta
\hat{p}_{j}
are grouped into
m
infinitely narrow jets. These
1/\epsilon
poles and collinear and soft singularities must match the corresponding poles and singularities of QCD, including their dependence on color and spin indices.

IV.3
Momentum mapping

We need to define the momenta in the singular diagrams that contribute to
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
. We start with momenta
p_{j}
for
m
partons. Then we emit partons, so that the there are
\hat{m}
final state partons with momenta
\hat{p}_{i}
. We require that the parton shower evolution maintain momentum conservation:

\sum_{i=1}^{\hat{m}}\hat{p}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}p_{j}\;.

(33)

A massless parton cannot split into more than one massless partons with non-zero momenta when the daughter partons are not exactly collinear. Additionally, when a soft gluon is emitted, momentum conservation cannot hold if the momenta of the original partons remain the same. Thus we need a momentum mapping that transfers some momentum between partons in order to retain momentum conservation.

Let
G
label the combination of Feynman graphs used for the bra and ket amplitudes. Define splitting variables
\zeta_{G}
that are adapted to the structure of the singular diagrams. The splitting variables
\zeta_{G}
can include, for example, the momentum fraction
1-z
and transverse momentum
k_{\mathrm{T}}
of an emitted gluon. Also define an integration measure
d\zeta_{G}
over the splitting variables. We define the final state momenta
\hat{p}_{j}
as functions of the
m
initial momenta
p_{i}
and splitting variables
\zeta_{G}
:

\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}=R(G;\zeta_{G},\{p\}_{m})\;.

(34)

This momentum mapping function obeys momentum conservation, Eq. (
33
). A familiar example of this that is sometimes used for a first order dipole shower is the momentum mapping of Catani and Seymour
CataniSeymour
.

We make different choices. When parton
l
splits to partons
l
and
m\!+\!1
in both the bra and ket amplitudes, there is a singularity when
\hat{p}_{l}
and
\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}
become collinear with
p_{l}
. Exactly at the singular point, the momenta of the remaining partons are unchanged:
\hat{p}_{i}=p_{i}
. When
\hat{p}_{l}
and
\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}
are not exactly collinear with
p_{l}
, we choose for
i\leq m
,
i\neq l
,

\hat{p}_{i}^{\mu}=\lambda\,\Lambda^{\mu}_{\ \nu}p_{i}^{\nu}\;,

(35)

where
\lambda
is a scalar and
\Lambda^{\mu}_{\ \nu}
is a Lorentz transformation, which are specified in Appendix
A
.

When one or more partons are emitted near a soft singularity, it is simplest to choose a momentum mapping that does not depend on which hard partons the soft partons were emitted from. We choose a momentum mapping given by a scaling and a Lorentz transformation for all of the partons
i
with
i\leq m
, as in Eq. (
35
). The definition of
\lambda
and
\Lambda^{\mu}_{\ \nu}
is given in Appendix
B
.

The definitions in Appendices
A
and
B
provide a global momentum mapping, in which the needed momentum for momentum conservation is taken from all of the remaining partons in the event. This contrasts with a local momentum mapping, in which the needed momentum is taken just one parton, as in Ref.
CataniSeymour
. The effects of these two kinds of mapping were compared in Ref.
panscales
.

IV.4
Splitting function

Combining Eq. (
2
) and Eq. (
32
) allows us to express the splitting operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
in terms of the derivative of
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
:

\mu_{\textsc{s}}\frac{d\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{d\mu_{\textsc{s}}}=\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\,\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\;.

(36)

This has the form of an evolution equation for
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
. The solution of this with a boundary condition at
\mu_{\textsc{s}}=\mu_{\mathrm{f}}
is

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}})^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}},\mu_{\textsc{s}})\;,\end{split}

(37)

where

\begin{split}\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}},\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&\mathbb{T}\exp\left(\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}}^{\mu_{\textsc{s}}}\!\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\mathcal{S}(\mu)\right)\;.\end{split}

(38)

Here
\mathbb{T}
denotes ordering according to the scale, with larger
\mu^{2}
to the right. If we expand Eq. (
38
) in powers of
\mathcal{S}
, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}},\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&1+\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}}^{\mu_{\textsc{s}}}\!\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\mathcal{S}(\mu)\\
&+\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}}^{\mu_{\textsc{s}}}\!\frac{d\mu_{1}}{\mu_{1}}\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}}^{\mu_{1}}\!\frac{d\mu_{2}}{\mu_{2}}\ \mathcal{S}(\mu_{2})\,\mathcal{S}(\mu_{1})\\
&+\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}}^{\mu_{\textsc{s}}}\!\frac{d\mu_{1}}{\mu_{1}}\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}}^{\mu_{1}}\!\frac{d\mu_{2}}{\mu_{2}}\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}}^{\mu_{2}}\!\frac{d\mu_{3}}{\mu_{3}}\\
&\quad\times\mathcal{S}(\mu_{3})\,\mathcal{S}(\mu_{2})\,\mathcal{S}(\mu_{1})\\
&+\cdots\;.\end{split}

(39)

At first order, this is

\mathcal{U}^{(1)}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}},\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}}^{\mu_{\textsc{s}}}\!\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\ \mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\mu)\;.

(40)

At second order,

\begin{split}\mathcal{U}^{(2)}&(\mu_{\mathrm{f}},\mu_{\textsc{s}})\\
={}&\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}}^{\mu_{\textsc{s}}}\!\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\ S^{(2)}(\mu^{2})\\
&+\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}}^{\mu_{\textsc{s}}}\!\frac{d\mu_{1}}{\mu_{1}}\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}}^{\mu_{1}}\!\frac{d\mu_{2}}{\mu_{2}}\,\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\mu_{2})\,\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\mu_{1})\;.\end{split}

(41)

The second order infrared singular operator
\mathcal{D}^{(2)}
typically contains nested singularities. For instance, one can have one collinear splitting at a scale
\mu_{1}
followed by a second collinear splitting at a much smaller scale
\mu_{2}
. This corresponds to a first order interaction that is generated by
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\mu_{1})
followed by a second first order interaction that is generated by
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\mu_{2})
. Of course, two first order interactions will occur in a parton shower, which generates any number of first order splittings or virtual interactions contained in
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}
. However, this product of two
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}
should not be part of the second order splitting operator
\mathcal{S}^{(2)}
. This is the structure indicated in Eq. (
41
).

IV.5
Need for a bigger space

In order to generate the operators
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
and the splitting operators
\mathcal{S}(\mu)
beyond leading order in
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
, we need to use the Feynman diagrams for QCD, with the goal of extracting their associated logarithmic infrared singularities. There are many Feynman diagrams, and their infrared singularities are not always simple. Our method to attack this problem is to represent the Feynman diagrams as linear operators on a vector space. Then we can use linear algebra to simplify their structure.

Inside a Feynman diagram for the ket amplitude or the bra amplitude, partons do not have definite spins, and the most useful description of color may be different from what we use to describe color in the statistical space used for on-shell partons. It is especially significant that the momenta of parton lines inside a Feynman diagram are generally not on shell. Furthermore, the momenta and flavors inside a Feynman diagram for the ket amplitude are not the same as the momenta inside a Feynman diagram for the bra amplitude. Indeed, these two diagrams are typically not the same.

For these reasons, the statistical space described in the previous section is not adequate for the purpose at hand: we will need a bigger vector space. The vector space that we propose has a structure adapted to the Feynman diagrams. We devote much of this paper to defining this space. The construction used to build up Feynman diagrams from elementary operators also has significant structure. We also devote much of this paper to explaining this structure. We leave for later papers the construction of order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}
diagrams and the extraction of their infrared singularities. In this paper, we illustrate the formalism by applying it to order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{1}
diagrams.

V
Quantum ket amplitude space

We need a precise language suitable for the description of Feynman diagrams for the ket quantum amplitude and for the conjugate bra quantum amplitude.

V.1
Vector space for the ket amplitude

We consider a Feynman diagram for the amplitude for the emission of some number of partons starting with a hard state with
m_{0}
partons. Inside the Feynman diagram, at an intermediate stage there are
m
partons with labels
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. Parton
i
has momentum
q_{i}
. These momenta are generally off shell. Each parton also has a flavor
f_{i}\in\{\mathrm{g},\mathrm{u},\bar{\mathrm{u}},\mathrm{d},\bar{\mathrm{d}},\dots\}
.

Each parton has a color index
a
with
a\in\{1,\dots,N_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-1\}
if it is a gluon or
a\in\{1,\dots,N_{\mathrm{c}}\}
if it is a quark or antiquark. The corresponding color basis states for parton
i
are
\big|{a_{i}}\big\rangle
with

\big\langle{\bar{a}_{i}}\big|{a_{i}}\big\rangle=\delta_{\bar{a}_{i},a_{i}}\;.

(42)

This contrasts with the description of color in the statistical space in Sec.
IV.1
, where we allow a basis for the color space that is not orthogonal and normalized, as we describe later in Eqs. (
81
) and (
82
).

The parton also carries an index
r
that specifies the component of the parton wave function that carries its Lorentz transformation properties: the
(1/2,0)\oplus(0,1/2)
representation for a quark or antiquark, or the
(1/2,1/2)
representation for a gluon. We now consider the
r
index in some detail.

Gluons.
Consider first the basis vectors for space-time vectors that represent gluons. Written in terms of the vector components, the inner product for vectors is the Lorentz invariant inner product

\big\langle{A}\big|{B}\big\rangle=A^{*}_{\nu}B^{\nu}=(A^{*})^{\mu}g_{\mu\nu}B^{\nu}\;.

(43)

We define basis vectors
|{r}\rangle
such that

\begin{split}\big|{B}\big\rangle={}&\sum_{r}\big|{r}\big\rangle B^{r}\;.\end{split}

(44)

Then also

\big\langle{A}\big|=\sum_{r}(A^{*})^{r}\big\langle{r}\big|\;.

(45)

Then

\big\langle{A}\big|{B}\big\rangle=\sum_{r,\bar{r}}(A^{*})^{r}\big\langle{r}\big|{\bar{r}}\big\rangle B^{\bar{r}}\;.

(46)

Thus the inner product of two basis vectors is the metric tensor

\big\langle{r}\big|{\bar{r}}\big\rangle=g_{r\bar{r}}\;.

(47)

It is also useful to define dual basis vectors
|{r}\rangle_{\textsc{d}}
by

\big|{r}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}=g^{r\bar{r}}\big|{\bar{r}}\big\rangle\;.

(48)

Then

\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{r}\big|{\bar{r}}\big\rangle=\delta_{\bar{r}}^{r}\;.

(49)

Using the dual basis vectors, we have the completeness relations

\sum_{r}\big|{r}\big\rangle\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{r}\big|=1\;.

(50)

The components of vectors
\big\langle{A}\big|
and
\big|{B}\big\rangle
can be obtained by taking inner products with the basis vectors:

\begin{split}\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{r}\big|{B}\big\rangle={}&B^{r}\;,\\
\big\langle{A}\big|{r}\big\rangle={}&A^{*}_{r}\;.\end{split}

(51)

Quarks.
For quark partons, we need Dirac spinors. If
A
and
B
are Dirac spinors, we use the inner product

\big\langle{A}\big|{B}\big\rangle=\bar{A}B=A^{*}_{\alpha}(\gamma^{0})_{\alpha\beta}B_{\beta}\;.

(52)

Here
\gamma^{0}
plays the role of the metric tensor in the spinor space. We follow the usual convention that uses the bar in
\bar{A}
with indices left implicit instead of displaying raised or lowered indices.

In order to have a notation that applies for both quarks and gluons, we choose a representation of the gamma matrices in which
\gamma^{0}
is a real, symmetric matrix

\gamma^{0}_{ij}=(\gamma^{0}_{ij})^{*}=\gamma^{0}_{ji}\;.

(53)

We also use
\gamma^{0}\gamma^{\mu\,\dagger}\gamma^{0}=\gamma^{\mu}
.

We define basis vectors
\big|{r}\big\rangle
such that

\begin{split}\big|{B}\big\rangle={}&\sum_{r}\big|{r}\big\rangle B_{r}\;.\end{split}

(54)

Then also

\begin{split}\big\langle{A}\big|={}&\sum_{r}(A^{*})_{r}\big\langle{r}\big|\;,\end{split}

(55)

so

\big\langle{A}\big|{B}\big\rangle=\sum_{r,\bar{r}}A^{*}_{r}\big\langle{r}\big|{\bar{r}}\big\rangle B_{\bar{r}}\;.

(56)

Thus the inner product of two basis vectors is the spinor version of the metric tensor

\big\langle{r}\big|{\bar{r}}\big\rangle=\gamma^{0}_{r\bar{r}}\;.

(57)

We define dual basis vectors by

\big|{r}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}=\gamma^{0}_{r\bar{r}}\big|{\bar{r}}\big\rangle\;.

(58)

Then

\begin{split}\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{r}\big|{\bar{r}}\big\rangle={}&\delta_{r\bar{r}}\;.\end{split}

(59)

This gives us the completeness relation

\begin{split}\sum_{r}\big|{r}\big\rangle\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{r}\big|={}&1\;.\end{split}

(60)

The components of vectors
\big\langle{A}\big|
and
\big|{B}\big\rangle
can be obtained by taking inner products with the basis vectors:

\begin{split}\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{r}\big|{B}\big\rangle={}&B_{r}\;,\\
\big\langle{A}\big|{r}\big\rangle={}&\bar{A}_{r}\;.\end{split}

(61)

It may be helpful to note that there is another completeness relation:
\sum_{r}|{r}\rangle_{\textsc{d}}\,\langle{r}|=1
. However, this relation is not as useful as Eq. (
60
) because
\langle{r}|{B}\rangle
is
(\gamma^{0}B)_{r}
rather than
B_{r}
and
\langle{A}|{r}\rangle_{\textsc{d}}
is
(\bar{A}\gamma^{0})_{r}
rather than
\bar{A}_{r}
.

Antiquarks.
The description of antiquarks is the same as for quarks, with the same basis vectors
|{r}\rangle
and
|{r}\rangle_{\textsc{d}}
. The difference between quarks and antiquarks comes in the Feynman rules: an incoming antiquark is represented by a spinor
\bar{V}
instead of
U
, an outgoing antiquark is represented by a spinor
V
instead of
\bar{U}
, and the order of
\gamma
matrices is reversed along an antiquark line compared to a quark line.

Combined quantum numbers.
As outlined above, each parton is described by a set of quantum numbers
\{q_{i},f_{i},a_{i},r_{i}\}
. In order to make our formulas more compact, we adopt the abbreviation

w_{i}=\{q_{i},f_{i},a_{i},r_{i}\}

(62)

for the quantum numbers that describe a parton in a Feynman diagram. Thus we use basis states
|{w_{i}}\rangle
to describe the state of one parton. The dual basis states are
{}_{\textsc{d}}\!\langle{w_{i}}|
, where the dual basis vectors differ from the basis vectors
\langle{w_{i}}|
only in their vector/spinor factor
\langle{r_{i}}|
. The inner product is

\begin{split}\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\bar{w}_{i}}\big|{w_{i}}\big\rangle={}&\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\bar{q},\bar{f},\bar{a},\bar{r}}\big|{q,f,a,r}\big\rangle\\
={}&(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{d}(\bar{q}-q)\,\delta_{\bar{f},f}\delta_{\bar{a},a}\delta_{\bar{r},r}\;.\end{split}

(63)

A set of
m
partons is then described by a vector

\begin{split}\big|{\{w\}_{m}}\big\rangle={}&\big|{\{w_{1},w_{2},\cdots,w_{m}\}}\big\rangle\\
={}&\big|{w_{1}}\big\rangle_{1}\otimes\big|{w_{2}}\big\rangle_{2}\cdots\otimes\big|{w_{m}}\big\rangle_{m}\;.\end{split}

(64)

Here the vector
|{w}\rangle_{i}
in the
i
th position represents the quantum numbers
w
of parton
i
. For any number of partons, the inner product of basis states is

\begin{split}&\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\bar{w}\}_{\bar{m}}}\big|{\{w\}_{m}}\big\rangle\\
&\qquad=\delta_{\bar{m},m}\prod_{i=1}^{m}(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{d}(\bar{q}_{i}-q_{i})\,\delta_{\bar{f}_{i},f_{i}}\delta_{\bar{a}_{i},a_{i}}\delta_{\bar{r}_{i},r_{i}}\;.\end{split}

(65)

We abbreviate the integral needed for summing over basis states for
m
partons as

\begin{split}\int\!d\{w\}_{m}={}&\prod_{i=1}^{m}\int\!dw_{i}\;,\end{split}

(66)

where

\begin{split}\int\!dw_{i}={}&\sum_{f_{i}}\sum_{c_{i}}\sum_{r_{i}}\int\frac{d^{d}q_{i}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\;.\end{split}

(67)

Note that we integrate over off-shell momenta
q_{i}
in
d=4-2\epsilon
dimensions. We have a completeness relation

1=\sum_{m}\int\!d\{w\}_{m}\,\big|{\{w\}_{m}}\big\rangle\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{w\}_{m}}\big|\;.

(68)

The basis vectors
\big|{\{w\}_{m}}\big\rangle
are not symmetric. In some cases we have to consider permutations of the single parton quantum numbers. In order to flip two partons we define a generic permutation operators
\bm{\pi}(l,k)
by

\begin{split}\bm{\pi}(l,k){}&\big|{\{w_{1},\dots,w_{l},\dots,w_{k},\dots,w_{m}\}}\big\rangle\\
={}&\big|{\{w_{1},\dots,w_{k},\dots,w_{l},\dots,w_{m}\}}\big\rangle\\
={}&\big|{w_{1}}\big\rangle_{1}\cdots\otimes\big|{w_{k}}\big\rangle_{l}\cdots\otimes\big|{w_{l}}\big\rangle_{k}\cdots\otimes\big|{w_{m}}\big\rangle_{m}\;.\end{split}

(69)

Most often, we use two specific permutation operators, defined by

\begin{split}\bm{\Pi}_{l}\big|{\{w\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle={}&\bm{\pi}(l,m+1)\big|{\{w\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle\;,\\
\bm{\Pi}\big|{\{w\}_{m\!+\!2}}\big\rangle={}&\bm{\pi}(m+1,m+2)\big|{\{w\}_{{m\!+\!2}}}\big\rangle\;.\end{split}

(70)

The operator
\bm{\Pi}_{l}
exchanges parton
l
with the last one, and
\bm{\Pi}
exchanges the last two partons.

We will use this vector space to represent and generate Feynman graphs. More specifically we are interested in Feynman graphs when
m
hard lines are dressed with real and virtual radiation. We will define operators acting on the ket amplitude space with the aim that the usual Feynman graphs can be understood as matrix elements of these operators. The simplest operator is the unit operator. The simplest operator is the unit operator,

\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\bar{w}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{1}\big|{\{w\}_{m}}\big\rangle=\hbox to104.93pt{\vbox to72.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 25.22371pt\lower-63.8274pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
