for a gluon line. The dot in Eq. (
156
) indicates contraction of vector/spinor indices, as in Eq. (
158
) below. For
\psi(x)
, the dependence on spinor indices is trivial:
Z_{\psi}
and
\gamma_{\psi}
are proportional to unit matrices on the spinor indices. However, in interpolating gauge,
Z_{A}
and
\gamma_{A}
are non-trivial tensors in the vector indices.

We can consider
Z_{\psi}
,
Z_{A}
,
\gamma_{\psi}
, and
\gamma_{A}
to be operators on the vector/spinor part of the ket amplitude space. Then the anomalous dimensions are defined by

\begin{split}\mu_{\textsc{r}}\,\frac{dZ_{\psi}}{d\mu_{\textsc{r}}}={}&2\gamma_{\psi}\,Z_{\psi}\;,\\
\mu_{\textsc{r}}\,\frac{dZ_{A}^{\mu\nu}}{d\mu_{\textsc{r}}}={}&2\gamma_{A}\,Z_{A}\;,\end{split}

(157)

where
Z_{\psi}
and
Z_{A}
are the field strength renormalization constants in interpolating gauge, which are given at first order in Ref.
Gauge
.
13
13

13

In Eq. (158) of Ref.
Gauge
, the coupling renormalization relation was given as
g_{\mathrm{B}}=Z_{g}g
. With the more standard conventions of Ref.
JCCfoundations
, this relation is
g_{\mathrm{B}}=\mu^{\epsilon}Z_{g}g
.

Including the vector/spinor indices, this is

\begin{split}\mu_{\textsc{r}}\,\frac{d\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\bar{r}}\big|Z_{\psi}\big|{r}\big\rangle}{d\mu_{\textsc{r}}}={}&2\sum_{r^{\prime}}\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\bar{r}}\big|\gamma_{\psi}\big|{r^{\prime}}\big\rangle\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{r^{\prime}}\big|Z_{\psi}\big|{r}\big\rangle\;,\\
\mu_{\textsc{r}}\,\frac{d\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\bar{r}}\big|Z_{A}\big|{r}\big\rangle}{d\mu_{\textsc{r}}}={}&2\sum_{r^{\prime}}\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\bar{r}}\big|\gamma_{A}\big|{r^{\prime}}\big\rangle\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{r^{\prime}}\big|Z_{A}\big|{r}\big\rangle\;.\end{split}

(158)

The anomalous dimensions are
Gauge

\begin{split}\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\bar{r}}\big|\gamma_{\psi}\big|{r}\big\rangle={}&\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\,S_{\epsilon}\left\{\frac{(v-1)^{2}}{v(v+1)}+\frac{\xi}{v}\right\}C_{\mathrm{F}}\,\delta_{\bar{r}r}\\
&+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2})\;,\\
\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\bar{r}}\big|\gamma_{A}\big|{r}\big\rangle={}&-\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\,S_{\epsilon}\left\{c_{\mathrm{A}}g^{\bar{r}}_{r}+\tilde{c}_{\mathrm{A}}h^{\bar{r}}_{r}\right\}\\
&+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2})\;.\end{split}

(159)

In
\gamma_{A}
, the constants
c_{\mathrm{A}}
and
\tilde{c}_{\mathrm{A}}
are

\begin{split}c_{\mathrm{A}}={}&\left[\frac{22v^{3}+35v^{2}+20v-1}{6v(v+1)^{2}}-\frac{\xi}{2v}\right]C_{\mathrm{A}}\\
&-\frac{4}{3}\,T_{\mathrm{R}}n_{\mathrm{f}}\;,\\
\tilde{c}_{\mathrm{A}}={}&-\frac{4v(2v+1)}{3(v+1)^{2}}\,C_{\mathrm{A}}\;.\end{split}

(160)

Our Green functions represented in
{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\textsc{h}}
have
m
external quark, antiquark, and gluon legs and are amputated on those legs. However, if we were to leave out the interactions
{\bm{N}}_{l}^{(0,1)}
in Eq. (
142
), they would lack the
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
dependence of Eq. (
156
). We introduce the needed
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
dependence by hand by including interactions
{\bm{N}}_{l}^{(0,1)}
. We define these interactions by

\begin{split}\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\bar{w}\}_{m}}\big|{\bm{N}}_{l}^{(0,1)}\big|{\chi(\{p,f,c,s\}_{m})}\big\rangle\hskip-128.0374pt{}&\\
={}&\sum_{\{a,r\}_{m}}\,\big\langle{\{a\}_{m}}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle\prod_{i=1}^{m}\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{r_{i}}\big|{\chi_{f_{i}}(p_{i},s_{i})}\big\rangle\\
&\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq l\end{subarray}}^{m}\left[\delta_{\bar{f}_{i},f_{i}}\,\delta_{\bar{a}_{i},a_{i}}\,\delta_{\bar{r}_{i},r_{i}}\right]\\
&\times\delta_{\bar{f}_{l},f_{l}}\,\delta_{\bar{a}_{l},a_{l}}N_{l}(f_{l},\mu_{\textsc{r}};\bar{r}_{l},r_{l})\;.\end{split}

(161)

This operator has no momentum dependence and is proportional to the unit matrix in flavors, colors, and vector/spinor indices for spectator partons, those with indices
i\neq l
. For parton
l
,
{\bm{N}}_{l}^{(0,1)}
is proportional to the unit matrix in flavor and color. When parton
l
is a quark or antiquark,
{\bm{N}}_{l}^{(0,1)}
is also proportional to the unit matrix in the spinor indices. When parton
l
is a gluon,
{\bm{N}}_{l}^{(0,1)}
has a nontrivial dependence on the vector indices. We define the constants
N_{l}(f_{l},\mu_{\textsc{r}};\bar{r}_{l},r_{l})
by

N_{l}(f_{l},\mu_{\textsc{r}};\bar{r}_{l},r_{l})=\log(\mu_{\textsc{r}}/\mu_{l})\,\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}})}{2\pi}\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\bar{r}_{l}}\big|\gamma_{l}^{(1)}\big|{r_{l}}\big\rangle.

(162)

Here
[\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}/(2\pi)]\,\gamma_{l}^{(1)}
is the contribution to
\gamma_{l}
in Eq. (
159
) that is proportional to the first power of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
and
\mu_{l}
is a fixed scale parameter. One could choose, for instance,
\mu_{l}^{2}=\min_{k}(2p_{l}\cdot p_{k})
. This definition gives us the desired dependence on
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
:

\mu_{\textsc{r}}\,\frac{d}{d\mu_{\textsc{r}}}\,N_{l}(f_{l},\mu_{\textsc{r}},\bar{r}_{l},r_{l})=\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}})}{2\pi}\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\bar{r}_{l}}\big|\gamma_{l}^{(1)}\big|{r_{l}}\big\rangle\;.

(163)

VI.9
Conjugate Feynman amplitude

In the ket Feynman amplitude, we can start with a vector
\big|{\chi}\big\rangle
and apply operators
{\bm{\Gamma}}
to it. In the corresponding construction in the bra Feynman amplitude, we start with a vector
\langle{\chi}|
and apply operators
\bm{\Gamma}^{\dagger}
to it. Using the completeness relation (
68
), this is

\begin{split}\big\langle{\chi}\big|\bm{\Gamma}_{1}^{\dagger}{}&\bm{\Gamma}_{2}^{\dagger}\cdots\\
={}&\sum_{m,\bar{m}}\int\!d\{w^{\prime}\}_{m}\int\!d\{\bar{w}^{\prime}\}_{\bar{m}}\\
&\times\big\langle{\chi}\big|{\{w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{\Gamma}_{1}^{\dagger}\bm{\Gamma}_{2}^{\dagger}\cdots\big|{\{\bar{w}^{\prime}\}_{\bar{m}}}\big\rangle\\
&\times\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\bar{w}^{\prime}\}_{\bar{m}}}\big|\;.\end{split}

(164)

Thus we need
\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{\Gamma}^{\dagger}\big|{\{\bar{w}^{\prime}\}_{\bar{m}}}\big\rangle
. As an example, for
\bm{\Gamma}_{l}^{(1,0)\dagger}
in Eq. (
125
), we have

\begin{split}&\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\right]^{1/2}\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\bm{\Gamma}}^{(1,0)\dagger}_{l}\big|{\{\bar{w}^{\prime}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle\\
&\qquad=\prod_{i\neq l}\left[(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{d}(\bar{q}^{\prime}_{i}-q^{\prime}_{i})\,\delta_{\bar{f}^{\prime}_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}\delta_{\bar{a}^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}\delta_{\bar{r}^{\prime}_{i},r^{\prime}_{i}}\right]\\
&\qquad\quad\times(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{d}(\bar{q}^{\prime}_{l}+\bar{q}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}-q^{\prime}_{l})\,\\
&\qquad\quad\times\overline{\Gamma}^{(0)}\big(w^{\prime}_{l};\{\bar{w}^{\prime}_{l},\bar{w}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}\}\big)\;.\end{split}

(165)

The functions
\overline{\Gamma}^{(0)}(w^{\prime}_{l};\{\bar{w}^{\prime}_{l},\bar{w}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}\})
are quite simply related to the functions
\Gamma^{(0)}(w_{l};\{\bar{w}_{l},\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1}\})
used in the ket amplitude. The spinor factors are the Dirac adjoints of the corresponding factors in
\Gamma(w_{l};\{\bar{w}_{l},\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1}\})
. Two significant differences are the replacement of
1/(q_{l}^{2}+{\mathrm{i}}0)
in the mother parton propagator by
1/(q_{l}^{\prime 2}-{\mathrm{i}}0)
and the reversal of the order of
\gamma
matrices.

VII
Quantum evolution space

The operator
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
acts on a vector space that includes both bra and ket amplitudes, the statistical space described in Sec.
IV.1
. Now we need a similar vector space that combines the evolution of Feynman diagrams for the ket amplitude and for the conjugate bra amplitude.

VII.1
Vector space for the ket
\times
bra amplitudes

In order to work with the operators
\bm{G}(G_{\mathrm{ket}})
and
\bm{G}^{\dagger}(G_{\mathrm{bra}})
together, we use the tensor product of the vector space for the quantum ket amplitude and the vector space for the quantum bra amplitude. We call this the quantum evolution space. Typically, we need only basis vectors with the same number of partons in the amplitude and the conjugate amplitude. These are

\begin{split}\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)={}&\big|{\{w\}_{m}}\big\rangle\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\;.\end{split}

(166)

Letting an operator
\bm{G}(G_{\mathrm{ket}})
act on the ket vectors and an operator
\bm{G}^{\dagger}(G_{\mathrm{bra}})
act on the bra vectors gives a product operator
\bm{G}(G_{\mathrm{ket}})\otimes{\bm{G}}^{\dagger}(G_{\mathrm{bra}})
defined by

\begin{split}\big[\bm{G}(G_{\mathrm{ket}})\otimes{}&\bm{G}^{\dagger}(G_{\mathrm{bra}})\big]\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
={}&\bm{G}(G_{\mathrm{ket}})\big|{\{w\}_{m}}\big\rangle\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{G}^{\dagger}(G_{\mathrm{bra}})\;.\end{split}

(167)

It is useful to introduce names for operators on this space. As a convention, we name operators in the quantum evolution space with hatted boldface letters. For instance, we define elementary operators
{\hat{\bm{C}}}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)
,
{\hat{\bm{X}}}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)
,
{\hat{\bm{X}}}_{lk}^{(0,1)}
and
{\hat{\bm{E}}}_{lk}^{(0,1)}
in this section.

We have already defined many elementary operators in the quantum space of Feynman amplitudes, such as
\bm{\Gamma}^{(1,0)}_{l}
. With them we can immediately define related elementary operators in the quantum evolution space.

VII.2
Hardness measure

We will define vertex operators in the vector space of Feynman amplitudes to build Feynman graphs. We would like to associate to every vertex with real radiation a scale that characterizes the hardness of the splitting. This scale should measure the distance from the infrared singular surfaces.

To do this we define a hardness measure
h(q)
that tests the hardness of a line carrying momentum
q
in the graph. This function has to be infrared sensitive,

h(q)\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad q^{2}\to 0\;.

(168)

The simplest hardness function would be simply the virtuality,

h(q)=q^{2}\;.

(169)

Another sensible choice is the virtuality based choice made in
Deductor
:

\begin{split}h(q)={}&\frac{q^{2}}{2q\cdot Q}\,Q^{2}\;.\end{split}

(170)

This function is denoted as
\Lambda^{\!2}(q_{l})
and explained in Ref.
ShowerTime
.

Both functional forms for
h(q)
are monotonic in a sequence of real parton emissions. Consider a first order splitting of parton
l
with momentum
q_{l}
followed by subsequent splittings of the daughter partons, which then have momenta
\bar{q}_{i}
and
\bar{q}_{j}
. Then

q_{l}=\bar{q}_{i}+\bar{q}_{j}\;.

(171)

Assuming that there are only real parton emissions, we have

\begin{split}\bar{q}_{i}^{2}\geq{}&0\;,\qquad\bar{q}_{i}\cdot Q>0\;,\\
\bar{q}_{j}^{2}\geq{}&0\;,\qquad\bar{q}_{j}\cdot Q>0\;.\end{split}

(172)

Under these conditions, the function
h(q)
defined by Eq. (
170
) obeys

\begin{split}h(\bar{q}_{i}+\bar{q}_{j})\geq{}&h(\bar{q}_{i})\;,\\
h(\bar{q}_{i}+\bar{q}_{j})\geq{}&h(\bar{q}_{j})\;.\end{split}

(173)

We present the proof of this in Appendix
D
. These relations hold trivially if
h(q)=q^{2}
.

Hardness measures based on transverse momentum are often used in parton shower algorithms. We do not consider these measures in this paper.

We measure the hardness of a splitting using the momenta of a set
A\subset\{1,\dots,m\}
of partons after the splitting in the ket amplitude and a set
B\subset\{1,\dots,m\}
of partons after the splitting in the bra amplitude. We define an operator
{\hat{\bm{h}}}(A,B;\mu)
on the quantum evolution space by

\begin{split}{\hat{\bm{h}}}(A,&B;\mu)\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
={}&2\mu\,\delta\bigg(\mu^{2}-\max\!\bigg[h\bigg(\sum_{i\in A}{q}_{i}\bigg),h\bigg(\sum_{j\in B}{q}^{\prime}_{j}\bigg)\bigg]\bigg)\\
&\times\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(174)

In most cases, we use two specializations of this operator. For single collinear emission in which parton
l
splits into partons
l
and
m+1
in both the ket and bra amplitudes, we use

\begin{split}{\hat{\bm{h}}}_{l}(\mu)&\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m+1}}\big)\\
={}&{\hat{\bm{h}}}\big(\{l,m+1\},\{l,m+1\};\mu\big)\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m+1}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(175)

For the single soft emission in which parton
l
splits into partons
l
and
m+1
in the ket amplitude and parton
k
splits into partons
k
and
m+1
in the bra amplitude, we use

\begin{split}{\hat{\bm{h}}}_{lk}(\mu)&\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m+1}}\big)\\
={}&{\hat{\bm{h}}}\big(\{l,m+1\},\{k,m+1\};\mu\big)\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m+1}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(176)

VII.3
Operators on the quantum evolution space

We have seen how to express Feynman diagrams using operators on the ket amplitude space and operators on the bra amplitude space. Now we need to put these together.

For operators describing real emissions, we also introduce a new scale
\mu
with the aid of the operator
\hat{\bm{h}}(A,B;\mu)
, which measures the hardness of the real emissions. In a later section, Sec.
X
, we will define the shower algorithm via a jet observable in which the scale
\mu
is the jet resolution scale.

For operators that describe virtual graphs, we do not define a new scale since the jet observable that we will use in the shower algorithm is sensitive only to real parton emissions, not virtual diagrams.

Tree level irreducible operators

We define an operator
{\hat{\bm{C}}}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)
that describes the splitting of parton
l
out of
m
partons to partons
l
and
m\!+\!1
in both the ket amplitude and the bra amplitude:

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\mu}\,{\hat{\bm{C}}}^{(1,0)}_{l}&(\mu)\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
={}&{\hat{\bm{h}}}_{l}(\mu)\,\frac{1}{2}\left[\bm{\Gamma}^{(1,0)}_{l}\otimes\bm{\Gamma}^{(1,0)\dagger}_{l}\right]\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(177)

We include a factor
1/2
to account for the symmetry between the two emitted partons and a factor
1/\mu
so that the natural integration measure for
{\hat{\bm{C}}}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)
is
d\mu/\mu
. The scale operator
{\hat{\bm{h}}}_{l}(\mu)
provides a delta function that restricts the allowed final states according to the given value of
\mu
.

Similarly we can represent simple interference diagrams that appear when parton
m+1
is emitted by parton
l
in the ket amplitude and by parton
k
in the bra amplitude:

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\mu}\,{\hat{\bm{X}}}^{(1,0)}_{lk}&(\mu)\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
={}&{\hat{\bm{h}}}_{lk}(\mu)\,\left[\bm{\Gamma}^{(1,0)}_{l}\otimes\bm{\Gamma}^{(1,0)\dagger}_{k}\right]\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(178)

One loop irreducible operators

We can use the exchange operators
\bm{X}_{lk}^{(0,1)}
and
\bm{E}_{lk}^{(0,1)}
defined in Eqs. (
144
) and (
145
) to define operators that describes an exchange between two partons,
l
and
k
:

\begin{split}{\hat{\bm{X}}}_{lk}^{(0,1)}={}&\bm{X}_{lk}^{(0,1)}\otimes\bm{1}+\bm{1}\otimes\bm{X}_{lk}^{(0,1)\dagger}\;,\\
\hat{\bm{E}}_{lk}^{(0,1)}={}&\bm{E}_{lk}^{(0,1)}\otimes\bm{1}+\bm{1}\otimes\bm{E}_{lk}^{(0,1)\dagger}\;.\end{split}

(179)

The exchange graphs appear either on the ket or the bra side.

Here we do not introduce a scale
\mu
based on the momenta of emitted partons because no partons are emitted. However, it is important that the operators depend on
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
. When the exchanged parton is a gluon, the operator has a soft singularity that arises from the integration over the momentum of the exchanged gluon, leading to a contribution proportional to
\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2\epsilon}/\epsilon
.

VIII
Extended statistical space

With the quantum evolution space, we can describe parton splittings and virtual graphs starting from
m
partons with momenta
q_{i}
in the ket amplitude and
q^{\prime}_{i}
in the bra amplitude. The parton momenta in these amplitudes are generally off shell. We now need to extend this to a larger space that can describe the final state after these splittings in which the parton momenta are on shell.

VIII.1
Jet momentum space

We need one more factor in the space that describes Feynman diagrams with parton emissions starting from
m
partons. We need this extra factor in order to describe the momentum mapping that gives on-shell momenta for the partons associated with a graph
G=(G_{\mathrm{ket}},G_{\mathrm{bra}})
.

As described in Sec.
III
, in the graph
G
, we can think of the on-shell final state partons as subjets of larger jets. Let there be
n_{\textsc{j}}
initial jets with momenta
\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}=\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n_{\textsc{j}}}\}
. The initial jets are idealized as having
p_{j}^{2}=0
. Let there be
\hat{n}_{\textsc{j}}
final state subjets with momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{n}_{\textsc{j}}}
with
\hat{p}_{i}^{2}=0
. For each initial jet
j
, a subset
J(j)
of the final state subjets are part of jet
j
. Each final state subjet is a part of one of the initial jets. There is an infrared singularity in
G
when the momenta
\hat{p}_{i}
for
i\in J(j)
are collinear with
p_{j}
or are soft and the momenta
\ell_{i}
of any exchanged gluons vanish.

When the
\hat{p}_{i}
for
i\in J(j)
are not zero or exactly collinear with momentum
p_{j}
, then we need a momentum mapping from the
\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}
to the
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{n}_{\textsc{j}}}
, as described in Sec.
IV.3
. We need to include information about the
\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}
in the vector space. Then we can include the operators that describe the momentum mapping along with the operators that describe the graph
G
according to the Feynman rules.

As in Eq. (
34
), we relate the final jet momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{n}_{\textsc{j}}}
to the initial jet momenta
\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}
and splitting parameters
\zeta_{G}
by a momentum mapping function

\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{n}_{\textsc{j}}}=R(G;\zeta_{G},\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}})

(180)

that obeys

\sum_{i=1}^{\hat{n}_{\textsc{j}}}\hat{p}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{j}}}p_{i}\;.

(181)

The momentum mapping also includes a Jacobian factor in the integration measure
d\zeta_{G}
for integration over the splitting parameters so that

d\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{n}_{\textsc{j}}}=d\zeta_{G}\ d\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}\;.

(182)

The momentum mapping becomes trivial as the momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{n}_{\mathrm{j}}}
approach the infrared singularities of the graph
G
. Beyond that, there is some freedom in choosing the function
R
. The functions
R
used in this paper are defined in Appendices
A
and
B
. Often the momentum mapping for emitting two partons will be constructed as a product of mappings from
m
partons to
m+1
partons and then to
m+2
partons.

In order to incorporate the momentum mapping function, we define a vector space, the jet momentum space, with basis vectors
\big|{\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}}\big)
that specify the momenta of an arbitrary number
n_{\textsc{j}}
of jets. The completeness relation for these basis vectors is

1=\sum_{{n_{\textsc{j}}}=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{{n_{\textsc{j}}}!}\int\!d\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}\ \big|{\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}}\big)\big({\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}}\big|\;,

(183)

using the integration measure
d\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}
from Eq. (
22
).

We specify the jet momentum mapping with the use of an operator
\bm{R}(G)
that acts on the space of jet momenta spanned by the basis vectors
\big|{\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}}\big)
. This operator is defined using the function
R(G;\zeta_{G},\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}})
by

\bm{R}(G)\big|{\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}}\big)=\int\!d\zeta_{G}\ \big|{\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{n}_{\textsc{j}}}}\big)\;,

(184)

where
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{n}_{\textsc{j}}}
is given in Eq. (
180
) and
\int\!d\zeta_{G}
indicates integration over the splitting parameters, with the property (
182
). This operator is explained in more detail in Appendices
A
,
B
, and
C
.

VIII.2
Extended statistical space

We supplement the vectors
\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)
in the quantum evolution space with the jet basis vectors
\big|{\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}}\big)
. This gives us a direct product space, the extended statistical space, with basis vectors

\begin{split}\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m};\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}}\big)={}&\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\otimes\big|{\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(185)

We define bra vectors in the extended statistical space so that the completeness relation is

\begin{split}1={}&\sum_{m,n_{\textsc{j}}}\frac{1}{n_{\textsc{j}}!}\!\int\!d\{w\}_{m}\int\!d\{w^{\prime}\}_{m}\!\int\!d\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}\\
&\times\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m};\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}}\big)\big({\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m};\{p\}_{n_{\textsc{j}}}}\big|\;.\end{split}

(186)

In our analysis, we mostly use terms with
n_{\textsc{j}}=m
. The integration measures are given by Eqs. (
66
) and (
22
).

VIII.3
Spectator parton evolution

We have defined operators on the quantum evolution space that generate the elementary parts of Feynman graphs. We now need to extend these operators to the extended statistical space by associating momentum mapping operators with them. We use calligraphic letters with hats, for example
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
, to name these operators.

The extended statistical space includes a factor to describe the jet momenta
\{p\}_{m}
. With this factor, each splitting operator acting on the quantum evolution space can be accompanied by a momentum mapping operator acting on the jet momentum space. For example, to extend the interference emission operator
{\hat{\bm{X}}}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)
, Eq. (
178
), to the extended statistical space, we add the single emission soft momentum mapping operator
\bm{R}_{\rm soft}^{(1)}
, Eq. (
392
). The simplest definition would be

\hat{\mathcal{X}}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)_{\mathrm{simple}}={\hat{\bm{X}}}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)\otimes\bm{R}_{\rm soft}^{(1)}\;.

(187)

With the inclusion of a momentum mapping operator like
\bm{R}_{\rm soft}^{(1)}
, at each step of Feynman graph evolution with one or more partons emitted, there is a corresponding step of momentum mapping. The jet momenta
\{p\}_{m}
are mapped to new momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}
.

With this formulation, we start with a quantum ket amplitude
\big|{\chi(\{p,f,c,s\}_{m})}\big\rangle
times a quantum bra amplitude
\big\langle{\chi(\{p,f,c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m})}\big|
, as given in Eq. (
83
) and (
84
). We start with an initial vector
\big|{\{p\}_{m}}\big)
in the jet momentum space. This gives a vector in the extended statistical space. We then have evolution in the extended statistical space according to a graph
G
. In the jet momentum space, this gives a new state
\big|{\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big)
. We then multiply the evolved quantum ket amplitude by a final state amplitude
\langle{\psi(\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{s}\}_{\hat{m}})}|
, Eq. (
87
). We also multiply the evolved quantum bra amplitude by a final state amplitude
|{\psi(\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}})}\rangle
, Eq. (
89
). This procedure will be defined precisely in Sec.
VIII.6
below.

Many of the partons in the ket amplitude are spectator partons, for which there is no interaction. If we were to use just Eq. (
187
), we would have for each spectator parton
i
in the ket amplitude a factor

\big\langle{\psi_{f_{i}}(\hat{p}_{i},\hat{s}_{i})}\big|{\chi_{f_{i}}(p_{i},s_{i})}\big\rangle\;.

(188)

There would be a similar factor for each spectator parton in the bra amplitude. If
\hat{p}_{i}
were equal to
p_{i}
, the factor in Eq. (
188
) would be just
\delta_{\hat{s}_{i},s_{i}}
(using the identity (
205
)). However, the global momentum mappings used in this paper modify the momenta of spectator partons. This implies that if we were to use just Eq. (
187
), the momentum mapping would have the side effect of modifying the spins of spectator partons.

In order to keep the spin of a spectator parton from changing when its momentum changes from
p_{i}
to
\hat{p}_{i}
, we apply an operator
\sum_{s}|{\chi_{f_{i}}(\hat{p}_{i},s)}\rangle\,\langle{\psi_{f}(p_{i},s)}|
acting on the ket amplitude space. Then

\begin{split}\sum_{s}\big|{\chi_{f_{i}}(\hat{p}_{i},s)}\big\rangle{}&\big\langle{\psi_{f_{i}}(p_{i},s)}\big|{\chi_{f_{i}}(p_{i},s_{i})}\big\rangle\\
={}&\big|{\chi_{f_{i}}(\hat{p}_{i},s_{i})}\big\rangle\;.\end{split}

(189)

With this change, the factor in Eq. (
188
) for each spectator parton becomes
\langle{\psi_{f_{i}}(\hat{p}_{i},\hat{s}_{i})}|{\chi_{f_{i}}(\hat{p}_{i},s_{i})}\rangle=\delta_{\hat{s}_{i},s_{i}}
. We also use the corresponding operator acting on the bra amplitude space.

We update the vector/spinor factors for all of the spectator partons
both in the bra and ket amplitudes by transforming these factors using an operator
{\hat{\bm{T}}}
in the quantum evolution space that has the effect of supplying the corresponding transformation for the spectator partons.
Let
A
and
B
be two sets of indices
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. The sets
A

and
B
are the sets of indices of partons that we will not consider to be spectator partons in the ket and bra state respectively. The definition of
{\hat{\bm{T}}}
for a momentum mapping
\{p\}_{m}\to\{\hat{p}\}_{m+n_{\textsc{r}}}
with
n_{\textsc{r}}
real emissions is

\displaystyle\big({\{\hat{w},\hat{w}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\hat{\bm{T}}}(A,B;\{\hat{p}\}_{m+n_{\textsc{r}}},\{p\}_{m})\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big){}

(190)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{m}\Big\{\delta_{\hat{f}_{i},f_{i}}\delta_{\hat{f}^{\prime}_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}\delta_{\hat{a}_{i},a_{i}}\delta_{\hat{a}^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}

\displaystyle\times(2\pi)^{d}\delta(\hat{q}_{i}-q_{i})\,(2\pi)^{d}\delta(\hat{q}^{\prime}_{i}-q^{\prime}_{i})\Big\}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i\in A}\delta_{\hat{r}_{i},r_{i}}\prod_{i\in B}\delta_{\hat{r}^{\prime}_{i},r^{\prime}_{i}}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i\notin A}\bigg\{\sum_{s}\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\hat{r}_{i}}\big|{\chi_{f_{i}}(\hat{p}_{i},s)}\big\rangle\,\big\langle{\psi_{f_{i}}(p_{i},s)}\big|{r_{i}}\big\rangle\bigg\}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i\notin B}\bigg\{\sum_{s}\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{r^{\prime}_{i}}\big|{\psi_{f_{i}}(p_{i},s)}\big\rangle\,\big\langle{\chi_{f_{i}}(\hat{p}_{i},s)}\big|{\hat{r}^{\prime}_{i}}\big\rangle\bigg\}\;.

Since the operator
{\hat{\bm{T}}}
acting on the quantum evolution space is closely associated with the momentum mapping, it is useful to combine it with the momentum mapping operator to define a momentum mapping operator
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
in the extended statistical space.

At second order level, we often need two kinds of mappings
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
on the extended statistical space, each with
n_{\textsc{r}}=
1 or 2. The first is a collinear mapping
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathrm{coll}}^{(n_{\textsc{r}})}(l)
that accompanies emission of one or two partons from parton
l
in the ket amplitude and in the bra amplitude. The second is a soft mapping
\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{(n_{\textsc{r}})}_{\mathrm{soft}}
that accompanies the emission of one or two partons from parton
l
in the ket amplitude and from parton
k
in the bra amplitude.

For the collinear mappings we define
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathrm{coll}}^{(n_{\textsc{r}})}(l)
by its non-zero matrix elements as

\displaystyle\big({\{\hat{w},\hat{w}^{\prime}\}_{m},\{\hat{p}\}_{m+n_{\textsc{r}}}}\big|\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathrm{coll}}^{(n_{\textsc{r}})}(l)\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m},\{p\}_{m}}\big){}

(191)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\big({\{\hat{w},\hat{w}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\hat{\bm{T}}}\big(\{l\},\{l\};\{\hat{p}\}_{m+n_{\textsc{r}}},\{p\}_{m}\big)\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)

\displaystyle\times\big({\{\hat{p}\}_{m+n_{\textsc{r}}}}\big|\bm{R}_{\mathrm{coll}}^{(n_{\textsc{r}})}(l)\big|{\{p\}_{m}}\big)\;.

For the soft mapping we define
\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{(n_{\textsc{r}})}_{\mathrm{soft}}
as

\displaystyle\big({\{\hat{w},\hat{w}^{\prime}\}_{m},\{\hat{p}\}_{m+n_{\textsc{r}}}}\big|\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{(n_{\textsc{r}})}_{\mathrm{soft}}\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m},\{p\}_{m}}\big){}

(192)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\big({\{\hat{w},\hat{w}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\hat{\bm{T}}}\big(\{\},\{\};\{\hat{p}\}_{m+n_{\textsc{r}}},\{p\}_{m}\big)\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)

\displaystyle\times\big({\{\hat{p}\}_{m+n_{\textsc{r}}}}\big|\bm{R}_{\mathrm{soft}}^{(n_{\textsc{r}})}\big|{\{p\}_{m}}\big)\;.

Here
\{\}
denotes the empty set. With this choice, the vector/spinor correction is applied to all of the
m
partons. Note that in the jet momentum space the number of jets increases by
n_{\textsc{r}}
, while in the quantum evolution space the number of the partons remains the same. This momentum mapping operator will always be accompanied by a splitting operator in which the number of partons in the quantum evolution space increases by
n_{\textsc{r}}
while the number of jets remains the same. Then the net effect is to increase both the number of partons and the number of jets by
n_{\textsc{r}}
.

VIII.4
Operators for generating graphs

We are now prepared to extend operators on the quantum evolution space that generate the elementary parts of Feynman graphs to the extended statistical space. We discuss here only first order irreducible operators. At second and higher perturbative orders, there are new operators that cannot be reduced to products of the first order operators ordered in their scales from harder to softer. We anticipate analyzing the second order operators in future work.

Tree level operators

To extend the collinear emission operator
\hat{\bm{C}}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)
, Eq. (
177
), we define

\hat{\mathcal{C}}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)=\big[{\hat{\bm{C}}}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)\otimes\bm{1}_{\mathrm{p}}\big]\,\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathrm{coll}}^{(1)}(l)\;.

(193)

We start with the collinear momentum mapping operator
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathrm{coll}}^{(1)}(l)
, Eq. (
191
), with a single emission, including adjustment to the vector/spinor amplitudes of the spectator partons. Next we have the actual collinear splitting operator trivially extended into the extended statistical space, using a unit operator
\bm{1}_{\mathrm{p}}
on the jet momentum space.

The operator
{\hat{\bm{X}}}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)
, Eq. (
178
), describes emission of a parton from parton
l
in the ket amplitude and from parton
k
in the bra amplitude. To extend this to the extended statistical space, we define

\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)=\big[{\hat{\bm{X}}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)\otimes\bm{1}_{\mathrm{p}}\big]\,\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathrm{soft}}^{(1)}\;.

(194)

This starts with the soft momentum mapping operator
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathrm{soft}}^{(1)}
, Eq. (
192
), with a single emission, including adjustment to the vector/spinor amplitudes of the spectator partons. This is followed by the operator
{\hat{\bm{X}}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
, trivially extended to the extended statistical space.

One loop operators

The extension of the one loop operators
{\hat{\bm{X}}}^{(0,1)}_{lk}
and
\hat{\bm{E}}^{(0,1)}_{lk}
in Eq. (
179
) to the extended statistical space is very simple since the needed momentum mapping operator is simply the unit operator. We define

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{X}}^{(0,1)}_{lk}={}&{\hat{\bm{X}}}^{(0,1)}_{lk}\otimes\bm{1}_{\mathrm{p}}\;,\\
\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{(0,1)}_{lk}={}&\hat{\bm{E}}^{(0,1)}_{lk}\otimes\bm{1}_{\mathrm{p}}\;.\end{split}

(195)

LSZ operators

The LSZ operators
\bm{L}_{l}
act on the space for the Feynman ket amplitudes. LSZ operators
\bm{L}_{k}^{\dagger}
acting on the space for the Feynman bra amplitudes are defined in the same way. Then we can define operators
\hat{\bm{L}}
that act on the complete quantum evolution space and include all of the final state parton lines by

\hat{\bm{L}}=\prod_{l,k}\bm{L}_{l}\otimes\bm{L}_{k}^{\dagger}\;.

(196)

This operator has a trivial extension to the extended statistical space,

\hat{\mathcal{L}}=\hat{\bm{L}}\otimes\bm{1}_{\mathrm{p}}\;,

(197)

where we have only the trivial unit transformation in the jet momentum space.

At first order, this is

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(1)}={}&\sum_{l}\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{l}^{(1)}\;,\end{split}

(198)

with

\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{l}^{(1)}=[\bm{L}^{(1)}_{l}\otimes\bm{1}]\otimes\bm{1}_{\mathrm{p}}+[\bm{1}\otimes\bm{L}^{(1)\dagger}_{l}]\otimes\bm{1}_{\mathrm{p}}\;.

(199)

Combined operators

A complete graph
G=(G_{\mathrm{ket}},G_{\mathrm{bra}})
is generated by a corresponding operator
\hat{\bm{G}}(G)
that acts on the quantum evolution space, as in Eq. (
167
). If we combine this with the associated momentum mapping, we call the associated operator on the extended statistical space
\hat{\mathcal{G}}(G)
. We can generate complete graphs using operators
\hat{\mathcal{G}}(G)
that are constructed as products of elementary operators such as
\hat{\mathcal{C}}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)
,
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
,
\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{(0,1)}_{lk}
, and
\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{l}^{(1)}
given above.

VIII.5
Mapping to the extended statistical space

We next need to define how to transform from the statistical space, with its on-shell partons, to the extended statistical space. We map a basis vector
\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)
in the statistical space to a vector
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)
in the extended statistical space. The subscript H on the operator
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}
reminds us that this is the mapping to the extended statistical space of the partons from the hard subgraph. The mapping is defined by

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\hskip-71.13188pt&\\
={}&\int\!d\{q,a,r\}_{m}\int\!d\{q^{\prime},a^{\prime},r^{\prime}\}_{m}\\
&\times\big|{\{q,f,a,r,q^{\prime},f,a^{\prime},r^{\prime}\}_{m};\{p\}_{m}}\big)\\
&\times\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{q,f,a,r\}_{m}}\big|{\chi(\{p,f,c,s\}_{m})}\big\rangle\\
&\times\big\langle{\chi(\{p,f,c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m})}\big|{\{q^{\prime},f,a^{\prime},r^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\;.\end{split}

(200)

Here
|{\chi(\{p,f,c,s\}_{m})}\rangle
is the initial state vector for the Feynman amplitude, Eq. (
83
), and
\langle{\chi(\{p,f,c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m})}|
is the initial state vector for the conjugate Feynman amplitude, Eq. (
84
).

This definition requires some explanation. The number of partons in the extended statistical space and the list of momenta
\{p\}_{m}
have been set to
m
and
\{p\}_{m}
given by the statistical space basis vector. The flavors
\{f\}_{m}
and
\{f^{\prime}\}_{m}
for the incoming parton lines have been set to the flavors
\{f\}_{m}
in the statistical basis vector.

There are integrations over the momenta
\{q\}_{m}
and
\{q^{\prime}\}_{m}
. These momenta will be determined by momentum conservation as linear combinations of the momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}
at the end of the evolution in the extended statistical space. Then, close to an infrared singularity when the
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}
are close to forming
m
massless jets and momenta
\ell
exchanged by virtual partons are close to zero, the
\{q\}_{m}
will be close to the original lightlike, positive energy, momenta
\{p\}_{m}
.

There are sums over the color indices
\{a\}_{m}
and
\{a^{\prime}\}_{m}
and vector/spinor indices
\{r\}_{m}
and
\{r^{\prime}\}_{m}
. The dependence on these indices is given by the inner products of the vectors
|{\chi(\{p,f,c,s\}_{m})}\rangle
and
\langle{\chi(\{p,f,c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m})}|
with the basis states
{}_{\textsc{d}}\!\langle{\{q,f,a,r\}_{m}}|
and
|{\{q^{\prime},f,a^{\prime},r^{\prime}\}_{m}}\rangle
.

VIII.6
Mapping to the statistical space

Starting from the vector
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}})
with
m
partons in the extended statistical space, evolution according to graphs
G
creates a vector
|{\Phi})
with typically more partons, which have new quantum numbers. The new partons are emitted according to the Feynman graphs and the jet momenta
\{p\}_{m}
are updated to
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}
according to the associated momentum mappings.

The evolved state can be expanded in basis vectors using the completeness relation (
186
),

\begin{split}\big|{\Phi}\big)={}&\frac{1}{m!}\,\int\!d\{w\}_{m}\int\!d\{w^{\prime}\}_{m}\int\!d\{p\}_{m}\\
&\times\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m};\{p\}_{m}}\big)\\
&\times g_{\Phi}(\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m};\{p\}_{m})\;,\end{split}

(201)

where the function
g_{\Phi}
is

g_{\Phi}=\big({\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m};\{p\}_{m}}\big|{\Phi}\big)\;.

(202)

We need to define how to transform this vector in the extended statistical space, with its off-shell partons, to the statistical space with on-shell partons. We map a basis vector
|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m};\{p\}_{m}})
in the extended statistical space to a vector
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}|{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m};\{p\}_{m}})
in the statistical space. The subscript S on the operator
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}
, with S for
soft
, reminds us that subsets of the
m
partons are close to infrared singularities. The definition is

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\big|{\{q,f,a,r,q^{\prime},f^{\prime},a^{\prime},r^{\prime}\}_{m};\{p\}_{m}}\big)\hskip-113.81102pt&\\
={}&\sum_{\{\tilde{f},c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{p,\tilde{f},c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
&\times\big\langle{\psi(\{p,\tilde{f},c,s\}_{m})}\big|{\{q,f,a,r\}_{m}}\big\rangle\\
&\times\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{q^{\prime},f^{\prime},a^{\prime},r^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\psi(\{p,\tilde{f},c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m})}\big\rangle\;.\end{split}

(203)

Here
\langle{\psi(\{p,\tilde{f},c,s\}_{m})}|
is the final state vector for the Feynman amplitude, Eq. (
87
), and
\big|{\psi(\{p,\tilde{f},c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m})}\big\rangle
is the final state vector for the conjugate Feynman amplitude, Eq. (
89
).

In Eq. (
203
), the number of partons in the statistical space is the same as in the extended statistical space. The momenta in the statistical space vector are
\{p\}_{m}
as specified in the jet momentum part of the extended statistical space basis vector. The momenta
p_{i}
are determined by the splitting variables and by momentum mapping used for the emissions.

There are sums over the color states
\{c\}_{m}
and
\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}
and spins
\{s\}_{m}
and
\{s^{\prime}\}_{m}
. The dependence on these is given by the inner products of the vectors
\langle{\psi(\{p,\tilde{f},c,s\}_{m})}|
and
|{\psi(\{p,\tilde{f},c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m})}\rangle
with the basis states
|{\{q,f,a,r\}_{m}}\rangle
and
{}_{\textsc{d}}\!\langle{\{q^{\prime},f^{\prime},a^{\prime},r^{\prime}\}_{m}}|
.

These inner products in Eq. (
203
) contain delta functions that set
q_{i}
to
p_{i}
and
q^{\prime}_{i}
to
p_{i}
. For each splitting in the evolution, there were integrations over the momenta of the daughter partons, restricted by momentum conservation. With the delta functions that set
q_{i}=q^{\prime}_{i}=p_{i}
, all of the other momenta in the Feynman graphs are fixed.

The flavor of parton
i
in the statistical space is
\tilde{f}_{i}
. The inner products in Eq. (
203
) contain delta functions that set
f_{i}
to
\tilde{f}_{i}
and
f^{\prime}_{i}
to
\tilde{f}_{i}
. Thus
f_{i}
must be the same as
f^{\prime}_{i}

We note that
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}
is the identity operator on the statistical space:

\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}=1\;.

(204)

This follows with the use of the identities

\begin{split}\sum_{r_{i}}\big\langle{\psi_{f_{i}}(p_{i},\tilde{s}_{i})}\big|{r_{i}}\big\rangle\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{r_{i}}\big|{\chi_{f_{i}}(p_{i},s_{i})}\big\rangle={}&\delta_{\tilde{s}_{i}s_{i}}\;,\\
\sum_{r^{\prime}_{i}}\big\langle{\chi_{f_{i}}(p_{i},s^{\prime}_{i})}\big|{r^{\prime}_{i}}\big\rangle\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{r^{\prime}_{i}}\big|{\psi_{f_{i}}(p_{i},\tilde{s}^{\prime}_{i})}\big\rangle={}&\delta_{\tilde{s}^{\prime}_{i}s^{\prime}_{i}}\;.\end{split}

(205)

To verify this for the ket amplitude, we write it out for the three possible flavor choices:

\begin{split}-\varepsilon(p_{i},s)^{*}\cdot\varepsilon(p_{i},\tilde{s})={}&\delta_{\tilde{s}s}\;,\\
\overline{U}(p_{i},\tilde{s})\frac{{\raise-0.34723pt\hbox to6.00235pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$n$}}}{2p_{i}\cdot n}U(p_{i},s)={}&\delta_{\tilde{s}s}\;,\\
\overline{V}(p_{i},s)\frac{{\raise-0.34723pt\hbox to6.00235pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$n$}}}{2p_{i}\cdot n}V(p_{i},\tilde{s})={}&\delta_{\tilde{s}s}\;.\end{split}

(206)

For the bra amplitude, the proof is analogous.

The operator
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}
is
not
the identity operator on the extended statistical space. It is, however, a projection operator:

(\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}\,\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}})\,(\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}\,\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}})=\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}\,\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\;.

(207)

VIII.7
Infrared limit of interference

In Eq. (
194
), we have defined an operator
\hat{\mathcal{X}}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)
on the extended statistical space. This operator represents the interference between the emission of parton
m+1
from parton
l
in the ket amplitude and the emission of parton
m+1
from parton
k
in the bra amplitude. The operator
\hat{\mathcal{X}}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)
creates a logarithmic infrared singularity in the soft limit
\bar{q}_{m\!+\!1}\to 0
in
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\hat{\mathcal{X}}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}
, in which the final state momenta
\bar{q}_{l}
,
\bar{q}_{k}
, and
\bar{q}_{m\!+\!1}
are on shell. However, there is a simplification available because not all of the contributions to
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}
produce this logarithmic infrared singularity. We will define the infrared singular part of
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
, which we call
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}
. Then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1-{\mathsf{IR}}}}=\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}

(208)

does
not
produce a logarithmic infrared singularity. The operator
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}
will occur frequently, so we give it a name:

\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}\;.

(209)

To define
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}
, we first approximate Eq. (
194
) by approximating
{\hat{\bm{X}}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}=\big[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{{\hat{\bm{X}}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}\otimes\bm{1}_{\mathrm{p}}\big]\,\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathrm{soft}}^{(1)}\;.

(210)

We define
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{{\hat{\bm{X}}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}
using an approximated version of Eq. (
178
):

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\mu}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{{\hat{\bm{X}}}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\hskip-99.58464pt{}&\\
={}&{\hat{\bm{h}}}_{lk}(\mu)\,\left[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\bm{\Gamma}^{(1,0)}_{l}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}\otimes{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\bm{\Gamma}^{(1,0)\dagger}_{k}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}}\right]\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(211)

The operator
\bm{\Gamma}^{(1,0)}_{l}
that acts on the ket state has the form given in Eq. (
128
):

\begin{split}&\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\right]^{1/2}\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\bar{w}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|{\bm{\Gamma}}^{(1,0)}_{l}\big|{\{w\}_{m}}\big\rangle\\
&\qquad=\prod_{i\neq l}\left[(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{d}(\bar{q}_{i}-q_{i})\,\delta_{\bar{f}_{i},f_{i}}\delta_{\bar{a}_{i},a_{i}}\delta_{\bar{r}_{i},r_{i}}\right]\\
&\qquad\quad\times(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{d}(\bar{q}_{l}+\bar{q}_{m\!+\!1}-q_{l})\,\\
&\qquad\quad\times\big[V_{3}(w_{l};\bar{w}_{l},\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1})+V_{3}(w_{l};\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1},\bar{w}_{l})\big]\;.\end{split}

(212)

Here the function
V_{3}(w_{l};\bar{w}_{l},\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1})
is given for each flavor choice by Eqs. (
129
), (
130
), (
131
), and (
132
). The operator
\bm{\Gamma}^{(1,0)\dagger}_{k}
is defined analogously.

To define
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\bm{\Gamma}^{(1,0)}_{l}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}
, we note first of all that an infrared singularity appears only when parton
m+1
is a gluon. Thus a
\mathrm{g}\to q+\bar{q}
splitting is not infrared singular. In the case of a
q\to q+\mathrm{g}
splitting or a
\bar{q}\to\bar{q}+\mathrm{g}
splitting, only the contribution from
\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1}=\mathrm{g}
is infrared singular. This contribution is provided by
V_{3}(w_{l};\bar{w}_{l},\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1})
and not by
V_{3}(w_{l};\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1},\bar{w}_{l})
. For a
\mathrm{g}\to\mathrm{g}+\mathrm{g}
splitting,
V_{3}(w_{l};\bar{w}_{l},\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1})
can provide an infrared singularity but
V_{3}(w_{l};\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1},\bar{w}_{l})
contains a factor
\bar{q}_{m\!+\!1}
that removes the singularity for
\bar{q}_{m\!+\!1}\to 0
. Thus we can define the infrared singular part of
{\bm{\Gamma}}^{(1,0)}_{l}
by keeping
V_{3}(w_{l};\bar{w}_{l},\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1})
in Eq. (
128
) and eliminating
V_{3}(w_{l};\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1},\bar{w}_{l})
and also eliminating the flavor choice
\mathrm{g}\to q+\bar{q}
entirely. Thus we define

\begin{split}&\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\right]^{1/2}\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\bar{w}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{{\bm{\Gamma}}^{(1,0)}_{l}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}\big|{\{w\}_{m}}\big\rangle\\
&\qquad=\prod_{i\neq l}\left[(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{d}(\bar{q}_{i}-q_{i})\,\delta_{\bar{f}_{i},f_{i}}\delta_{\bar{a}_{i},a_{i}}\delta_{\bar{r}_{i},r_{i}}\right]\\
&\qquad\quad\times(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{d}(\bar{q}_{l}+\bar{q}_{m\!+\!1}-q_{l})\,\\
&\qquad\quad\times{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{V_{3}(w_{l};\bar{w}_{l},\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}\,\theta(\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1}=\mathrm{g})\;.\end{split}

(213)

The same change is applied to
{\bm{\Gamma}}^{(1,0)\dagger}_{k}
.

We now need a definition for
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{V_{3}(w_{l};\bar{w}_{l},\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}
. This depends on the flavor choice.

For a
q\to q+\mathrm{g}
splitting, one more approximation is needed. In Eq. (
129
) for
V_{3}(w_{l};\bar{w}_{l},\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1})
, there is a factor
{\raise-1.31944pt\hbox to4.8229pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$q$}}_{l}={\raise-0.41112pt\hbox to8.06947pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\bar{q}_{l}$}}+{\raise 0.33888pt\hbox to5.00002pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\bar{q}$}}_{m\!+\!1}
. In the infrared limit,
\bar{q}_{m\!+\!1}\to 0
. Thus we set
\bar{q}_{m\!+\!1}
to zero in this factor in
V_{3}(w_{l};\bar{w}_{l},\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1})
, giving

\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{V_{3}(w_{l};\bar{w}_{l},\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}\hskip-65.44142pt{}&\\
={}&{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{s}}t^{\bar{a}_{m\!+\!1}}_{\bar{a}_{l}a_{l}}\left[\gamma^{\bar{r}_{m\!+\!1}}{\raise 0.33888pt\hbox to5.00002pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\bar{q}$}}_{l}\right]_{\bar{r}_{l}r_{l}}\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{q_{l}^{2}+{\mathrm{i}}0}\\
&\times\theta(f_{l}\in\{\mathrm{u},\mathrm{d},\dots\})\,\theta(\bar{f}_{l}=f_{l})\,\theta(\bar{f}_{m\!+\!1}=\mathrm{g})\;.\end{split}

(214)

For a
\bar{q}\to\bar{q}+\mathrm{g}
splitting, an analogous derivation gives

\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{V_{3}(w_{l};\bar{w}_{l},\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}\hskip-65.44142pt{}&\\
={}&-{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{s}}t^{\bar{a}_{m\!+\!1}}_{a_{l}\bar{a}_{l}}\,\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{q_{l}^{2}+{\mathrm{i}}0}\,\left[{\raise 0.33888pt\hbox to5.00002pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\bar{q}$}}_{l}\gamma^{\bar{r}_{m\!+\!1}}\right]_{r_{l}\bar{r}_{l}}\\
&\times\theta(f_{l}\in\{\bar{\mathrm{u}},\bar{\mathrm{d}},\dots\})\,\theta(\bar{f}_{l}=f_{l})\,\theta(\bar{f}_{m\!+\!1}=\mathrm{g})\;.\end{split}

(215)

For a
\mathrm{g}\to\mathrm{g}+\mathrm{g}
splitting, we use Eq. (
132
) with
N_{\mu r_{l}}(q_{l})=N_{\mu r_{l}}(\bar{q}_{l}+\bar{q}_{m\!+\!1})
replaced by
N_{\mu r_{l}}(\bar{q}_{l})
:

\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{V_{3}(w_{l};\bar{w}_{l},\bar{w}_{m\!+\!1})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}\hskip-65.44142pt{}&\\
={}&-\mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{s}}f_{a_{l},\bar{a}_{l},\bar{a}_{m\!+\!1}}\,\frac{{\mathrm{i}}N_{\mu r_{l}}(\bar{q}_{l})}{q_{l}^{2}+{\mathrm{i}}0}\\
&\times\!\left[g^{\bar{r}_{l}\bar{r}_{m\!+\!1}}\,\bar{q}_{l}^{\mu}+g^{\mu\bar{r}_{m\!+\!1}}\,\bar{q}_{l}^{\bar{r}_{l}}-2g^{\mu\bar{r}_{l}}\,\bar{q}_{l}^{\bar{r}_{m\!+\!1}}\right]\\
&\times\theta(f_{l}=\bar{f}_{l}=\bar{f}_{m\!+\!1}=\mathrm{g})\;.\end{split}

(216)

We make the analogous changes in
{\bm{\Gamma}}^{(1,0)\dagger}_{k}
. This defines
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}=\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
for each flavor choice.

IX
Structure of graphs

We now have a vector space, the extended statistical space, with enough structure to describe Feynman graphs for the ket amplitude and the bra amplitude and, in addition, enough structure to describe the momentum mapping that can take us from on-shell momenta
\{p\}_{m}
for
m
partons to on-shell momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}
for
\hat{m}
partons, with
\hat{m}\geq m
.

Let
\hat{\mathcal{G}}(G)
be the operator on the extended statistical space that generates the graph
G=(G_{\mathrm{ket}},G_{\mathrm{bra}})
and let
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
be the sum over graphs of
\hat{\mathcal{G}}(G)
:

\hat{\mathcal{G}}=\sum_{G}\hat{\mathcal{G}}(G)\;.

(217)

The initial state to which we apply
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
is a linear combination of basis states
|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m},\{p\}_{m}})
, obtained by applying the operator
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}
to an
m
parton state
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}})
in the statistical space. See Eq. (
200
). We assume that no subsets of the starting momenta
p_{i}
are close to being collinear or soft. The operator
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
can be expanded in perturbation theory and then expanded according to how many real emissions and virtual loops it contains:

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{G}}={}&1+\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(1)}+\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\right]^{2}\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(2)}+\cdots\\
={}&1+\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\,\left[\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(1,0)}+\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(0,1)}\right]\\
&+\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\right]^{2}\,\left[\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(2,0)}+\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(1,1)}+\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(0,2)}\right]+\cdots\end{split}

(218)

Here
\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(n_{\textsc{r}},n_{\textsc{v}})}
has
n_{\textsc{r}}
real emissions and
n_{\textsc{v}}
virtual loops. The graphs
G
then correspond to individual contributions to
\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(n_{\textsc{r}},n_{\textsc{v}})}
.

We have operators that generate elementary parton splittings and exchanges. These operators provide factors in the Feynman graphs
G
. Using these operators, we can create operators
\hat{\mathcal{G}}(G)
that create complete graphs. These include LSZ factors discussed in Secs.
VI.4
and
VIII.4
, so that they define an effective cross section. The graphs
G
are renormalized with
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
renormalization at a scale
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
. They thus include counter terms that remove ultraviolet divergences that come from virtual loops. We do not indicate the dependence of
G
and the corresponding operators on
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
in this and the following section.

The initial state in the extended statistical space is obtained by starting with a basis vector
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}})
in the statistical space. Then
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}})
gives us the initial state in the extended statistical space to which the operator
\hat{\mathcal{G}}(G)
is applied. The operator
\hat{\mathcal{G}}(G)
, acting on a state
|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m},\{p\}_{m}})
, produces a linear combination of basis states
|{\{\hat{w},\hat{w}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}},\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}})
in the extended statistical space. The parton momenta
\{\hat{q},\hat{q}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}
in
\{\hat{w},\hat{w}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}
are, at this point, undefined.

The operator
\hat{\mathcal{G}}(G)
includes a momentum mapping. For this, we start with parton momenta
\{p\}_{m}
in the initial state
|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m},\{p\}_{m}})
. The
p_{i}
are lightlike momenta with
\sum p_{i}=Q
. Then the final momenta in
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}
in
|{\{\hat{w},\hat{w}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}},\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}})
are
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}=R(G;\zeta_{G},\{p\}_{m})
. Typically, the factors that contribute to
\hat{\mathcal{G}}(G)
each contribute to the overall momentum mapping.

Next, the operator
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}
returns us to the statistical space, giving us

\big({\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big|\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}\,\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.

(219)

Here
|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}})
has
\hat{m}
partons with on-shell momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}
that were determined by the momentum mapping in
\hat{\mathcal{G}}(G)
. The momenta
\{\hat{q}\}_{\hat{m}}
and
\{\hat{q}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}
, which were undefined, are now are set to
\{\hat{q}\}_{\hat{m}}=\{\hat{q}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}=\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}
. It is only when the final state momenta are put on shell that the infrared singularities of interest to us appear.

With this final state, the initial momenta
\{q,q^{\prime}\}_{m}
are linear combinations of the final momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}
and the momenta
\ell
of any exchanged gluons. The momenta
\ell
do not enter that hard subgraph in Fig.
1
but rather flow through the effective multiparton vertices in Fig.
2
.

The operator
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}
sets the flavors
\{\hat{f}\}_{\hat{m}}
in the bra amplitude equal to the flavors
\{\hat{f}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}
in the ket amplitude. The operator
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}
also provides an appropriate mapping for colors and spins.

In the following section, we introduce a factorization of this complete
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
into a hard factor, with an effective lower cutoff on the scale
\mu
of splittings,
\mu_{\textsc{s}}<\mu
, and a soft factor, with an effective upper cutoff
\mu<\mu_{\textsc{s}}
.

X
Hard-soft factorization in the extended statistical space

The matrix element (
219
) produced by
\hat{\mathcal{G}}(G)
is singular when the momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}
form
m
infinitely narrow jets with momenta
\{p\}_{m}
. Of course, there are also singularities when subsets of the
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}
form more than
m
infinitely narrow jets. Although
\hat{\mathcal{G}}(G)
contains the infrared singularities that we seek to organize, it does not isolate these singularities. To isolate infrared singularities, we factor
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
into an infrared singular factor times a nonsingular factor. Since we are interested in using parton shower evolution to reproduce the infrared singularities of QCD, we need only the singular factor.

We write
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
in the form

\hat{\mathcal{G}}=\hat{\mathcal{A}}\,\hat{\mathcal{K}}\;,

(220)

where
\hat{\mathcal{A}}
is an infrared singular factor and
\hat{\mathcal{K}}
is an operator on the extended statistical space that we are free to define as long as it has two properties. First, the perturbative expansion of
\hat{\mathcal{K}}
begins with 1 at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{0}
. Second,
\hat{\mathcal{K}}
is free of infrared singularities. Eq. (
220
) implies

\hat{\mathcal{A}}=\hat{\mathcal{G}}\,\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{-1}\;.

(221)

The aim here is choose
\hat{\mathcal{K}}
so as to make the singular factor
\hat{\mathcal{A}}
as simple as possible. We can write Eq. (
221
) as

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{A}}={}&\left(1+\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(1)}+\cdots\right)\left(1-\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\,\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(1)}+\cdots\right)\\
={}&1+\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\left(\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(1)}-\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(1)}\right)+\cdots\;.\end{split}

(222)

The operator
\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(1)}
has contributions that we can label with an index
i
from several sorts of real or virtual first order graphs,

\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(1)}=\sum_{i}\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{i}^{(1)}\;.

(223)

Now we can choose corresponding contributions
\hat{\mathcal{K}}_{i}^{(1)}
to remove unwanted nonsingular terms from
\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{i}^{(1)}
.

We have encountered an example of this in Sec.
VIII.7
, in which
i
denotes first order graphs representing interference between parton emission from parton
l
and parton
k
. In this example

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}={}&\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)\;,\\
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}={}&\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}\;,\\
\hat{\mathcal{K}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}={}&\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1-{\mathsf{IR}}}}\;.\end{split}

(224)

At higher orders, similar considerations apply.

The net result of these manipulations is

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{G}}={}&\hat{\mathcal{A}}\,\hat{\mathcal{K}}\;.\end{split}

(225)

The operator
\hat{\mathcal{K}}
is infrared finite. Thus the infrared singularities of
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
are the same as the infrared singularities of
\hat{\mathcal{A}}
. For this reason, parton shower evolution can be based on
\hat{\mathcal{A}}
alone.

It is useful to define
\hat{\mathcal{A}}
so that it is independent of the renormalization scale
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
:

\mu_{\textsc{r}}\,\frac{d\hat{\mathcal{A}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}})}{d\mu_{\textsc{r}}}=0

(226)

We normally do not display the functional dependence of operators on
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
, but here we do display
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
as an argument of
\hat{\mathcal{A}}
. The dependence on
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
includes the dependence of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}})
on
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
. With Eq. (
226
), we can adjust
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
as needed to improve the usefulness of shower evolution. Eq. (
226
) should hold order by order in perturbation theory up to the order at which
\hat{\mathcal{A}}
is defined. This is straightforward at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{1}
. We will address this issue at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}
in future work.

We can now go further to separate singular and nonsingular contributions. Consider a graph
G=(G_{\mathrm{ket}},G_{\mathrm{bra}})
with
\hat{m}
final state partons and
m
initial partons. The graph is singular when the momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}
form
m
infinitely narrow jets with momenta
\{p\}_{m}
. Of course, there are also singularities when subsets of the
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}
form more than
m
infinitely narrow jets. Specifically,
G
can be singular at points at which the emitted partons are grouped into jet subsets
J(j)
,
j\in\{1,\dots,n_{J}\}
and a soft subset
S
. At these singular points, emitted parton momenta for
i\in S
vanish and emitted parton momenta for
i\in J(j)
are collinear. If we integrate the emitted parton momenta near the singularities using dimensional regularization, then the integration produces poles
1/\epsilon^{n}
. Additional poles appear from the virtual diagrams.

To isolate the singularities, we use the hardness measure
h
described in Sec.
VII.2
to classify the final states according to how many of the splittings in
G
were resolvable into separate jets at a scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
. Let
N_{J}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
be the number of new jets created by the splittings in
\hat{\mathcal{A}}(G)
that are resolvable at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
, as determined by a jet algorithm to be defined below. We distinguish between the two cases
N_{J}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=0
and
N_{J}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\geq 1
. We write

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{A}}(G)={}&\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}(G,\mu_{\textsc{s}})+\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}(G,\mu_{\textsc{s}})\;,\end{split}

(227)

with

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}(G,\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&\mathbb{J}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\star\hat{\mathcal{A}}(G)\;,\\
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i,\geq 1}(G,\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&\mathbb{J}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\star\hat{\mathcal{A}}(G)\;.\end{split}

(228)

Here we have used jet counting mappings
\mathbb{J}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
and
\mathbb{J}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
that are linear mappings on the space of operators
\hat{\mathcal{A}}
:

\begin{split}\mathbb{J}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})&\star\left(\alpha\,\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}+\beta\,\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\beta}\right)\\
={}&\alpha\,\mathbb{J}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\star\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}+\beta\,\mathbb{J}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\star\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\beta}\;,\\
\mathbb{J}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})&\star\left(\alpha\,\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}+\beta\,\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\beta}\right)\\
={}&\alpha\,\mathbb{J}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\star\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}+\beta\,\mathbb{J}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\star\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\beta}\;.\end{split}

(229)

Acting on a contribution
\hat{\mathcal{A}}(G)
that corresponds to an individual graph,
\mathbb{J}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\star\hat{\mathcal{A}}(G)
is
\hat{\mathcal{A}}(G)
with theta functions inserted into the integrations over splitting variables for each parton emission such that the emissions produce no new resolvable jets. Similarly,
\mathbb{J}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\star\hat{\mathcal{A}}(G)
produces at least one new jet that is resolvable at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
.

Summing over graphs
G
, this gives the decomposition

\hat{\mathcal{A}}=\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})+\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\;.

(230)

In the first term,
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
produces no extra jets that are resolvable at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
. In the second term,
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
produces at least one extra jet resolvable at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
.

We now define the jet counting mappings
\mathbb{J}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
and
\mathbb{J}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
. The number of new, resolvable jets is determined by the graph
G
and by the relation between the initial momenta
\{p\}_{m}
and the final momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}
, as fixed by the momentum mapping associated with
G
. Graphs with no real emissions have
\hat{m}=m
and
\{\hat{p}\}_{m}=\{p\}_{m}
. Therefore, these graphs contribute only to
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
. It is graphs with real emissions that can contribute to
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
.

We combine jets starting from the final state produced by the splittings in
\hat{\mathcal{A}}(G)
and working toward the starting hard state using the hardness measure
h
. The number
N_{J}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
of new jets resolvable at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
satisfies either
N_{J}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=0
or
N_{J}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\geq 1
. We define the conditions under which
N_{J}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\geq 1
.

Combining two partons.
Consider combining two partons with labels
i
and
j
. For
i,j
to be resolvable jets, we require, first, that

h(\hat{p}_{i}+\hat{p}_{j})>\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2}\;.

(231)

In addition, we require one or the other of two conditions. We ask that there be some parton index
a
such that
G_{\mathrm{ket}}
has a splitting
a\to i+j
. Or we ask that there be some index
b
such that
G_{\mathrm{bra}}
has a splitting
b\to i+j
. If one of these conditions holds in addition to
h(\hat{p}_{i}+\hat{p}_{j})>\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2}
, then
N_{J}(\mu_{s})\geq 1
.

Combining three partons.
Consider combining three partons with labels
i
,
j
, and
k
. For
i,j,k
to contain new jets, we require, first, that

h(\hat{p}_{i}+\hat{p}_{j}+\hat{p}_{k})>\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2}\;.

(232)

In addition, we require one or the other of two conditions. We ask that there be some index
a
such that
G_{\mathrm{ket}}
has a splitting
a\to i+j+k
. This could be a sequence of elementary splittings or a single elementary splitting. Or we ask that there be some index
b
such that
G_{\mathrm{bra}}
has a splitting
b\to i+j+k
. If one of these conditions holds in addition to
h(\hat{p}_{i}+\hat{p}_{j}+\hat{p}_{k})>\mu_{\textsc{s}}
, then
J(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\geq 1
.

If we work at higher than order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}
, then we consider combining more than three final state partons in the same way.

Notice that this algorithm combines partons into jets according to the hardness measure
h
and also according to the shower history corresponding to a particular operator
\hat{\mathcal{A}}(G)
. We could, for instance, encounter a parton pair
(i,j)
for which
h(\hat{p}_{i}+\hat{p}_{j})
is the smallest of the hardness measures for pairs of partons, but for which partons
i
and
j
are not the daughter partons in any splitting in the graph
G
. Such accidental combinations are ignored, even though these jets would be combined in an ordinary jet algorithm of the sort that is applied to experimental data.
14
14

14

When there are many jets produced by the parton shower, the chance that the pair
(i,j)
with the smallest
h(\hat{p}_{i}+\hat{p}_{j})
corresponds to the last splitting is typically quite small. This makes it impracticable to use just a simple jet algorithm that does not take the splitting history into account.

The operator

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\right]^{n}\sum_{n_{\textsc{r}}=0}^{n}\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{(n_{\textsc{r}},n-n_{\textsc{r}})}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\end{split}

(233)

is of special interest. Acting on a state
\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m_{0}},\{p\}_{m_{0}}}\big)
with
m_{0}\geq m
,
\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{(n_{\textsc{r}},n-n_{\textsc{r}})}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
creates
n_{\textsc{r}}
extra partons that are unresolvable from the previous partons at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
and contains
n_{\textsc{v}}=n-n_{\textsc{r}}
virtual loops. Evidently,
\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{(n_{\textsc{r}},n-n_{\textsc{r}})}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
has infrared singularities from final state partons becoming collinear or soft and may also have infrared poles
1/\epsilon
from integrating over loop momenta.

The operator
\hat{\mathcal{A}}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
is related to another operator

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&1+\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\;.\end{split}

(234)

Up to second order, this is

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\textsc{h}}^{(1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}^{(1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\;,\\
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\textsc{h}}^{(2)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}^{(2)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})-\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}^{(1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\,\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}^{(1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\;.\end{split}

(235)

We now sketch an argument that
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
is free of infrared singularities.

At first order,
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}^{(1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
produces the emission of a parton. Integrating over the splitting variables with the restriction that the splitting is resolvable at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
does not produce an infrared singularity.

At second order,
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}^{(2)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
could describe two emissions. According to Eq. (
173
), the first emission must be the hardest. It is thus resolvable at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
. The second, softer, emission could be unresolvable. Thus
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}^{(2)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
could create an infrared singularity from the integration over the splitting variables of the second emission. However, just in the limit that produces the infrared singularity, there is a contribution to
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}^{(1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\,\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}^{(1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
that gives two emissions, the first hard and the second containing exactly the same infrared singularity. Because of the minus sign in this contribution, the singularities cancel.

Similarly,
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}^{(2)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
could describe a parton emission that is resolvable at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
followed by a virtual exchange graph or a virtual self-energy graph. The integration over the loop momentum in the virtual graph can then produce an infrared divergence. However, there is a contribution to
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}^{(1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\,\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}^{(1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
that gives a matching resolvable emission and then a matching infrared divergent virtual graph. Again, the singularities cancel.

At higher perturbative orders,
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
has at least one parton emission that is resolvable at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
. This can be followed by softer emissions or by virtual graphs that produce no resolvable jets but do produce infrared singularities. However, the same infrared singularities will be cancelled by singularities in
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
.

We will examine in future papers how this cancellation works at second order in
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
.

We conclude that
\hat{\mathcal{A}}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
can be decomposed into a product

\hat{\mathcal{A}}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\left\{1+\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\right\}\;.

(236)

The first factor,
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
, generates infrared singularities. The second factor has no infrared singularities. The operator
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
first introduced in Sec.
IV.2
is designed to reflect the infrared singularities of QCD. For this reason, we will base the definition of
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
on
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
.

XI
Hard-soft factorization in the statistical space

Let us approximate the cross section for an infrared safe observable represented by an operator
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}
in the statistical space, starting with a statistical state
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}})
as

\sigma^{(m)}_{\textsc{j}}=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}\,\mathcal{A}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.

(237)

Here

\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\hat{\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}\;,

(238)

where
\hat{\mathcal{A}}
was defined in Eq. (
225
) from
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
. Using

\mathcal{G}=\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\hat{\mathcal{G}}\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}\;,

(239)

the relation between
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{G}
is

\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{A}\,\mathcal{K}\;,

(240)

where

\begin{split}\mathcal{K}={}&[\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\hat{\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}]^{-1}\,\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}[\hat{\mathcal{A}}\hat{\mathcal{K}}]\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}\;.\end{split}

(241)

The operator
\hat{\mathcal{K}}
is free of infrared singularities and poles. Thus the infrared behavior of
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}[\hat{\mathcal{A}}\hat{\mathcal{K}}]\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}
matches the infrared behavior of
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\hat{\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}
. We conclude that
\mathcal{K}
is free of infrared singularities and poles. Thus
\mathcal{A}
is
\mathcal{G}
with an infrared finite part removed.

In Eq. (
237
), the basis state represents a hard state with
m
partons in which none of the partons are nearly collinear with other partons or soft. We can think of these partons as jets, labelled with momenta, flavors, colors, and spins.
15
15

15

Each jet has a single flavor
f
. However, color and spin are treated in the description used in the statistical space, Sec.
IV.1
, using labels
\{c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m_{0}}
.

We separate
\mathcal{A}
into two terms according to whether or not it produces resolvable jets at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
:

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})+\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\;,

(242)

where

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}\;,\\
\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}\;.\end{split}

(243)

Then we can write
\mathcal{A}
in a form analogous to Eq. (
236
) in the extended statistical space:

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\left\{1+\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\right\}\;.

(244)

As in Eq. (
236
), we conclude that the operator in braces here is free of infrared singularities and poles because the infrared behavior of
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}
matches the infrared behavior the full operator of
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\hat{\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}
.

XI.1
Infrared renormalization via probability conservation

The zero jet operator has a perturbative expansion

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&1+\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\,\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\\
&+\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\right]^{2}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(2)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})+\cdots\;.\end{split}

(245)

Although each operator
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
is infrared singular, when we measure the contribution to the total probability coming from this operator using
\big({1}\big|\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
, we know that
\big({1}\big|\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
must be infrared finite because of cancellations between real emission graphs and virtual graphs. Thus, as in Ref.
NSAllOrder
. we can define another operator, which, using the “
\star
” notation of Sec.
X
, we denote by
\mathbb{P}\star\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
. This operator has no infrared singularities, does not create new partons, does not change the momenta and flavors of partons, and obeys

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\big({1}\big|\big[\mathbb{P}\star\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\big]\;.

(246)

If partons did not have colors and spins,
\big[\mathbb{P}\star\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}\big]|{\{p,f\}_{m_{0}}})
would simply be
|{\{p,f\}_{m_{0}}})
times an eigenvalue obtained by integrating over the splitting variables for the unresolved splittings in
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
. With colors and spins, the operator
\mathbb{P}\star\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
does not change the momenta and flavors of partons but modifies the colors and spins of the partons in a statistical state on which it acts.

In what follows, we will adopt a more compact notation, from Ref.
NSAllOrder
, for
\mathbb{P}\star\mathcal{A}_{0}
:

\mathbb{P}\star\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\;.

(247)

With this notation, Eq. (
246
) is

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\big({1}\big|\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\;.

(248)

We will discuss details of the definition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\cdots}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
further in Sec.
XII.4
. For now, it suffices to outline the intended physical idea. Let
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
be applied to a statistical space state
\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m_{0}}}\big)
with
m_{0}
partons. We can consider the
m_{0}
partons to be
m_{0}
jets if we extend the idea of a jet to be a QCD object that carries flavor, color, and spin in addition to momentum. The resulting state,

\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m_{0}}}\big)\;,

is a linear combination of states with
\{m_{0},m_{0}+1,\dots,m_{0}+n\}
partons. Thus the
m_{0}
jets now have substantial internal structure. However, this structure cannot be resolved at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
. Using the operator
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
, we replace this state with a state

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m_{0}}}\big)

that has
m_{0}
jets with the same momenta and flavors
\{p,f\}_{m_{0}}
as before but with the colors and spins rearranged so that the total probabilities of the two states are the same

\begin{split}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}&(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m_{0}}}\big)\\
={}&\big({1}\big|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m_{0}}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(249)

Thus the effect of the unresolved interactions at scales smaller than
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
is accounted for by replacing the original
m_{0}
jets by the same number of what we can call infrared renormalized jets. We can think of this as being similar to ultraviolet renormalization.

To make use of this, define an infrared nonsingular operator
\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
by

\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\left\{1+\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\right\}\;.

(250)

Then define an operator
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
that relates
\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
and
\mathcal{A}
by

\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\,\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\mathcal{A}\;.

(251)

We can write this as

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}{}&\left\{1+\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\right\}\\
={}&\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\left\{1+\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\right\}\;.\end{split}

(252)

This implies

\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}=\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\;,

(253)

so that

\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}^{-1}\;.

(254)

We learn two things from this representation. First,
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
creates parton splittings, but all of these splittings are unresolvable at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
. Second,

\begin{split}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}^{-1}\\
