={}&\big({1}\big|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}^{-1}\;,\end{split}

(255)

so

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\big({1}\big|\;.

(256)

Eq. (
251
) gives us an interesting result when we insert it into Eq. (
237
):

\sigma^{(m)}_{\textsc{j}}=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.

(257)

Suppose that
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}
is infrared safe at a scale
\mu_{\textsc{j}}
that is larger than
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
. Since
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}
does not resolve the splittings in
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
, the operators
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}
and
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
commute:

\sigma^{(m)}_{\textsc{j}}=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.

(258)

Then Eq. (
256
) gives us

\sigma^{(m)}_{\textsc{j}}=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.

(259)

In Eq. (
237
) the cross section
\sigma^{(m)}_{\textsc{j}}
is written using what might be called bare jets, so that we need to work in
4-2\epsilon
dimensions to express the result for
\sigma^{(m)}_{\textsc{j}}
. When
\mu_{\textsc{j}}>\mu_{\textsc{s}}
, we can use Eq. (
259
), in which
\sigma^{(m)}_{\textsc{j}}
is written using what we can call infrared renormalized jets, created by the operator
\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
. This equation can be applied in four dimensions.

We define a shower generator
\mathcal{S}(\mu)
by

\mu_{\textsc{s}}\frac{d}{d\mu_{\textsc{s}}}\,\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\,\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})

(260)

or, since
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\,\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
is independent of
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
,

\mu_{\textsc{s}}\frac{d}{d\mu_{\textsc{s}}}\,\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=-\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\,\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\;.

(261)

This is a renormalization group equation for the infrared renormalization of
\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
. The operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu)
is the analogue of the anomalous dimension in the renormalization group associated with ultraviolet renormalization.

We will return to an examination of the structure of
\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
in Sec.
XI.3
.

XI.2
Parton shower from
\mathcal{D}

The operator
\mathcal{D}(\mu)
is used to construct a parton shower according to the argument of Ref.
NSAllOrder
. We review this argument here. Consider the cross section
\sigma_{\textsc{j}}
for an infrared safe QCD observable
16
16

16

One could consider the production of particles that do not have strong interactions, but that is beyond the scope of this paper.

\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}
based on a statistical state
|{\rho})
:

\begin{split}\sigma_{\textsc{j}}={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}\big|{\rho}\big)\;.\end{split}

(262)

We take
|{\rho})
to be the statistical state for
e^{+}e^{-}\to\mathrm{hadrons}
calculated at an arbitrarily high order of perturbation theory, without approximations. We also take the operators that we will use to be calculated at an arbitrarily high order of perturbation theory. We will then discuss approximations to this that account for the fact that calculations to arbitrarily high orders of perturbation theory are not available.

Now let
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
be a hardness scale as described in the previous sections. In operators that depend on
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
, we set the renormalization scale to
\mu_{\textsc{r}}=\mu_{\textsc{s}}
. We will normally choose
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
to be a hard scale
\mu_{\textsc{h}}
close to
\sqrt{Q^{2}}
, but we can leave this choice adjustable.

We insert
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\,\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
into Eq. (
262
), giving

\begin{split}\sigma_{\textsc{j}}={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\,\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\big|{\rho}\big)\;.\end{split}

(263)

The statistical state
\big|{\rho}\big)
contains infrared singularities corresponding to emissions of partons that are nearly collinear or zero momentum and poles corresponding to integrations over virtual loops. However, the operator
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
removes these infrared poles and removes collinear and soft singularities below the scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
. Thus
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})|{\rho})
is infrared finite.

There are now two cases to consider for the operator
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}
. First, suppose that
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}
is infrared safe at a scale
\mu_{\textsc{j}}
that is larger than
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
.
17
17

17

To be a little more precise,
\mu_{\textsc{j}}
should be enough larger than
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
that powers of
\mu_{\textsc{s}}/\mu_{\textsc{j}}
can be neglected.
Then
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}
commutes with
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
, so that

\begin{split}\sigma_{\textsc{j}}={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\,\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}\,\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\big|{\rho}\big)\;.\end{split}

(264)

Now use
({1}|\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=({1}|
, which we take to hold at arbitrarily high perturbative order even though we do not know
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
beyond a few perturbative orders. This gives

\begin{split}\sigma_{\textsc{j}}={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\big|{\rho}\big)\;.\end{split}

(265)

With the choice
\mu_{\textsc{s}}=\mu_{\textsc{h}}
, this is a compact statement of the standard method for calculating a cross section in QCD at next-to-leading order or a higher perturbative order. The calculation is in
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})|{\rho})
, which is derived from Feynman graphs with their infrared singularities subtracted. Of course, the perturbative expansion of
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})|{\rho})
is truncated at whatever order is available.

Consider now what is needed when the infrared safety scale
\mu_{\textsc{j}}
of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}
is much smaller than
\sqrt{Q^{2}}
. Then we could choose a scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}=\mu_{\mathrm{f}}
that is smaller than
\mu_{\textsc{j}}
and use

\begin{split}\sigma_{\textsc{j}}={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}})\,\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}})\big|{\rho}\big)\\
={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}})\,\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}\,\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}})\big|{\rho}\big)\\
={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}\,\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}})\big|{\rho}\big)\;.\end{split}

(266)

This formulation has a problem: the perturbative expansion of
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}})|{\rho})
contains large logarithms of
\mu_{\mathrm{f}}/\sqrt{Q^{2}}
. We can calculate
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}})
only to low perturbative orders, so that the large logarithms hinder the usefulness of Eq. (
266
). Instead, we can define

\begin{split}\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})={}&\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}})^{-1}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})\;.\end{split}

(267)

Then

\begin{split}\sigma_{\textsc{j}}={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})\big|{\rho}\big)\;.\end{split}

(268)

Here it is sensible to truncate the perturbative expansion of
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})\big|{\rho}\big)
at whatever order is available. Then, instead of expanding
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})
to a fixed order of perturbation theory, we write it as

\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})=\mathbb{T}\exp\left(\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}}^{\mu_{\textsc{h}}}\!\frac{d\mu_{\textsc{s}}}{\mu_{\textsc{s}}}\,\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\right)\;,

(269)

where
\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
is the shower generator defined in Eq. (
260
). We can expand
\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
:

\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}})}{2\pi}\right]^{n}\mathcal{S}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{r}})\;.

(270)

The coefficients
\mathcal{S}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{r}})
depend on the renormalization scale
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
, but in
\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
this dependence is cancelled by the dependence of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}})
on
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
. Thus one can choose the value of
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
. In order to avoid large logarithms of
\mu_{\textsc{s}}/\mu_{\textsc{r}}
in the coefficients, a sensible choice is
\mu_{\textsc{r}}=\mu_{\textsc{s}}
. Now if we truncate the perturbative expansion of
\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
at a finite order, then the exponential in Eq. (
269
) will give an approximation to
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})
that contains all orders of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
. This has at least the possibility of providing a useful summation of the terms with large logarithms.

With the formulation (
268
), we start with
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})\big|{\rho}\big)
, which has had the infrared singularities that were present in
|{\rho})
removed at a large scale
\mu_{\textsc{h}}
, so that this calculation does not generate large logarithms, Then we generate a parton shower with
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})
, then measure the observable with
({1}|\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}
.

An alternative expression to Eq. (
268
) for a perturbative hard scattering followed by a parton shower starts with a hard scattering state
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})|{\rho})
, where
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})|{\rho})
represents a different perturbative calculation with its infrared singularities removed. Then

\begin{split}\sigma_{\textsc{j}}={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}}\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})\,\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})\tilde{\mathcal{D}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})\\
&\times\tilde{\mathcal{D}}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})\big|{\rho}\big)\;.\end{split}

(271)

The operator
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})\tilde{\mathcal{D}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})
matches the fixed order perturbative calculation to the parton shower according to the
MC@NLO
prescription
MCatNLO
.

There is a complication in the argument for adjusting
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
. The Green functions represented in
\mathcal{S}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{r}})
depend on the gauge parameters
v^{2}
and
\xi
, although we have generally not indicated this dependence. The gauge parameters are renormalized, so that they depend on
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
. That is, for general Green functions, we should use not only the running
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}})
but also running gauge parameters
v^{2}(\mu_{\textsc{r}})
and
\xi(\mu_{\textsc{r}})
. However,
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
is constructed to represent the infrared singularities of the cross section for
e^{+}+e^{-}\to

hadrons
. The cross section, and thus its infrared behavior, is independent of
v^{2}
and
\xi
. The construction of
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
involves approximations that can introduce
v^{2}(\mu_{\textsc{r}})
and
\xi(\mu_{\textsc{r}})
dependence to contributions to the cross section that are not infrared singular. As we will see in Sec.
XII
, there is no
v^{2}
or
\xi
dependence in the first order shower as defined in this paper. However
v^{2}
or
\xi
could appear at higher orders, depending on how the higher order splitting operators are defined. The infrared nonsingular contributions can be adjusted to simplify the shower evolution, as we have already done for other issues. We conclude that when we adjust
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
, we can leave
v^{2}
and
\xi
at fixed values, for example
(v^{2},\xi)=(4,1)
. This adjusts the behavior of the splitting operators away from the infrared singularities, but gives a physically equivalent parton shower. We could, alternatively, use running gauge parameters
v^{2}(\mu_{\textsc{r}})
and
\xi(\mu_{\textsc{r}})
. We leave the investigation of these possibilities to future work.

XI.3
The operator
\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})

In Sec.
XI.1
, we defined the operator
\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
which we described as creating jets that are infrared renormalized at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
. If we take the scale to be
\mu_{\textsc{s}}=\mu_{\textsc{h}}
, the state
\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}})
in Eq. (
259
) is close to the starting basis state
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}})
, with perturbative corrections that are not enhanced by large logarithms. However, if
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
is substantially smaller than
\mu_{\textsc{h}}
, the perturbative corrections are enhanced by large logarithms and can contain many more partons than
m
. In that case, we can write

\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})\,\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})\;.

(272)

Now,
\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
is expressed as an operator
\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})
with a well behaved perturbative expansion times a parton shower operator
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})
that takes the state from the large scale
\mu_{\textsc{h}}
to the smaller scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
. The shower then stops at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
, so that
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
serves as an infrared renormalization scale for the jets produced by
\mathcal{I}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
.

XI.4
Infrared singularities

Consider the matrix element of
\big|{\rho}\big)
with a statistical space basis state,
({\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}}|{\rho})
. This matrix element contains infrared poles and has logarithmic infrared singularities whenever two or more of the momenta
\hat{p}_{i}
become collinear or some of these momenta become soft. How can we characterize these poles and singularities?

We can write the matrix element in the form

\displaystyle\big({\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big|{\rho}\big)\hskip-71.13188pt

(273)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\big({\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big|\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\,\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\big|{\rho}\big)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\big({\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big|\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})\big|{\rho}\big)\;.

Inserting a sum over intermediate states, this becomes

\displaystyle\big({\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big|{\rho}\big)\hskip-56.9055pt

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{m}\int\!d\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}

(274)

\displaystyle\times\big({\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big|\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)

\displaystyle\times\big({\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})\big|{\rho}\big)\;.

In this expression,
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})|{\rho})
equals
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})|{\rho})
when expanded to arbitrarily high perturbative order. However, if
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})|{\rho})
is known only up to order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{N}
, then
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})|{\rho})
truncated at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{N}
contains only up to
m=N+2
partons. When
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
is substantially smaller than
\mu_{\textsc{h}}
, we will be interested in many more partons than
N+2
. Thus we write
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})|{\rho})
in the form
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})|{\rho})
. Then it is a good approximation to expand
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}})|{\rho})
to order
N
and to expand the exponent in
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\textsc{s}},\mu_{\textsc{h}})
to order
N
. That is, we use perturbation theory matched to a parton shower as a good approximation to
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})|{\rho})
.

Note that the matrix element in the last line of Eq. (
XI.4
) is infrared finite. It has no infrared poles and its singularities as functions of the parton momenta
\{p\}_{m}
are removed below scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
.

The remaining factor in Eq. (
XI.4
) is the matrix element of
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
. This matrix element contains the infrared singularities.

Fig.
2
from Sec.
IV.2
is a pictorial representation of Eq. (
XI.4
). As we have seen in this paper, there is some freedom available in the definition of
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
since we can remove parts of
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
that are not infrared singular. We have done that in the previous sections. Modifying
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
then modifies the infrared finite matrix element in the last line of Eq. (
XI.4
) without changing
\big|{\rho}\big)
. We use Eq. (
XI.4
) to define
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{N}
, which allows us to define shower splitting functions at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{N}
.

XII
First order splitting functions

In this section we provide the first order operators needed to build the splitting functions for a first order parton shower. These operators also provide building blocks for order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}
parton shower evolution. We also provide a brief review of the construction that we have used in this paper as it applies at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
.

XII.1
Introduction

In Eq. (
104
), we defined the operator
{\bm{G}}
that represents the graphs that can contribute to the infrared singular part of the Feynman amplitude:

{\bm{G}}={\bm{L}}{\bm{W}}{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\textsc{h}}\;.

(275)

The operator
{\bm{L}}
provides the LSZ factor for the outgoing partons. The operator
{\bm{W}}
provides real radiation with possible vertex and self-energy corrections. The operator
{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\textsc{h}}
represents the 1PI graphs involving the hard vertex that was introduced by the composite operator
\mathcal{H}(x)
, but defined so that there is no contribution from the ultraviolet region of loop momenta
\ell
. The dependence of
{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\textsc{h}}
on the renormalization scale is also adjusted according to the anomalous dimensions of the quark and gluon fields in
\mathcal{H}(x)
. In Sec.
VI
we defined and discussed the perturbative expansion of the these operators.

We expand
{\bm{G}}
in perturbation theory. Working at lowest order, we will need the order
g_{\mathrm{s}}
part of
{\bm{W}}
:
{\bm{\Gamma}}^{(1,0)}_{l}
, which describes the splitting of parton
l
into two partons, Eq. (
128
). We will also need the order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
part of
{\bm{L}}
,
{\bm{L}}_{l}^{(1)}
, which describes a self energy graph on line
l
, Eq. (
120
). Finally, we will need the order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
part of
{\bm{\Gamma}}_{\textsc{h}}
from Eq. (
142
). This includes
{\bm{E}}_{lk}^{(0,1)}
, which describes the exchange of a parton between partons
l
and
k
, Eq. (
145
). This is to be combined with the corresponding contribution from the operators
{\bm{N}}_{l}^{(0,1)}
, Eq. (
161
).

Quantum evolution space

We use the elementary operators in the operator
{\bm{G}}
to define an operator
{\hat{\bm{G}}}
that acts on the quantum evolution space and represents the product of Feynman graphs for the ket amplitude, Feynman graphs for the conjugate bra amplitude, and an operator
{\hat{\bm{h}}}
that measures of the hardness
\mu
of splittings. Following the notation that
{\hat{\bm{G}}}^{(n_{\textsc{r}},n_{\textsc{v}})}
is the part of
{\hat{\bm{G}}}
with
n_{\textsc{r}}
real emissions and
n_{\textsc{v}}
virtual loops, we have at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
,

{\hat{\bm{G}}}=1+\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\left({\hat{\bm{G}}}^{(1,0)}+{\hat{\bm{G}}}^{(0,1)}\right)+\cdots\;.

(276)

The real emission part is

{\hat{\bm{G}}}^{(1,0)}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l}{\bm{\Gamma}}^{(1,0)}_{l}\otimes{\bm{\Gamma}}^{(1,0)\dagger}_{l}+\sum_{k\neq l}{\bm{\Gamma}}^{(1,0)}_{l}\otimes{\bm{\Gamma}}^{(1,0)\dagger}_{k}\;.

(277)

The first term describes the splitting of parton
l
both in the ket and bra amplitudes. This operator leads to collinear and soft singularities. The statistical factor 1/2 is needed because the two partons produced by the splitting are indistinguishable. The second term represents the graph in which parton
m+1
is emitted from parton
l
in the ket amplitude and by parton
k
in the bra amplitude. This operator is singular only in the limit in which parton
m+1
is soft, and only when this parton is a gluon. We can represent the full real emission operator by Feynman graphs:

\begin{split}&\big({\{\bar{w},\bar{w}^{\prime}\}_{m+1}}\big|{\hat{\bm{G}}}^{(1,0)}\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;.\end{split}

(278)

Here the lines for spectator partons are not shown.

The operator
{\hat{\bm{G}}}^{(1,0)}
can be written as

{\hat{\bm{G}}}^{(1,0)}=\sum_{l}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\bigg[{\hat{\bm{C}}}_{l}^{(1,0)}(\mu)+\sum_{k\neq l}{\hat{\bm{X}}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)\bigg]\;.

(279)

The definitions (
177
) and (
178
) of
{\hat{\bm{C}}}_{l}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
and
{\hat{\bm{X}}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
include an operator
{\hat{\bm{h}}}(A,B;\mu)
, Eq. (
174
), that sets the scale
\mu
of the splittings. However

\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,{\hat{\bm{h}}}(A,B;\mu)=1\;.

(280)

Thus the scale setting operator is not present in Eq. (
277
). The scale definition is used later in Eq. (
291
).

The part of
{\hat{\bm{G}}}
that gives first order virtual contributions is

\begin{split}{\hat{\bm{G}}}^{(0,1)}={}&\sum_{l}\left[{\bm{L}}_{l}^{(1)}\otimes\bm{1}+\bm{1}\otimes{\bm{L}}_{l}^{(1)}\right]\\
&+\sum_{l}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\neq l}\left[{\bm{E}}_{lk}^{(0,1)}\otimes\bm{1}+\bm{1}\otimes{\bm{E}}_{lk}^{(0,1)\,\dagger}\right]\\
&+\sum_{l}\left[{\bm{N}}_{l}^{(0,1)}\otimes\bm{1}+\bm{1}\otimes{\bm{N}}_{l}^{(0,1)\dagger}\right]\;.\end{split}

(281)

Here there is no splitting scale
\mu
since there is no splitting. The first term is the contribution of the LSZ factors at one-loop level and the second term represents a single parton exchange between parton
l
and parton
k
in either the ket amplitude or the bra amplitude. The exchange operator
\bm{E}_{lk}^{(0,1)}
was defined in Eq. (
145
) and
{\bm{N}}_{l}^{(0,1)}
was defined in Eq. (
161
).

Extended statistical space

These operators on the quantum evolution space give rise to operators in the extended statistical space, for which we need a momentum mapping operator. Thus, for instance, the operator
{\hat{\bm{C}}}_{l}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
is extended to an operator
\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{l}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
. For the real emission operator,

\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(1,0)}=\sum_{l}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\bigg[\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{l}^{(1,0)}(\mu)+\sum_{k\neq l}\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)\bigg]\;.

(282)

The operator
\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{l}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
, Eq. (
193
), comes with a collinear style mapping of the jet momenta, while the operator
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
, Eq. (
194
), has a soft style mapping. We define these mappings in Appendices
A
and
B
, respectively.

For the extension of the virtual operator
{\hat{\bm{G}}}^{(0,1)}
to the extended statistical space, we supplement
{\hat{\bm{G}}}^{(0,1)}
in Eq. (
281
) by a momentum mapping, which is simply the unit operator (as in Eq. (
199
)):

\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(0,1)}={\hat{\bm{G}}}^{(0,1)}\otimes\bm{1}_{\mathrm{p}}\;.

(283)

The next step is to simplify the operators in
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
so as to retain parts that create infrared singularities but remove parts that are not infrared singular. We denote infrared singular parts by
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\cdots}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}
. According to Eq. (
220
), the operator
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
can be written as

\hat{\mathcal{G}}=\hat{\mathcal{A}}\hat{\mathcal{K}}\;,

(284)

where
\hat{\mathcal{K}}
is an infrared finite operator and
\hat{\mathcal{A}}
retains the infrared singularities of
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
. Expanding this at first order,

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{A}}={}&1+\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\left(\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(1,0)}-\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(1,0)}+\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(0,1)}-\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(0,1)}\right)\\
&+\cdots\;.\end{split}

(285)

The part of the singular operator that represents first order real emissions is

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{(1,0)}={}&\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(1,0)}-\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(1,0)}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\hat{\mathcal{S}}^{(1,0)}(\mu)\;.\end{split}

(286)

Here we have introduced the operator

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{S}}^{(1,0)}(\mu)={}&\sum_{l}\hat{\mathcal{C}}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\neq l\end{subarray}}\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)\;,\end{split}

(287)

where, as in Sec.
VIII.7
,

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\hat{\mathcal{X}}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{IR}}}\;.\end{split}

(288)

From Eqs. (
282
), (
286
), and (
287
), we obtain the infrared finite operator
\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(1,0)}
:

\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(1,0)}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\hat{\mathcal{X}}^{(1,0)}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1-{\mathsf{IR}}}}\;.

(289)

We will see in Sec.
XII.4
that we do not need an explicit representation for
\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{(0,1)}
in order to define the evolution of a first order shower. We can choose
\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(0,1)}=0
so that
\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{(0,1)}
is simply
\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(0,1)}
as in Eq. (
283
). We leave other possible choices to future work at higher order, where
\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{(0,1)}
appears explicitly.

Introduction of the shower scale

The next step is introduce the shower scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
and to break up
\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{(1,0)}
into the part that creates no jets that are resolvable at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
,
\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{(1,0)}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
, and the part that creates at least one jet resolvable at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
,
\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{(1,0)}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
, as in Eq. (
227
):

\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{(1,0)}=\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{(1,0)}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})+\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{(1,0)}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\;.

(290)

The jet measurement is based on the hardness scale of the splitting, so that

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{(1,0)}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&\int_{0}^{\mu_{\textsc{s}}}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\hat{\mathcal{S}}^{(1,0)}(\mu)\;,\\
\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{(1,0)}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&\int_{\mu_{\textsc{s}}}^{\infty}\frac{d\mu}{\mu}\,\hat{\mathcal{S}}^{(1,0)}(\mu)\;.\end{split}

(291)

Evolution in the statistical space

These considerations give us first order operators on the extended statistical space. The final step is to return to the statistical space, as described in Sec.
XI
. Now the operator
\hat{\mathcal{A}}
is translated into an operator
\mathcal{A}
that acts on the statistical space, with

\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\hat{\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}\;.

(292)

As in the extended statistical space, we divide
\mathcal{A}
into an operator
\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
that creates no additional jets that are resolvable at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
and an operator
\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
that creates at least one additional jet that is resolvable at scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
.

In the statistical space, we impose probability conservation in shower evolution by using an operator mapping
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\cdots}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
with the property

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\big({1}\big|\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\;.

(293)

This allows us to define the infrared singular operator
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
by

\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}^{-1}\;,

(294)

as in Sec.
XI
. We will define the action of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\cdots}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
at first order in Sec.
XII.4
below.

Given
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
, the splitting operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu)
that acts on the statistical space is defined by

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}(\mu)={}&\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu)\,\mu\frac{d\mathcal{D}(\mu)}{d\mu}\;.\end{split}

(295)

The splitting operator has a perturbative expansion that starts with

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}(\mu)={}&\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\left(\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu)+\mathcal{S}^{(0,1)}(\mu)\right)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2})\;.\end{split}

(296)

Then

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu)={}&\mu\frac{d\mathcal{D}^{(1,0)}(\mu)}{d\mu}\;,\\
\mathcal{S}^{(0,1)}(\mu)={}&\mu\frac{d\mathcal{D}^{(0,1)}(\mu)}{d\mu}\;.\end{split}

(297)

There are two contributions to
\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
, The first corresponds to the emission of a new parton
m+1
from parton
l
in the ket state and in the bra state. This has a collinear singularity and a soft singularity. The second corresponds to the emission of a new parton
m+1
from parton
l
in the ket state and from parton
k
in the bra state. This has only a soft singularity. We describe these operators in the following subsections.

There is also a part of
\mathcal{D}
that describes first order virtual graphs,
\mathcal{D}^{(0,1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
. We will find in Sec.
XII.4
that we can determine
\mathcal{D}^{(0,1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
from
\mathcal{D}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
by using the mapping
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\cdots}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
.

XII.2
Collinear splitting

\begin{split}\big({\{\bar{w},\bar{w}^{\prime}\}_{m+1},\{\bar{p}\}_{m+1}}\big|\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{l}^{(1,0)}(\mu)\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m},\{p\}_{m}}\big)\hskip-142.26378pt&\\
={}&\big({\{\bar{p}\}_{m+1}}\big|\mathcal{P}^{(1)}_{\rm coll}(l)\big|{\{p\}_{m}}\big)\\
&\times 2\mu^{2}\,\delta\big(\mu^{2}-\max[h(q_{l}),h(q^{\prime}_{l})]\big)\\
&\times\hbox to147.94pt{\vbox to72.1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 38.40327pt\lower-36.48904pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

Figure 6:
First order, topology 1.
Collinear emission diagram, the non-interacting lines are omitted here.

For a first order collinear splitting, with the emission of parton
m+1
from parton
l
in the ket state and in the bra state, we have an operator
{\hat{\mathcal{C}}}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)
that acts on the extended statistical space. This is illustrated in Fig.
6
. In the statistical space, this becomes an operator
\mathcal{C}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)
defined by

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}_{l}^{(1,0)}(\mu)={}&\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\,{\hat{\mathcal{C}}}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)\,\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}\;.\end{split}

(298)

The operator
\hat{\mathcal{C}}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)
is defined in Eq. (
193
). To
\hat{\mathcal{C}}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)
, we apply the mapping
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}
defined in Sec.
VIII.5
and the mapping
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}
defined in Sec.
VIII.6
. When we apply these definitions to a first order splitting using straightforward algebra, we obtain quite simple results. The result for
\mathcal{C}_{l}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
depends on the flavor choice and can be written in a rather compact form as

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}_{l}^{(1,0)}{}&(\mu)\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
={}&4\pi^{2}\!\!\!\sum_{\{\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{\hat{f}_{i},f_{i}}\int\!d\zeta_{\textsc{c}}\,\\
&\times\theta(f_{l}=\hat{f}_{l}+\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1})\\
&\times\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big)\,\\
&\times 2\mu^{2}\,\delta\big(\mu^{2}-h(\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1})\big)\\
&\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq l\end{subarray}}^{m}\delta_{\hat{s}_{i},s_{i}}\delta_{\hat{s}^{\prime}_{i},s^{\prime}_{i}}\,\frac{1}{[(\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1})^{2}]^{2}}\\
&\times\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\hat{c}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|t_{l}^{\dagger}(f_{l}\to\hat{f}_{l}+\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1})\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle\ \\
&\times\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|t_{l}(f_{l}\to\hat{f}_{l}+\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1})\big|{\{\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\\
&\times\big\langle{\hat{s}_{l},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|\bm{c}(p_{l},f_{l};\hat{p}_{l},\hat{f}_{l};\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1})\big|{s_{l}}\big\rangle\\
&\times\big\langle{s^{\prime}_{l}}\big|\bm{c}^{\dagger}(p_{l},f_{l};\hat{p}_{l},\hat{f}_{l};\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1})\big|{\hat{s}^{\prime}_{l},\hat{s}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle\;.\end{split}

(299)

We now turn to an explanation of the parts of Eq. (
299
), including the operator
t_{l}^{\dagger}
, which gives the color dependence of
\mathcal{C}_{l}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
, and the operator
\bm{c}
, which gives its spin dependence

The spectator partons retain their flavors. For the flavors of parton
l
and its daughter partons,
l
and
m+1
, we require flavor conservation:
f_{l}=\hat{f}_{l}+\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1}
with the evident QCD definition of adding flavors. For each type of splitting except for
\mathrm{g}\to\mathrm{g}+\mathrm{g}
, there are two equivalent contributions related by interchanging the daughter parton quantum numbers. We give results for one of these choices. The other choice is obtained by the interchange
l\leftrightarrow m+1
.

There is an integration over the splitting variables
\zeta_{\textsc{c}}
as given in general by Eq. (
182
) and for the collinear splitting by Eq. (
362
). The splitting scale
\mu
is set according to
\mu^{2}=h(\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1})
using the hardness function
h
from Eq. (
169
) or Eq. (
170
). The delta function setting
\mu
restricts the integration over
\zeta_{\textsc{c}}
.

The momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{m\!+\!1}
of the partons after the splitting are given as functions of
\zeta_{\textsc{c}}
and the momenta
\{p\}_{m}
before the splitting using the momentum mapping function given in general in Eq. (
34
) and for the collinear splitting in Eq. (
365
).

The momenta of the spectator partons are modified slightly by the momentum mapping. However, because of the mapping of spectator vector/spinor indices defined in Sec.
VIII.3
, the spins of the spectator partons are unchanged by the interaction, both for the ket partons and for the bra partons.

There is a new color state, obtained by inserting the color matrix for the given splitting onto line
l
in the ket color state and in the bra final state, as defined in Eqs. (
300
) and (
302
) below. The result
depends on the color basis used. For the trace basis, the result is described in Sec. 7.2 of Ref.
NSI
. We can describe how the color factors come about using the definitions given after Eq. (
298
). In any color
basis, the color factors are

\displaystyle\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\hat{c}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|t_{l}^{\dagger}(f_{l}\to\hat{f}_{l}+\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1})\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle

(300)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\{\hat{a}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\sum_{\{a\}_{m}}\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\hat{c}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|{\{\hat{a}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle\big\langle{\{a\}_{m}}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\times\big\langle{\hat{a}_{l},\hat{a}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|\bm{t}_{l}^{\dagger}(f_{l};\hat{f}_{l},\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1})\big|{a_{l}}\big\rangle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq l\end{subarray}}^{m}\delta_{\hat{a}_{i},a_{i}}\;,

where the single parton color operator is defined by its non-zero matrix elements,

\begin{split}&\big\langle{\hat{a}_{l},\hat{a}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|\bm{t}_{l}^{\dagger}(q;q,\mathrm{g})\big|{a_{l}}\big\rangle=t^{\hat{a}_{m\!+\!1}}_{\hat{a}_{l},a_{l}}\;,\\
&\big\langle{\hat{a}_{l},\hat{a}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|\bm{t}_{l}^{\dagger}(\bar{q};\bar{q},\mathrm{g})\big|{a_{l}}\big\rangle=-t^{\hat{a}_{m\!+\!1}}_{a_{l}\hat{a}_{l}}\;,\\
&\big\langle{\hat{a}_{l},\hat{a}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|\bm{t}_{l}^{\dagger}(\mathrm{g};\mathrm{g},\mathrm{g})\big|{a_{l}}\big\rangle={\mathrm{i}}f_{a_{l},\hat{a}_{l},\hat{a}_{m\!+\!1}}\;,\\
&\big\langle{\hat{a}_{l},\hat{a}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|\bm{t}_{l}^{\dagger}(\mathrm{g};q,\bar{q})\big|{a_{l}}\big\rangle=t^{a_{l}}_{\hat{a}_{l},\hat{a}_{m\!+\!1}}\;.\end{split}

(301)

Similarly for the bra amplitudes we have

\displaystyle\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|t_{l}(f_{l}\to\hat{f}_{l}+\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1})\big|{\{\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}

(302)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\{\hat{a}^{\prime}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\sum_{\{a^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{a^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\big\langle{\{\hat{a}^{\prime}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|{\{\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}

\displaystyle\times\big\langle{a^{\prime}_{l}}\big|\bm{t}_{l}(f_{l};\hat{f}_{l},\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1})\big|{\hat{a}^{\prime}_{l},\hat{a}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq l\end{subarray}}^{m}\delta_{\hat{a}^{\prime}_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}}\;,

where the single parton color operators are

\begin{split}&\big\langle{a^{\prime}_{l}}\big|\bm{t}_{l}(q;q,\mathrm{g})\big|{\hat{a}^{\prime}_{l},\hat{a}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle=t^{\hat{a}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}}_{a^{\prime}_{l},\hat{a}^{\prime}_{l}}\;,\\
&\big\langle{a^{\prime}_{l}}\big|\bm{t}_{l}(\bar{q};\bar{q},\mathrm{g})\big|{\hat{a}^{\prime}_{l},\hat{a}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle=-t^{\hat{a}_{m\!+\!1}}_{a^{\prime}_{l}\hat{a}^{\prime}_{l}}\;,\\
&\big\langle{a^{\prime}_{l}}\big|\bm{t}_{l}(\mathrm{g};\mathrm{g},\mathrm{g})\big|{\hat{a}^{\prime}_{l},\hat{a}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle={\mathrm{i}}f_{a^{\prime}_{l},\hat{a}^{\prime}_{l},\hat{a}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}}\;,\\
&\big\langle{a^{\prime}_{l}}\big|\bm{t}_{l}(\mathrm{g};q,\bar{q})\big|{\hat{a}^{\prime}_{l},\hat{a}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle=t^{a^{\prime}_{l}}_{\hat{a}^{\prime}_{l},\hat{a}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}}\;.\end{split}

(303)

For the factor that describes the parton spins, we define an operator,
\bm{c}(p_{l},f_{l};\hat{p}_{l},\hat{f}_{l};\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1})
. For
q\to q+\mathrm{g}
splitting, where
q
is a quark flavor and
\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1}=\mathrm{g}
, we start with

\begin{split}&\big\langle{\hat{s}_{l},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|\bm{c}(p_{l},q;\hat{p}_{l},q;\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\mathrm{g})\big|{s_{l}}\big\rangle\\
&\qquad=\overline{U}(\hat{p}_{l},\hat{s}_{l})\,{\raise-0.34723pt\hbox to4.66318pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\varepsilon$}}(\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1})^{*}\,\frac{{\raise-1.31944pt\hbox to4.8229pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$q$}}_{l}{\raise-0.34723pt\hbox to6.00235pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$n$}}U(p_{l},s_{l})}{2p_{l}\!\cdot\!n}\;,\end{split}

(304)

with
q_{l}=\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}
. We can simplify this expression. The collinear momentum mapping in Appendix
A
gives us

\begin{split}q_{l}^{\mu}={}&\left[1-\frac{q_{l}^{2}}{(2p_{l}\cdot n)^{2}}\right]p_{l}^{\mu}+\frac{q_{l}^{2}}{2p_{l}\cdot n}\,n^{\mu}\,.\end{split}

(305)

(In Appendix
A
,
q_{l}^{2}/(2p_{l}\cdot n)^{2}
is abbreviated as
a_{l}y
.) The factors in Eq. (
305
) follow from the choice that
q_{l}-p_{l}
is lightlike. Using
{\raise-1.31944pt\hbox to5.03125pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$p$}}_{l}U(p_{l},s_{l})=0
,
\{{\raise-1.31944pt\hbox to5.03125pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$p$}}_{l},{\raise-0.34723pt\hbox to6.00235pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$n$}}\}=2p_{l}\cdot n
, and
{\raise-0.34723pt\hbox to6.00235pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$n$}}{\raise-0.34723pt\hbox to6.00235pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$n$}}=1
, we have

\begin{split}{\raise-1.31944pt\hbox to7.89236pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$q_{l}$}}{\raise-0.34723pt\hbox to6.00235pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$n$}}\,U(p_{l},s_{l})={}&\left[1-\frac{q_{l}^{2}}{(2p_{l}\cdot n)^{2}}\right]2p_{l}\cdot n\,U(p_{l},s_{l})\\
&+\frac{q_{l}^{2}}{2p_{l}\cdot n}\,U(p_{l},s_{l})\\
={}&2p_{l}\cdot n\,U(p_{l},s_{l})\,.\end{split}

(306)

This gives

\begin{split}&\big\langle{\hat{s}_{l},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|\bm{c}(p_{l},q;\hat{p}_{l},q;\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\mathrm{g})\big|{s_{l}}\big\rangle\\
&\qquad=\overline{U}(\hat{p}_{l},\hat{s}_{l})\,{\raise-0.34723pt\hbox to4.66318pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\varepsilon$}}(\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1})^{*}U(p_{l},s_{l})\,.\end{split}

(307)

For the case
\bar{q}\to\bar{q}+\mathrm{g}
with
\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1}=\mathrm{g}
, an analogous derivation gives

\begin{split}&\big\langle{\hat{s}_{l},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|\bm{c}(p_{l},\bar{q};\hat{p}_{l},\bar{q};\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\mathrm{g})\big|{s_{l}}\big\rangle\\
&\qquad\qquad=\overline{V}(p_{l},s_{l})\,{\raise-0.34723pt\hbox to4.66318pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\varepsilon$}}(\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1})^{*}V(\hat{p}_{l},\hat{s}_{l})\,.\end{split}

(308)

For the case
\mathrm{g}\to q+\bar{q}
with
\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1}=\bar{q}
, we start with

\begin{split}&\big\langle{\hat{s}_{l},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|\bm{c}(p_{l},\mathrm{g};\hat{p}_{l},q;\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\bar{q})\big|{s_{l}}\big\rangle\\
&\quad=\overline{U}(\hat{p}_{l},\hat{s}_{l})\,\gamma^{\mu}V(\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1})N_{\mu\alpha}(q_{l})\,\varepsilon^{\alpha}(p_{l},s_{l})\,,\end{split}

(309)

where
N_{\mu\alpha}(q_{l})
is the numerator of the gluon propagator, Eq. (
7
). We note from Eq. (
305
) that
q_{l}
is a liner combination of
p_{l}
and
n
. In addition, the vector
\tilde{q}
in Eq. (
7
) is also a liner combination of
p_{l}
and
n
. Since
N_{\mu\alpha}(q_{l})
is contracted with
\varepsilon^{\alpha}(p_{l},s_{l})
, which is orthogonal to
p_{l}
and
n
, we find that

N_{\mu\alpha}(q_{l})\varepsilon^{\alpha}(p_{l},s_{l})=-\varepsilon_{\mu}(p_{l},s_{l})\;.

(310)

With this we have

\begin{split}&\big\langle{\hat{s}_{l},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|\bm{c}(p_{l},\mathrm{g};\hat{p}_{l},q;\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\bar{q})\big|{s_{l}}\big\rangle\\
&\qquad\qquad=-\overline{U}(\hat{p}_{l},\hat{s}_{l})\,{\raise-0.34723pt\hbox to4.66318pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\varepsilon$}}(p_{l},s_{l})V(\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1})\,.\end{split}

(311)

For a
\mathrm{g}\to\mathrm{g}+\mathrm{g}
splitting, we start with

\begin{split}&\big\langle{\hat{s}_{l},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|\bm{c}(p_{l},\mathrm{g};\hat{p}_{l},\mathrm{g};\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\mathrm{g})\big|{s_{l}}\big\rangle\\
&\quad=\varepsilon_{\alpha}(\hat{p}_{l},\hat{s}_{l})^{*}\,\varepsilon_{\beta}(\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1})^{*}\,N_{\mu\gamma}(q_{l})\varepsilon^{\gamma}(p_{l},s_{l})\\
&\qquad\times\Big[g^{\alpha\beta}\,q_{l}^{\mu}+g^{\mu\beta}\,(\hat{p}_{l}^{\alpha}-2\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}^{\alpha})\\
&\qquad\qquad+g^{\mu\alpha}\,(-2\hat{p}_{l}^{\beta}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}^{\beta})\Big]\,.\end{split}

(312)

Here we have included both parts of the
\mathrm{g}\to\mathrm{g}+\mathrm{g}
splitting function in Eq. (
128
) and have used
q_{l}^{\mu}=\hat{p}_{l}^{\mu}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}^{\mu}
. Now we can use Eq. (
310
) to replace
N_{\mu\alpha}(q_{l})\varepsilon^{\alpha}(p_{l},s_{l})
by
-\varepsilon_{\mu}(p_{l},s_{l})
. Then
q_{l}\cdot\varepsilon(p_{l},s_{l})=0
because, according to Eq. (
305
),
q_{l}
is a linear combination of
p_{l}
and
n
, which are both orthogonal to
\varepsilon(p_{l},s_{l})
. In addition,
\hat{p}_{l}\cdot\varepsilon(\hat{p}_{l},\hat{s}_{l})^{*}=0
and
\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}\cdot\varepsilon(\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1})^{*}=0
. This leaves

\begin{split}&\big\langle{\hat{s}_{l},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|\bm{c}(p_{l},\mathrm{g};\hat{p}_{l},\mathrm{g};\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\mathrm{g})\big|{s_{l}}\big\rangle\\
&\quad=-2\,\varepsilon_{\alpha}(\hat{p}_{l},\hat{s}_{l})^{*}\,\varepsilon_{\beta}(\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1})^{*}\,\varepsilon_{\mu}(p_{l},s_{l})\\
&\qquad\times\Big[g^{\mu\beta}\,\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}^{\alpha}+g^{\mu\alpha}\,\hat{p}_{l}^{\beta}\Big]\,.\end{split}

(313)

The bra amplitudes
\langle{s^{\prime}_{l}}|\bm{c}^{\dagger}|{\hat{s}^{\prime}_{l},\hat{s}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}}\rangle
can be derived with similar reasoning. The results are listed in Appendix
E
.

XII.3
Splitting with interference

\begin{split}\big({\{\bar{w},\bar{w}^{\prime}\}_{m+1},\{\bar{p}\}_{m+1}}\big|\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)\big|{\{w,w^{\prime}\}_{m},\{p\}_{m}}\big)\hskip-142.26378pt&\\
={}&\big({\{\bar{p}\}_{m+1}}\big|\mathcal{P}^{(1)}_{\rm soft}\big|{\{p\}_{m}}\big)\\
&\times 2\mu^{2}\,\delta\big(\mu^{2}-\max[h(q_{l}),h(q^{\prime}_{k})]\big)\\
&\times\hbox to150.39pt{\vbox to76.58pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.17387pt\lower-58.56297pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
