\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

Figure 7:
First order, topology 2
: interference diagram. Parton
m\!+\!1
must be a soft gluon, as in Sec.
VI
.

We can now use
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
to define

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)={}&\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\,\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)\,\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}\;,\end{split}

(314)

which is illustrated in Fig.
7
. The
\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)
operator is defined in Eq. (
209
) as the infrared approximation of the full interference operator
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{lk}(\mu)
. Applying the mapping operators
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}
and making straightforward algebraic
manipulations as in the collinear case, we find that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big){}

(315)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 8\pi^{2}\!\!\sum_{\{\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\!\!\delta_{\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1},\mathrm{g}}\,\prod_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{\hat{f}_{i},f_{i}}\int\!d\zeta_{\textsc{s}}\,

\displaystyle\times\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big)\,

\displaystyle\times 2\mu^{2}\,\delta\big(\mu^{2}-\max[h(q_{l}),h(q_{k})]\big)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq l\end{subarray}}^{m}\delta_{\hat{s}_{i},s_{i}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq k\end{subarray}}^{m}\delta_{\hat{s}^{\prime}_{j},s^{\prime}_{j}}\,

\displaystyle\times\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\hat{c}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big|t^{\dagger}(f_{l}\to f_{l}+\mathrm{g})\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\times\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|t(f_{k}\to f_{k}+\mathrm{g})\big|{\{\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}

\displaystyle\times\frac{\hat{p}_{l}\cdot\varepsilon(\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1})^{*}}{\hat{p}_{l}\cdot\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}}\,\delta_{\hat{s}_{l}s_{l}}

\displaystyle\times\frac{\hat{p}_{k}\cdot\varepsilon(\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{s}_{m\!+\!1})}{\hat{p}_{k}\cdot\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}}\,\delta_{\hat{s}^{\prime}_{k}s^{\prime}_{k}}\;,

with
q_{l}=\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}
and
q_{k}=\hat{p}_{k}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}
.

The result in Eq. (
315
) is similar to that in Eq. (
299
). Again, the spectator partons retain their flavors. Partons
l
and
k
also retain their flavors. The emitted parton carries the label
m+1
and is a gluon.

There is an integration over the splitting variables
\zeta_{\textsc{s}}
for the soft momentum mapping as described in Sec.
B
. This is simply an integration over
\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}
The splitting scale
\mu
is set according to
\mu^{2}=\max[h(\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}),h(\hat{p}_{k}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1})]
. The delta function setting
\mu
then restricts the integration over
\zeta_{\textsc{s}}
.

The momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{m\!+\!1}
of the partons after the splitting are given by
\zeta_{\textsc{s}}
and the momenta
\{p\}_{m}
before the splitting by the momentum mapping function given for the soft splitting in Eq. (
379
).

As in the collinear splitting, the momenta of the spectator partons are modified slightly by the momentum mapping. However, the spins of the spectator partons in the ket amplitude are unchanged,
\hat{s}_{i}=s_{i}
for
i\neq l
and the spins of the spectator partons in the bra amplitude are unchanged,
\hat{s}_{j}=s_{j}
for
j\neq k
.

As for the collinear splitting, there is a new color state, obtained by inserting the color matrix for the splitting
f_{l}\to f_{l}+\mathrm{g}
into the ket color state and inserting the color matrix for the splitting
f_{k}\to f_{k}+\mathrm{g}
into the bra color state.

In the final two lines of Eq. (
315
), there is the spin dependent part of the ket amplitude and the bra amplitude. We have extracted the infrared singular part,
\mathcal{E}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
, of
\mathcal{X}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
, so the splitting amplitudes are given by the eikonal approximation.

XII.4
Implementing probability conservation

We have seen how to construct the splitting operator
\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
that corresponds to one real emission. We now need the operator
\mathcal{S}^{(0,1)}(\mu)
that corresponds to first order virtual graphs. As explained in Sec.
XI.1
, if we start with
\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{s}}\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\mathbb{P}_{\textsc{h}}
, then
\big({1}\big|\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
will be free of infrared singularities, but will not equal 1 exactly. However, we can adjust the definition of
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
so that we obtain an infrared singular operator with exact probability conservation,

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=1\;.

(316)

To accomplish this, we define a linear mapping,
\mathcal{F}\to{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
, of operators on the statistical space
NSThrustSum
. The operator
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
leaves the number
m
of partons and
\{p,f\}_{m}
unchanged, but can be an operator on the color and spin variables. The operator
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
gives the same inclusive probability as
\mathcal{F}
:

\big({1}\big|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{F}\;.

(317)

In this section, we will see how to define
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
for the operators
\mathcal{F}
that occur at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
.

Following Sec.
XI.1
, we define
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
according to Eq. (
254
):

\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}^{-1}\;.

(318)

Up to first order, this is

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}

\displaystyle\Big\{1+\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})+\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(0,1)}\right)+\cdots\Big\}\Big\{1+\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\!\left({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(0,1)}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\right)\!+\cdots\Big\}^{-1}

(319)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 1+\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\Big(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}+\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(0,1)}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(0,1)}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\Big)+\cdots\;.

Here we have used the fact that
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(0,1)}
does not depend on
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
, although it does depend on
\mu_{\textsc{r}}
.

To proceed, we need to define
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
for the operators for the operators
\mathcal{F}
that are part of
\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
like, for instance,
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{lk}^{(1,0)}(\mu)
. There is some freedom available for this definition. We want the operator
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
to leave momenta and flavors unchanged. However it can be an operator on the color and spin space. A quite general operator with this structure takes the form

\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)={}&\sum_{\{\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{p,f,\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
&\times\sum_{i}\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\hat{c},\hat{s}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{F}_{i}^{\mathrm{ket}}(\{p,f\}_{m})\big|{\{c,s\}_{m}}\big\rangle\big\langle{\{c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{F}_{i}^{\mathrm{bra}}(\{p,f\}_{m})\big|{\{\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\;,\end{split}

(320)

with operators
\bm{F}_{i}^{\mathrm{ket}}
and
\bm{F}_{i}^{\mathrm{bra}}
to be defined.
The operators
\bm{F}^{\mathrm{ket}}_{i}
act on the ket color and spin space and the operators
\bm{F}^{\mathrm{bra}}_{i}
act on the bra color and spin space. Each of these operators depends on the parton momenta and flavors,
\{p,f\}_{m}
. The inclusive probability that corresponds to
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
is then, using Eq. (
25
),

\big({1}\big|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)=\sum_{i}\big\langle{\{c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{F}^{\mathrm{bra}}_{i}(\{p,f\}_{m})\bm{F}^{\mathrm{ket}}_{i}(\{p,f\}_{m})\big|{\{c,s\}_{m}}\big\rangle\;.

(321)

We see that only the product
\bm{F}^{\mathrm{bra}}_{i}\bm{F}^{\mathrm{ket}}_{i}
matters for the inclusive probability. Thus we could put the entire color
\times
spin dependence into a ket operator or into a bra operator or perhaps something else. At first order, we choose two contributions with
\bm{F}^{\mathrm{ket}}_{1}(\{p,f\}_{m})=\bm{F}^{\mathrm{bra}}_{2}(\{p,f\}_{m})=\bm{F}(\{p,f\}_{m})
and
\bm{F}^{\mathrm{ket}}_{2}(\{p,f\}_{m})=\bm{F}^{\mathrm{bra}}_{1}(\{p,f\}_{m})=(1/2)\,\bm{1}
:

\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)={}&\sum_{\{\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{p,f,\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
&\times\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\hat{c},\hat{s}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{F}(\{p,f\}_{m})\big|{\{c,s\}_{m}}\big\rangle\big\langle{\{c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{1}\big|{\{\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\hat{c},\hat{s}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{1}\big|{\{c,s\}_{m}}\big\rangle\big\langle{\{c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{F}(\{p,f\}_{m})\big|{\{\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\Big\}\;.\end{split}

(322)

That is

\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)={}&\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\{\hat{c},\hat{s}\}_{m}}\big|{\{p,f,\hat{c},c^{\prime},\hat{s},s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\hat{c},\hat{s}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{F}(\{p,f\}_{m})\big|{\{c,s\}_{m}}\big\rangle\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\{\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{p,f,c,\hat{c}^{\prime},s,\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\,\big\langle{\{c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{F}(\{p,f\}_{m})\big|{\{\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\;.\end{split}

(323)

We use Eq. (
82
) to insert unit operators into Eq. (
323
), giving

\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)={}&\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\{\hat{c},\tilde{c},\hat{s}\}_{m}}\big|{\{p,f,\hat{c},c^{\prime},\hat{s},s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\hat{c}\}_{m}}\big|{\{\tilde{c}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\,\big\langle{\{\tilde{c},\hat{s}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{F}(\{p,f\}_{m})\big|{\{c,s\}_{m}}\big\rangle\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\{\hat{c}^{\prime},\tilde{c}^{\prime},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{p,f,c,\hat{c}^{\prime},s,\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\big\langle{\{c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{F}(\{p,f\}_{m})\big|{\{\tilde{c}^{\prime},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\tilde{c}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\,.\end{split}

(324)

With the choice (
322
) for
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
, Eq. (
321
) becomes

\big({1}\big|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)=\big\langle{\{c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{F}(\{p,f\}_{m})\big|{\{c,s\}_{m}}\big\rangle\;.

(325)

We want
\big({1}\big|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{F}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)
, so we define the matrix element of
\bm{F}
by

\big\langle{\{c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{F}(\{p,f\}_{m})\big|{\{c,s\}_{m}}\big\rangle=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{F}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.

(326)

Then Eq. (
324
) gives us the definition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
:

\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{F}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)={}&\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\{\hat{c},\tilde{c},\hat{s}\}_{m}}\big|{\{p,f,\hat{c},c^{\prime},\hat{s},s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\,\big({1}\big|\mathcal{F}\big|{\{p,f,c,\tilde{c},s,\hat{s}\}_{m}}\big)\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\hat{c}\}_{m}}\big|{\{\tilde{c}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\{\hat{c}^{\prime},\tilde{c}^{\prime},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{p,f,c,\hat{c}^{\prime},s,\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\,\big({1}\big|\mathcal{F}\big|{\{p,f,\tilde{c}^{\prime},c^{\prime},\hat{s}^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\,\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\tilde{c}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\,\;.\end{split}

(327)

We will use this canonical form for
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(1,0)}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
. For
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(0,1)}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
, we simply take
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(0,1)}=\mathcal{G}^{(0,1)}
and obtain
\mathcal{G}^{(0,1)}
from
\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{(0,1)}
in Eq. (
281
). With this definition,
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(0,1)}
is already in the canonical form, so that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(0,1)}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}=\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(0,1)}\;.

(328)

This gives us a contribution to
\mathcal{D}
in Eq. (
319
),
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(0,1)}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(0,1)}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}=0
. This leaves us with

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}

\displaystyle 1+\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}{2\pi}\Big(\mathcal{D}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})+\mathcal{D}^{(0,1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\Big)+\cdots\;,

(329)

where
18
18

18

Note that
-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
is first order in
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
and does not create any new partons, so it counts as part of
\mathcal{D}^{(0,1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
. In fact, it is
all
of
\mathcal{D}^{(0,1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
.

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\;,\\
\mathcal{D}^{(0,1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\;.\end{split}

(330)

The first order splitting operators are then

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&\mu_{\textsc{s}}\,\frac{d\mathcal{D}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{d\mu_{\textsc{s}}}\;,\\
\mathcal{S}^{(0,1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&\mu_{\textsc{s}}\,\frac{d\mathcal{D}^{(0,1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{d\mu_{\textsc{s}}}\;.\end{split}

(331)

That is

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&\mu_{\textsc{s}}\,\frac{d\mathcal{A}_{0}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{d\mu_{\textsc{s}}}\;,\\
\mathcal{S}^{(0,1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})={}&-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\;.\end{split}

(332)

This gives us the splitting operators

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu)={}&\sum_{l}\mathcal{C}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l,k\\
k\neq l\end{subarray}}\mathcal{E}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)\end{split}

(333)

and

\mathcal{S}^{(0,1)}(\mu)=-\sum_{l}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{C}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l,k\\
k\neq l\end{subarray}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{E}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\;.

(334)

We are now ready to examine the operators that contribute to
\mathcal{S}^{(0,1)}(\mu)
.

We begin with
\mathcal{E}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)
. In order to be able to apply the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\cdots}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
operator we need the inclusive probability associated with
\mathcal{E}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)
. Using Eq. (
315
) for
\mathcal{E}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)
, we find

\displaystyle\big({1}\big|\mathcal{E}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)

\displaystyle\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)

(335)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle E(\mu,p_{l},p_{k})\,\big\langle{\{s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{s\}_{m}}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\times\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{T}_{k}\cdot\bm{T}_{l}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle\;,

where

\begin{split}E(\mu,p_{l},p_{k})={}&8\pi^{2}\int\!d\zeta_{\textsc{s}}\,\frac{\hat{p}_{l}\cdot P_{\perp}(\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1})\cdot\hat{p}_{k}}{\hat{p}_{l}\cdot\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}\ \hat{p}_{k}\cdot\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}}\,\\
&\times 2\mu^{2}\,\delta\!\left(\mu^{2}-\max\left[h(q_{l}),h(q_{k})\right]\right)\;,\end{split}

(336)

with
q_{l}=\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}
,
q_{k}=\hat{p}_{k}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}
, and

\begin{split}P_{\perp}^{\mu\nu}(p)={}&\sum_{s}\varepsilon^{\mu}(p,s)^{*}\varepsilon^{\nu}(p,s)\\
={}&-g^{\mu\nu}+\frac{p^{\mu}n^{\nu}+n^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p\cdot n}-\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{(p\cdot n)^{2}}\;.\end{split}

(337)

The integrand in Eq. (
336
) depends on the momenta
\hat{p}_{l}
,
\hat{p}_{k}
and
\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}
, which are related to the initial momenta
p_{l}
and
p_{k}
and to the splitting variables
\zeta_{\textsc{s}}
via the soft momentum mapping discussed in Appendix
B
. We note that
E(\mu,p_{l},p_{k})
contains the function defining the hardness scale
\mu
together with the amplitudes for emitting the soft gluon from parton
l
in the ket state and from parton
k
in the bra state, evaluated in the eikonal approximation.

We introduce a shorthand notation
\bm{T}_{k}\cdot\bm{T}_{l}
for the color operator describing emitting a gluon from line
l
and then absorbing this gluon on line
k
, summed over the color indices
a
of the gluon:

\begin{split}\big({1_{\mathrm{c}}}\big|t_{l}^{\dagger}(&f_{l}\to f_{l}+\mathrm{g})\otimes t_{k}(f_{k}\to f_{k}+\mathrm{g})\big|{\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
={}&\sum_{\{\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{m+1}}\big\langle{\{\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{m+1}}\big|{\{\hat{c}\}_{m+1}}\big\rangle\\
&\qquad\times\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|t_{k}(f_{k}\to f_{k}+\mathrm{g})\big|{\{\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{m+1}}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\\
&\qquad\times\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\hat{c}\}_{m+1}}\big|t_{l}^{\dagger}(f_{l}\!\to f_{l}+\mathrm{g})\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle\\
={}&\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|t_{k}(f_{k}\!\to\!f_{k}\!+\!\mathrm{g})\,t_{l}^{\dagger}(f_{l}\!\to\!f_{l}\!+\!\mathrm{g})\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle\\
={}&\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{T}_{k}\cdot\bm{T}_{l}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle\;.\end{split}

(338)

Here we have used the completeness relation of the color basis vectors.

Now we can construct the operator
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{E}_{lk}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
according to the definition (
327
):

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{E}^{(1,0)}_{lk}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)

(339)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle E(\mu,p_{l},p_{k})\,\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\{\tilde{c},\tilde{c}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{p,f,\tilde{c},\tilde{c}^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)

\displaystyle\times\Big\{\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\tilde{c}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{T}_{k}\cdot\bm{T}_{l}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{\tilde{c}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}

\displaystyle+\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\!\big\langle{\{\tilde{c}\}_{m}}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|\bm{T}_{k}\cdot\bm{T}_{l}\big|{\{\tilde{c}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\Big\}\;.

Note that in the first term, the matrix element
\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}|{\{\tilde{c}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\rangle\!\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}
sets
\{\tilde{c}^{\prime}\}_{m}=\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}
, while in the second term, the matrix element
{}_{\textsc{d}}\!\langle{\{\tilde{c}\}_{m}}|{\{c\}_{m}}\rangle
sets
\{\tilde{c}\}_{m}=\{c\}_{m}
. We see that the state obtained by applying
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{E}_{lk}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
has the same
m
partons with momenta, flavors and spins,
\{p,f,s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}
, as in the original state. However, the color state of the partons is changed.

We now consider
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{C}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
, beginning with Eq. (
299
) for
\mathcal{C}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)
. First we calculate the inclusive probability associated with
\mathcal{C}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)
:

\displaystyle\big({1}\big|\mathcal{C}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)

(340)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{1},f_{2}\\
f_{l}=f_{1}+f_{2}\end{subarray}}\big\langle{s^{\prime}_{l}}\big|\bm{P}_{f_{1},f_{2}}(p_{l},f_{l})\big|{s_{l}}\big\rangle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq l\end{subarray}}^{m}\delta_{s_{i},s^{\prime}_{i}}\,

\displaystyle\times\frac{\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|t_{l}(f_{l}\to f_{1}+f_{2})\,t_{l}^{\dagger}(f_{l}\to f_{1}+f_{2})\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle}{C(f_{1},f_{2})}\;.

The spins of the spectator partons are unchanged. We address the operator
\bm{P}_{f_{1},f_{2}}(p_{l},f_{l})
that acts on the spin space for parton
l
below. First, we examine the color operator.

The color factor in Eq. (
340
) can be simplified by using the definition of the color operator from Eqs. (
300
), (
301
), (
302
), and (
303
):

\displaystyle\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|t_{l}(f_{l}\to f_{1}+f_{2})\,t^{\dagger}_{l}(f_{l}\to f_{1}+f_{2})\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle

(341)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\{a,a^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{a^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\big\langle{\{a\}_{m}}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\times\big\langle{a^{\prime}_{l}}\big|\bm{t}_{l}(f_{l};f_{1},f_{2})\bm{t}_{l}^{\dagger}(f_{l};f_{1},f_{2})\big|{a_{l}}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq l\end{subarray}}^{m}\delta_{a^{\prime}_{i},a_{i}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle C(f_{1},f_{2})\,\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle\;,

where the flavor dependent color constant
C(f_{1},f_{2})
is

\begin{split}&C(f_{1},f_{2})=\begin{cases}C_{\mathrm{F}}\quad&f_{1}\neq\mathrm{g}\;\&\;f_{2}=\mathrm{g}\;,\\
C_{\mathrm{F}}\quad&f_{1}=\mathrm{g}\;\&\;f_{2}\neq\mathrm{g}\;,\\
C_{\mathrm{A}}\quad&f_{1}=f_{2}=\mathrm{g}\;,\\
T_{\mathrm{R}}\quad&\hat{f}_{1}=-f_{2}\neq\mathrm{g}\;.\end{cases}\end{split}

(342)

This is the usual color structure for a collinear emission.

Now, we examine the operator
\bm{P}_{f_{1},f_{2}}(p_{l},f_{l})
, which is defined by

\begin{split}\big\langle{s^{\prime}}\big|\bm{P}_{f_{1},f_{2}}(\mu,p_{l},f_{l})\big|{s}\big\rangle\hskip-56.9055pt&\\
={}&8\pi^{2}C(f_{1},f_{2})\int d\zeta_{\textsc{c}}\,\big[(\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1})^{2}\big]^{-1}\\
&\times\big\langle{s^{\prime}_{l}}\big|\bm{c}^{\dagger}(p_{l},f_{l};\hat{p}_{l},\hat{f}_{l};\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1})\\
&\qquad\times\bm{c}(p_{l},f_{l};\hat{p}_{l},\hat{f}_{l};\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{f}_{m\!+\!1})\big|{s_{l}}\big\rangle\;.\end{split}

(343)

There is an integration over the collinear splitting variables,
\zeta_{\textsc{c}}
. The momenta
\hat{p}_{l}
,
\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}
are related to the momentum of the emitter parton,
p_{l}
and the splitting variables by the collinear momentum mapping described in Appendix
A
. The operators
\bm{c}
depend on the parton flavors involved and are defined in Eqs. (
307
), (
308
), (
309
), and (
312
) in Sec.
XII.2
.

The expression in Eq. (
343
) has a simple spin dependence. After integrating over the splitting variables
\zeta_{\textsc{c}}
, the spin matrix is invariant under rotations about the
\vec{p}_{l}
axis. For this reason, it is proportional to the unit matrix in the spin space and can be obtained by averaging over the spin variables. We define a splitting probability function summed over the daughter parton flavors as

P(\mu,p_{l},f_{l})=\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{1},f_{2}\\
f_{l}=f_{1}+f_{2}\end{subarray}}\!\!\sum_{s_{l}}\frac{\big\langle{s_{l}}\big|\bm{P}_{f_{1},f_{2}}(\mu,p_{l},f_{l})\big|{s_{l}}\big\rangle}{n_{\mathrm{s}}(f_{l})}\,,

(344)

where
n_{\mathrm{s}}(f)
is the number of the spin states: for fermions
n_{\mathrm{s}}(q)=n_{\mathrm{s}}(\bar{q})=2
and for gluons
n_{\mathrm{s}}(\mathrm{g})=d-2
.

With this, the inclusive probability associated with
\mathcal{C}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)
is

\displaystyle\big({1}\big|\mathcal{C}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)

(345)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle P(\mu,p_{l},f_{l})\,\big\langle{\{c^{\prime},s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{c,s\}_{m}}\big\rangle\;.

Given this result for
\big({1}\big|\mathcal{C}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)
, we can use Eq. (
327
) to obtain
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{C}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{C}^{(1,0)}_{l}(\mu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{P}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)

(346)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle P(\mu,p_{l},f_{l})\,\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.

This operator leaves the flavors, colors, and spins of the spectator partons and also the active parton
l
unchanged. It does depend on the flavor
f_{l}
of parton
l
.

XIII
Conclusions and outlook

Our aim in this paper has been to provide a formalism in which one can define the evolution operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu)
for a parton shower for
e^{+}e^{-}
annihilation at second order in
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
or beyond.

To construct
\mathcal{S}(\mu)
at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}
, we need the infrared singular operator
\mathcal{D}(\mu)

NSAllOrder
at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}
. Since
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu)
provides the subtraction of infrared singularities from a perturbative calculation of a cross section, constructing
\mathcal{D}(\mu)
at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}
is similar in complexity to constructing a fixed order cross section at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}
beyond the Born level. This has proven to be a problem of some difficulty. Thus the formalism that we have presented is necessarily rather complex. It is perhaps useful here to review a few of the most important features of this formalism.

For a state of many partons, we define a scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
that measures how well separated the parton momenta are. That is,
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
is small when any two partons are nearly collinear or when any parton momentum is very soft. We regard parton shower evolution as a renormalization group evolution that tells how a vector
|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{s}})})
that represents the state of the system evolves as the scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
of the partons decreases from a very large value associated with the hard process. This evolution is controlled by the infrared singularities of QCD.

The formalism defines a
quantum shower
evolution in the sense that
|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{s}})})
is a vector in a space, the
statistical space
, described by momenta and flavors for the partons and by colors and spins for the partons in the ket amplitude and separate colors and spins for the partons in the conjugate bra amplitude. In this way, the shower evolution can match the infrared singularities of Feynman graphs for the amplitude and for the conjugate amplitude, including quantum interference.

It is usual to define the amplitudes needed for shower evolution using Feynman gauge. However, this leads to complications when one tries to go beyond leading order for the splitting operators. Instead, we use interpolating gauge
Doust
;
BaulieuZwanziger
;
Gauge
, which simplifies the infrared singularities of QCD. Indeed, the use of interpolating gauge simplifies the treatment of infrared singularities from interference graphs even at first order.

To go beyond first order for the evolution operators, we describe Feynman diagrams for the ket and bra amplitudes in a precise way that allows for the use of linear algebra to organize the factors in the diagrams. For this purpose, we introduce a bigger space than the statistical space, the
extended statistical space
. In the extended statistical space, parton momenta are generally off shell. Furthermore, a contribution from a Feynman graph for the ket amplitude can multiply a contribution to the conjugate bra amplitude from a different Feynman graph. Thus evolution in the extended statistical space includes quantum interference in momentum and flavor as well as color and spin.

The extended statistical space also includes information that allows a momentum mapping from
m
on-shell momenta to
\hat{m}
on-shell momenta after splittings while preserving exact momentum conservation.

Based on what we anticipate is the general structure of the operators
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
that describe graphs in the extended statistical space, we find that the graphs factor into a hard factor with scales greater than a separation scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
and an infrared singular operator
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
with scales smaller than
\mu_{\textsc{s}}
.

Once we have
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
at a certain perturbative order, we map it into an operator
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
that operates on the statistical space. Then the evolution operator in the statistical space is
\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
defined by
\mu_{\textsc{s}}\,d\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})/d\mu_{\textsc{s}}=\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\,\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
, evaluated at the available perturbative order. The definition is arranged so that the splitting operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
conserves probability: the total probability for partons to split into more partons plus the probability for the partons not to split equals 1.

We have seen how this formalism works to define the evolution operators
\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
for first order parton emission and
\mathcal{S}^{(0,1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
for first order virtual graphs. With probability conservation,
\mathcal{S}^{(0,1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
is defined by integrating
\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
over the splitting variables.

We leave for future work the operators
\mathcal{S}^{(2,0)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
for two real parton emissions,
\mathcal{S}^{(1,1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
for one real parton emission and a first order virtual graph, and
\mathcal{S}^{(0,2)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
for second order virtual graphs.

Knowing the evolution operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
gives us an evolution equation in the statistical space. This does not give us a practical parton shower event generator, any more than knowing the Schrödinger equation for molecules made from nuclei and electrons gives us directly a practical method for calculating the energy levels of these molecules. Rather, once we have the evolution equation with an evolution operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
, we need to devise an approximate evolution operator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{approx}}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
that is simple enough that it can be implemented in a parton shower event generator. One can then define

\Delta\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})=\mathcal{S}(\mu)-\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{approx}}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})\;.

(347)

Then one can check whether including just a few powers of
\Delta\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
in the shower makes a substantial difference in the results for an observable of interest.

This has been the strategy in the first order parton shower event generator
Deductor

Deductor
. Here spin is simply neglected by averaging over spins, but some approximation is needed for color. The operator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{approx}}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
is obtained by making an approximation denoted as the LC+ approximation
NScolor
. This is plausibly a very good approximation because it is exact for collinear emissions and for collinear
\times
soft emissions. Note that the statement that the LC+ approximation is exact for collinear emissions and for collinear
\times
soft emissions depends on knowing the exact
\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
. We can then test the numerical effect of including a few powers of
\Delta\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
in the shower
NSNewColor
.

For the first order splitting operator
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
constructed in this paper, we would anticipate using
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{approx}}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
based on the LC+ approximation. We leave finding an approximation for second order for future work. One possibility is to define
\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{approx}}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
to be zero, so that the entire
\mathcal{S}^{(2)}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
is treated as part of
\Delta\mathcal{S}(\mu_{\textsc{s}})
. One could then test the effect of including a few second order splittings in the shower.

One of us (DES) thanks the theory group of DESY Laboratory, Hamburg for its hospitality during some of the work on this paper and also thanks the Mainz Institute for Theoretical Physics for its hospitality during some of this work.

Appendix A
Collinear momentum mapping

In order to describe a splitting of parton
l
in the ket state and the same parton in the bra state into two partons, we need a momentum mapping operator
\bm{R}_{\mathrm{coll}}^{(1)}(l)
that maps the momenta
\{p\}_{m}
to new momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{m\!+\!1}
, which are functions of the
m
initial momenta
p_{i}
and splitting variables
\zeta_{\textsc{c}}
, as in Eq. (
184
). We will also need a momentum mapping
\bm{R}_{\mathrm{coll}}^{(2)}(l)
for the splitting of one parton into three partons.

Consider first the collinear splitting of Fig.
6
. We start with the momentum
p_{l}
of the parton that splits, with
p_{l}^{2}=0
. We use an auxiliary lightlike vector
n_{l}
in the plane of
p_{l}
and the total momentum
Q
:

n_{l}=\frac{2p_{l}\cdot Q\,Q-Q^{2}\,p_{l}}{Q^{2}}\;.

(348)

Then

Q=a_{l}(p_{l}+n_{l})\;,

(349)

where

a_{l}=\frac{Q^{2}}{2p_{l}\cdot Q}\;.

(350)

Parton
l
splits into partons
l
and
m\!+\!1
with momenta parameterized by splitting parameters
z
and
k_{\perp}
, where the vector
k_{\perp}
is orthogonal to both
p_{l}
and
n_{l}
:

\begin{split}\hat{p}_{l}={}&zp_{l}+\frac{\bm{k}^{2}}{2z\,p_{l}\cdot n_{l}}\,n_{l}+k_{\perp}\;,\\
\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}={}&(1-z)p_{l}+\frac{\bm{k}^{2}}{2(1-z)\,p_{l}\cdot n_{l}}\,n_{l}-k_{\perp}\;.\end{split}

(351)

Considering
k_{\perp}
to be a vector
\bm{k}
in
2-2\epsilon
dimensions, we have
k_{\perp}^{2}=-\bm{k}^{2}
. We define

P=\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}\;.

(352)

Then
P^{2}>0
. It is useful to define a dimensionless measure
y
of the virtuality in the splitting by

y=\frac{P^{2}}{2p_{l}\cdot Q}=\frac{P^{2}}{a_{l}2p_{l}\cdot n_{l}}\;.

(353)

Using
y
, we can write
P
as

P=p_{l}+a_{l}y\,n_{l}\;.

(354)

The virtuality measure
y
is related to the splitting variables by

a_{l}y=\frac{\bm{k}^{2}}{z(1-z)2p_{l}\cdot n_{l}}\;.

(355)

Denote the momenta of the remaining partons after the splitting by
\hat{p}_{i}
. For a splitting with
y=0
, we have
P=p_{l}
. Then we can let
\hat{p}_{i}=p_{i}
for with
1\leq i\leq m
and
i\neq l
. However, when
y>0
we need to map
p_{i}
for the remaining partons to new momenta
\hat{p}_{i}
with
\hat{p}_{i}^{2}=0
and

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq l\end{subarray}}^{m}\hat{p}_{i}+\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq l\end{subarray}}^{m}p_{i}+p_{l}\;.

(356)

This can be accomplished quite simply by letting
\hat{p}_{i}
be related to
p_{i}
by a scaling times a Lorentz transformation
19
19

19

In a first order dipole shower, one sometimes takes the momentum
P-p_{l}
from a single dipole partner parton. Here, as in
Deductor
, we take the needed momentum from
all
of the remaining partons, so that the fractional change in the momentum of any one parton is small.

\hat{p}_{i}^{\mu}=\sqrt{1-y}\,\Lambda^{\mu}_{\nu}p_{i}^{\nu}\;.

(357)

The Lorentz transformation is a boost in the
p_{l}
-
n_{l}
plane. The transformation is most simply stated in component form. Let

p_{i}=\frac{p_{i}\cdot n_{l}}{p_{l}\cdot n_{l}}\,p_{l}+\frac{p_{i}\cdot p_{l}}{p_{l}\cdot n_{l}}\,n_{l}+p_{i\perp}\;.

(358)

with
p_{i\perp}\cdot p_{l}=p_{i\perp}\cdot n_{l}=0
. Then define

\hat{p}_{i}=\frac{p_{i}\cdot n_{l}}{p_{l}\cdot n_{l}}\,p_{l}+(1-y)\frac{p_{i}\cdot p_{l}}{p_{l}\cdot n_{l}}\,n_{l}+\sqrt{1-y}\,p_{i\perp}\;.

(359)

We leave the component of
p_{i}
along
p_{l}
unchanged but scale the component along
n_{l}
and also the transverse components so as to leave
p_{i}^{2}
unchanged. Using

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq l\end{subarray}}^{m}p_{i}=Q-p_{l}=(a_{l}-1)p_{l}+a_{l}n_{l}\;,

(360)

we recover the momentum conservation equation (
356
):

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq l\end{subarray}}^{m}(\hat{p}_{i}-p_{i})=-a_{l}y\,n_{l}=-(P-p_{l})\;.

(361)

The splitting variables for the collinear splitting are
\zeta_{\textsc{c}}=\{z,\bm{k}\}
. We define a measure for integration over the splitting variables by

d\zeta_{\textsc{c}}=J_{\textsc{c}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3-2\epsilon}}\frac{dz}{2z(1-z)}\,d^{2-2\epsilon}d\bm{k}\;.

(362)

Here
J_{\textsc{c}}
is an additional Jacobian factor to be specified.

We integrate over the momenta before the splitting using the measure
\{dp\}_{m}
in Eq. (
22
). We integrate over the momenta after the splitting using the measure
\{d\hat{p}\}_{m\!+\!1}
for
m+1
partons. This should match the measure of integrating over the momenta before the splitting together with integrating over the splitting variables, as in Eq. (4.37) of Ref.
NSI
and Eq. (
182
):

\{d\hat{p}\}_{m\!+\!1}=\{dp\}_{m}\,d\zeta_{\textsc{c}}\;.

(363)

Enforcing this matching gives us, after some calculation,

J_{\textsc{c}}=(1-a_{l}y)^{2-2\epsilon}(1-y)^{m-3-(m-2)\epsilon}\;.

(364)

Of course, we could choose any functions of
z
and
\bm{k}
as alternative splitting variables
\zeta_{\textsc{c}}

NSI
. Then we would use an alternative Jacobian
J_{c}
obtained by multiplying
J_{c}
given in Eq. (
364
) by the Jacobian for the change to the new splitting variables.

The action of the momentum mapping operator
\bm{R}_{\mathrm{coll}}^{(1)}(l)
in Eq. (
184
) for the first order collinear splitting can now be specified as

\bm{R}_{\mathrm{coll}}^{(1)}(l)\big|{\{p\}_{m}}\big)=\int\!d\zeta_{\textsc{c}}\ \big|{\{\hat{p}\}_{m\!+\!1}}\big)\;,

(365)

where
\{\hat{p}\}_{m\!+\!1}
is given in Eqs. (
351
) and (
357
) and
d\zeta_{\textsc{c}}
is given in Eq. (
362
).

We will also need a momentum mapping for a
1\to 3
parton splitting. We can follow rather closely the construction above for a
1\to 2
splitting. We use splitting variables
\zeta_{\mathrm{c},3}=\{z_{1},z_{2},\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2}\}
with

\begin{split}\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}={}&z_{1}p_{l}+\frac{\bm{k}_{1}^{2}}{2z_{l}\,p_{l}\cdot n_{l}}\,n_{l}+k_{1,\perp}\;,\\
\hat{p}_{m\!+\!2}={}&z_{2}p_{l}+\frac{\bm{k}_{2}^{2}}{2z_{2}\,p_{l}\cdot n_{l}}\,n_{l}+k_{2,\perp}\;,\\
\hat{p}_{l}={}&z_{l}p_{l}+\frac{\bm{k}_{l}^{2}}{2z_{l}\,p_{l}\cdot n_{l}}\,n_{l}+k_{l,\perp}\;,\end{split}

(366)

where

\begin{split}z_{l}={}&1-z_{1}-z_{2}\;,\\
k_{l,\perp}={}&-k_{1,\perp}-k_{2,\perp}\;.\end{split}

(367)

The remaining momenta need to be defined so that momentum conservation is maintained:

\sum_{i=1}^{m\!+\!2}\hat{p}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\;.

(368)

This can be achieved with the definition of
\hat{p}_{i}
with
i\leq m,i\neq l
,

\hat{p}_{i}^{\mu}=\sqrt{1-y_{3}}\,\Lambda^{\mu}_{\nu}p_{i}^{\nu}\;,

(369)

where

y_{3}=\frac{(\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!2})^{2}}{2p_{l}\cdot Q}

(370)

and
\Lambda^{\mu}_{\nu}
is the Lorentz transformation defined by

Q^{\mu}-\hat{p}_{l}^{\mu}-\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}^{\mu}-\hat{p}_{m\!+\!2}^{\mu}=\sqrt{1-y_{3}}\,\Lambda^{\mu}_{\nu}(Q^{\nu}-p_{l}^{\nu})\;.

(371)

This Lorentz transformation is
NSI

\begin{split}\Lambda^{\mu}_{\,\nu}={}&g^{\mu}_{\,\nu}-\frac{2(\hat{K}+K)^{\mu}(\hat{K}+K)_{\nu}}{(\hat{K}+K)^{2}}+\frac{2\hat{K}^{\mu}K_{\nu}}{K^{2}}\;,\end{split}

(372)

where

\begin{split}K^{\mu}={}&\sqrt{1-y_{3}}\,(Q^{\nu}-p_{l}^{\nu})\;,\\
\hat{K}^{\mu}={}&Q^{\mu}-\hat{p}_{l}^{\mu}-\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}^{\mu}-\hat{p}_{m\!+\!2}^{\mu}\;.\end{split}

(373)

To integrate over the splitting variables, we define a measure
d\zeta_{\mathrm{c},3}
such that
\{d\hat{p}\}_{m\!+\!2}=\{dp\}_{m}\,d\zeta_{\mathrm{c},3}
:

d\zeta_{\mathrm{c},3}=\frac{J_{\textsc{c}}}{(2\pi)^{6-4\epsilon}}\frac{dz_{1}\,dz_{2}}{4z_{1}z_{2}(1-z_{1}-z_{2})}\,d^{2-2\epsilon}\bm{k}_{1}\,d^{2-2\epsilon}\bm{k}_{2}\;.

(374)

We find that the Jacobian here is
J_{\textsc{c}}
as given in Eq. (
364
).

We can now define a momentum mapping operator
\bm{R}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{coll}}(l)
for the second order collinear splitting as

\bm{R}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{coll}}(l)\big|{\{p\}_{m}}\big)=\int\!d\zeta_{\mathrm{c},3}\ \big|{\{\hat{p}\}_{m\!+\!2}}\big)\;,

(375)

where
\{\hat{p}\}_{m\!+\!2}
is given in Eqs. (
366
), (
367
) and (
369
) and
d\zeta_{\mathrm{c},3}
is given in Eq. (
374
).

Appendix B
Soft momentum mapping

Consider the interference diagram of Fig.
7
. The momentum mapping needed here is simple because there is no collinear singularity, only a soft singularity when
\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}\to 0
. We can use the components of
\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}
as the splitting variables
\zeta_{\textsc{s}}
for the soft splitting.

In fact, we can easily consider a slightly more general case, in which several soft partons are emitted. Let the momenta of the soft partons be
\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\dots,\hat{p}_{m\!+\!n}
. Then we can write the measure for integrating over the splitting variables as

d\zeta_{\textsc{s}}=\prod_{J=1}^{n}\left\{\frac{d^{4-2\epsilon}p_{m\!+\!J}}{(2\pi)^{4-2\epsilon}}(2\pi)\,\delta_{+}(p_{m\!+\!J}^{2})\right\}J_{\textsc{s}}\;,

(376)

with a Jacobian factor
J_{\textsc{s}}
to be specified. Define

q=\sum_{J=1}^{n}p_{m\!+\!J}\;.

(377)

We need to transform the remaining momenta so as to maintain momentum conservation

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{p}_{i}+q=\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\;.

(378)

This can be accomplished quite simply by letting
\hat{p}_{i}
for
i\leq m
be related to
p_{i}
by a scaling times a Lorentz transformation in the
Q,q
plane:

\hat{p}_{i}^{\mu}=\sqrt{1-\alpha}\,\Lambda^{\mu}_{\,\nu}p_{i}^{\nu}\;.

(379)

Then momentum conservation yields

Q^{\mu}-q^{\mu}=\sqrt{1-\alpha}\,\Lambda^{\mu}_{\,\nu}Q^{\nu}\;.

(380)

Since the Lorentz transformation preserves squares of vectors, this requires

(Q-q)^{2}=(1-\alpha)Q^{2}\;.

(381)

This gives

\alpha=\frac{2Q\cdot q-q^{2}}{Q^{2}}\;.

(382)

Then the Lorentz transformation is given by Eq. (
372
), with

\begin{split}K^{\mu}={}&\sqrt{1-\alpha}\,Q^{\mu}\;,\\
\hat{K}^{\mu}={}&Q^{\mu}-q^{\mu}\;.\end{split}

(383)

We require

\{d\hat{p}\}_{m\!+\!n}=\{dp\}_{m}\,d\zeta_{\textsc{s}}\;.

(384)

A simple calculation shows that this relation holds if

J_{\textsc{s}}=(1-\alpha)^{m-2-(m-1)\epsilon}\;.

(385)

At first order,
q=\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}
is lightlike. Then the mapping is quite simple. The value of
\alpha
is

\alpha=\frac{2Q\cdot\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}}{Q^{2}}\;.

(386)

Define a vector
u
by

\alpha\,Q=u+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}\;.

(387)

Then using Eq. (
386
), we see that
u^{2}=0
. The Lorentz transformation is defined by

Q^{\mu}-\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}^{\mu}=\sqrt{1-\alpha}\,\Lambda^{\mu}_{\nu}Q^{\nu}\;.

(388)

Multiplying by
\alpha
and using Eq. (
387
), we have

u^{\mu}+(1-\alpha)\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}^{\mu}=\sqrt{1-\alpha}\,\Lambda^{\mu}_{\nu}(u^{\nu}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}^{\mu})\;.

(389)

If we call the boost angle
\omega
, this is

u^{\mu}+(1-\alpha)\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}^{\mu}=\sqrt{1-\alpha}\,(e^{-\omega}u^{\mu}+e^{\omega}\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}^{\mu})\;.

(390)

We thus identify the boost factor as

e^{\omega}=\sqrt{1-\alpha}\;.

(391)

We see that as long as the emitted gluon is soft, the parton momenta
p_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq m
undergo a small scaling and a small boost in the
Q
-
\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}
plane.

The action of the momentum mapping operator
\bm{R}_{\mathrm{soft}}^{(n)}
in Eq. (
184
) for
n
soft parton emissions can now be specified as

\bm{R}_{\mathrm{soft}}^{(n)}\big|{\{p\}_{m}}\big)=\int\!d\zeta_{\textsc{s}}\ \big|{\{\hat{p}\}_{m\!+\!n}}\big)\;,

(392)

where
\{\hat{p}\}_{m\!+\!n}
is given by Eq. (
379
) and the choice of the components of
\hat{p}_{m\!+\!J}
as the splitting variables
\zeta_{\textsc{s}}
and where
d\zeta_{\textsc{s}}
is given in Eq. (
376
).

Appendix C
Momentum mapping operator

We have specified momentum mapping functions
R
corresponding to graphs
G
that map parton momenta
\{p\}_{m}
for
m
partons together with splitting parameters
\zeta_{G}
into parton momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}
for more partons,

\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}=R(G;\zeta_{G},\{p\}_{m})\;.

(393)

In this paper
\hat{m}=m+1
or
\hat{m}=m+2
.

One can choose the splitting parameters to be

\zeta_{G}=\{p_{m\!+\!1},\dots,p_{\hat{m}}\}\;,

(394)

as in Appendix
B
, although this is sometimes not the most useful choice, as in Appendix
A
. To integrate over the splitting parameters we use a measure
d\zeta_{G}
that includes an appropriate Jacobian such that

d\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}=d\zeta_{G}\ d\{p\}_{m}\;.

(395)

The needed functions
R
and the measure
d\zeta
were defined in Appendices
A
and
B
.

In the extended statistical space, we define a space that specifies just on-shell parton momenta, considered as jet momenta, with basis vectors
\big|{\{p\}_{m}}\big)
. We define the completeness relation for these basis vectors to be

1=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\int\!d\{p\}_{m}\ \big|{\{p\}_{m}}\big)\big({\{p\}_{m}}\big|\;.

(396)

We have defined an operator
\bm{R}(G)
that maps a basis vector in the
m
parton space to the a linear combination of vectors in the
\hat{m}
parton space.
20
20

20

The notation
\mathcal{P}(G)
was used for an operator similar to
\bm{R}(G)
for first order splittings in Ref.
NSI
. The operator
\mathcal{P}(G)
included flavor. If we simply let all partons be gluons so that flavor plays no role, then the definition was
\mathcal{P}\big|{\{p\}_{m}}\big)=[1/(m+1)]\bm{R}\big|{\{p\}_{m}}\big)
.
This operator was defined briefly in Sec.
VIII.2
and in Appendices
A
and
B
. In this appendix, we provide a justification for the definition, paying particular attention to the choice of normalization.

Let
\big|{\rho}\big)
be a vector in the
m
parton space. Define

\rho(\{p\}_{m})=\big({\{p\}_{m}}\big|{\rho}\big)\;.

(397)

The total probability associated with
\big|{\rho}\big)
is
({1_{\mathrm{p}}}|{\rho})
where
({1_{\mathrm{p}}}|{\{p\}_{m}})
= 1, as in Eq. (
25
), If we normalize the total probability to 1, we have

\begin{split}1={}&\big({1_{\mathrm{p}}}\big|{\rho}\big)\\
={}&\frac{1}{m!}\int\!d\{p\}_{m}\ \big({1}\big|{\{p\}_{m}}\big)\big({\{p\}_{m}}\big|{\rho}\big)\\
={}&\int\!d\{p\}_{m}\ \frac{\rho(\{p\}_{m})}{m!}\;.\end{split}

(398)

This tells us that
\rho(\{p\}_{m})/m!
is to be interpreted as the probability density for parton 1 to have momentum
p_{1}
, parton 2 to have momentum
p_{2}
, …, and parton
m
to have momentum
p_{m}
.

Given
\rho(\{p\}_{m})
, we can define a symmetrized version by summing over permutations
\pi
of the indices:

\rho_{\textsc{s}}(\{p\}_{m})=\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\pi\in S_{m}}\rho(\{p_{\pi}\}_{m})\;.

(399)

Since the partons are identical particles, the symmetric version is the one that has a physical meaning independent of labeling. Its normalization is

\begin{split}\frac{1}{m!}\int\!d\{p\}_{m}&\ \rho_{\textsc{s}}(\{p\}_{m})\\
={}&\frac{1}{m!}\int\!d\{p\}_{m}\ \frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\pi\in S_{m}}\rho(\{p_{\pi}\}_{m})\\
={}&\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\pi\in S_{m}}\int\!d\{p\}_{m}\ \frac{\rho(\{p_{\pi}\}_{m})}{m!}\\
={}&\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\pi\in S_{m}}\int\!d\{p_{\pi}\}_{m}\ \frac{\rho(\{p_{\pi}\}_{m})}{m!}\\
={}&\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\pi\in S_{m}}\int\!d\{p\}_{m}\ \frac{\rho(\{p\}_{m})}{m!}\\
={}&\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\pi\in S_{m}}1\\
={}&1\;.\end{split}

(400)

This is the expected normalization condition, interpreted as meaning that
\int\!d\{p\}_{m}
counts the same physical state
m!
times.

We now need an operator that represents the momentum mapping. As in Eq. (
184
), we define

\bm{R}(G)\big|{\{p\}_{m}}\big)=\int\!d\zeta_{G}\ \big|{R(G;\zeta_{G},\{p\}_{m})}\big)\;.

(401)

This clearly maps the momenta into the right place according to the given momentum mapping function
R(G;\zeta_{G},\{p\}_{m})
. However, it is not immediately clear if the normalization is right so as to conserve probability. We now investigate this question.

Let
|{\hat{\rho}})
be the state vector that we get by applying
\bm{R}(G)
to a vector
|{\rho})
with
m
partons:

\big|{\hat{\rho}}\big)=\bm{R}(G)\big|{\rho}\big)\;.

(402)

Note that only
\bm{R}(G)
, with no Feynman graph factors, is applied to
\big|{\rho}\big)
here. Then, using
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}=R(G;\zeta_{G},\{p\}_{m})
,

\begin{split}\big|{\hat{\rho}}\big)={}&\frac{1}{m!}\int\!d\{p\}_{m}\ \bm{R}(G)\big|{\{p\}_{m}}\big)\big({\{p\}_{m}}\big|{\rho}\big)\\
={}&\frac{1}{m!}\int\!d\{p\}_{m}\int\!d\zeta_{G}\ \big|{\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big)\big({\{p\}_{m}}\big|{\rho}\big)\\
={}&\frac{1}{m!}\int\!d\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}\ \big|{\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big)\big({\{p\}_{m}}\big|{\rho}\big)\\
={}&\int\!d\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}\ \big|{\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big)\frac{\rho(\{p\}_{m})}{m!}\;.\end{split}

(403)

We also have

\begin{split}\big|{\hat{\rho}}\big)={}&\frac{1}{\hat{m}!}\int\!d\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}\ \big|{\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big)\,\hat{\rho}(\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}})\;.\end{split}

(404)

Comparing these, we have

\frac{\hat{\rho}(\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}})}{\hat{m}!}=\frac{\rho(\{p\}_{m})}{m!}\;.

(405)

We see that the normalization according to Eq. (
398
) and its analogue for
\{\hat{p}\}_{\hat{m}}
is preserved.

Appendix D
Hardness inequality

In this section, we provide a proof that the inequalities (
173
) hold for the hardness function
h(q)
defined in Eq. (
170
).

Consider a splitting of parton
l
to partons
i
and
j
for partons with
q_{l}=\bar{q}_{i}+\bar{q}_{j}
and with
\bar{q}_{i}^{2}\geq 0
,
\bar{q}_{i}\cdot Q>0
and
\bar{q}_{j}^{2}\geq 0
,
\bar{q}_{j}^{2}\cdot Q>0
. We pick a lightlike vector
n
. Then we choose a reference frame in which
\vec{Q}=0
and
\vec{n}
lies along the
-z
axis. In this frame,
(n^{+},n^{-},\bm{n}_{\perp})=(0,n^{-},\bm{0})
. We let the vectors have components in this reference frame

\begin{split}q_{l}={}&\left(P^{+},\,\frac{(\bar{\bm{q}}_{i}+\bar{\bm{q}}_{j})^{2}+q_{l}^{2}}{2P^{+}},\,\bar{\bm{q}}_{i}+\bar{\bm{q}}_{j}\right)\;,\\
\bar{q}_{i}={}&\left(zP^{+},\,\frac{\bar{\bm{q}}_{i}^{2}+\bar{q}_{i}^{2}}{2zP^{+}},\,\bar{\bm{q}}_{i}\right)\;,\\
\bar{q}_{j}={}&\left((1-z)P^{+},\,\frac{\bar{\bm{q}}_{j}^{2}+\bar{q}_{j}^{2}}{2(1-z)P^{+}},\,\bar{\bm{q}}_{j}\right)\;.\end{split}

(406)

We then set
q_{l}^{-}=\bar{q}_{i}^{-}+\bar{q}_{j}^{-}
and solve for
q_{l}^{2}
, giving

q_{l}^{2}=\frac{((1-z)\bar{\bm{q}}_{i}-z\bar{\bm{q}}_{j})^{2}}{z(1-z)}+\frac{\bar{q}_{i}^{2}}{z}+\frac{\bar{q}_{j}^{2}}{(1-z)}\;.

(407)

The definitions of
z
and
1-z
are

\begin{split}z={}&\bar{q}_{i}\cdot n/q_{l}\cdot n\;,\\
(1-z)={}&\bar{q}_{j}\cdot n/q_{l}\cdot n\;.\end{split}

(408)

This gives
ShowerTime

\begin{split}\frac{q_{l}^{2}}{2q_{l}\cdot n}\,Q^{2}={}&\frac{((1-z)\bar{\bm{q}}_{i}-z\bar{\bm{q}}_{j})^{2}}{z(1-z)2q_{l}\cdot n}\,Q^{2}\\
&+\frac{\bar{q}_{i}^{2}}{2\bar{q}_{i}\cdot n}\,Q^{2}+\frac{\bar{q}_{j}^{2}}{2\bar{q}_{j}\cdot n}\,Q^{2}\;.\end{split}

(409)

We can use this to derive the inequality that we need. Since the terms on the right-hand side of Eq. (
409
) are all nonnegative, we have

\frac{q_{l}^{2}}{2q_{l}\cdot n}\,Q^{2}>\frac{\bar{q}_{i}^{2}}{2\bar{q}_{i}\cdot n}\,Q^{2}\;.

(410)

This implies

2\bar{q}_{i}\cdot n\,q_{l}^{2}\,Q^{2}>{2q_{l}\cdot n}\,\bar{q}_{i}^{2}\,Q^{2}\;.

(411)

Now let
n_{\textsc{c}}
be another lightlike vector chosen so that
\vec{n}_{\textsc{c}}
lies along the
+z
axis in the reference frame used above. Then
(n_{\textsc{c}}^{+},n_{\textsc{c}}^{-},\bm{n}_{\textsc{c}})=(n_{\textsc{c}}^{+},0,\bm{0})
. Then the same derivation with the roles of
+
and
-
components interchanged gives

2\bar{q}_{i}\cdot n_{\textsc{c}}\,q_{l}^{2}\,Q^{2}>2q_{l}\cdot n_{\textsc{c}}\,\bar{q}_{i}^{2}\,Q^{2}\;.

(412)

Choose
n_{\textsc{c}}^{+}=Q^{+}
and, for the previous lightlike vector
n
,
n^{-}=Q^{-}
. Then

n+n_{\textsc{c}}=Q\;.

(413)

Then adding the two inequalities gives

2\bar{q}_{i}\cdot Q\,q_{l}^{2}\,Q^{2}>2q_{l}\cdot Q\,\bar{q}_{i}^{2}\,Q^{2}\;.

(414)

That is

\frac{q_{l}^{2}}{2q_{l}\cdot Q}\,Q^{2}>\frac{\bar{q}_{i}^{2}}{2\bar{q}_{i}\cdot Q}\,Q^{2}\;.

(415)

We also derive

\frac{q_{l}^{2}}{2q_{l}\cdot Q}\,Q^{2}>\frac{\bar{q}_{j}^{2}}{2\bar{q}_{j}\cdot Q}\,Q^{2}\;.

(416)

These are the inequalities (
173
).

Appendix E
Conjugate amplitudes for collinear splittings

In this appendix, we list the bra amplitudes
\big\langle{s^{\prime}_{l}}\big|\bm{c}^{\dagger}\big|{\hat{s}^{\prime}_{l},\hat{s}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle
for the spin dependence in collinear splittings. The derivations are similar to those in Sec.
XII.2
for the ket amplitudes.

For a
q\to q+\mathrm{g}
splitting, we find

\begin{split}&\big\langle{s^{\prime}_{l}}\big|\bm{c}^{\dagger}(p_{l},q;\hat{p}_{l},q;\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\mathrm{g})\big|{\hat{s}^{\prime}_{l},\hat{s}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle\\
&\qquad=\overline{U}(p_{l},s^{\prime}_{l})\,{\raise-0.34723pt\hbox to4.66318pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\varepsilon$}}(\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{s}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1})\,U(\hat{p}_{l},\hat{s}^{\prime}_{l})\,.\end{split}

(417)

For a
\bar{q}\to\bar{q}+\mathrm{g}
splitting, we find

\begin{split}&\big\langle{s^{\prime}_{l}}\big|\bm{c}^{\dagger}(p_{l},\bar{q};\hat{p}_{l},\bar{q};\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\mathrm{g})\big|{\hat{s}^{\prime}_{l},\hat{s}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle\\
&\qquad=\overline{V}(\hat{p}_{l},\hat{s}^{\prime}_{l})\,{\raise-0.34723pt\hbox to4.66318pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\varepsilon$}}(\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{s}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1})V(p_{l},s^{\prime}_{l})\,.\end{split}

(418)

For a
\mathrm{g}\to q+\bar{q}
splitting, we find

\begin{split}&\big\langle{s^{\prime}_{l}}\big|\bm{c}^{\dagger}(p_{l},\mathrm{g};\hat{p}_{l},q;\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\bar{q})\big|{\hat{s}^{\prime}_{l},\hat{s}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle\\
&\qquad=-\overline{V}(\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{s}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1})\,{\raise-0.34723pt\hbox to4.66318pt{\hss/\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\varepsilon$}}(p_{l},s^{\prime}_{l})^{*}U(\hat{p}_{l},\hat{s}^{\prime}_{l})\,.\end{split}

(419)

For a
\mathrm{g}\to\mathrm{g}+\mathrm{g}
splitting, we find

\begin{split}&\big\langle{s^{\prime}_{l}}\big|\bm{c}^{\dagger}(p_{l},\mathrm{g};\hat{p}_{l},\mathrm{g};\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\mathrm{g})\big|{\hat{s}^{\prime}_{l},\hat{s}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1}}\big\rangle\\
&\qquad=-2\,\varepsilon_{\alpha}(\hat{p}_{l},\hat{s}^{\prime}_{l})\,\varepsilon_{\beta}(\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1},\hat{s}^{\prime}_{m\!+\!1})\,\varepsilon_{\mu}(p_{l},s^{\prime}_{l})^{*}\\
&\qquad\times\Big[g^{\mu\beta}\,\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}^{\alpha}+g^{\mu\alpha}\,\hat{p}_{l}^{\beta}\Big]\,.\end{split}

(420)
